Nucleon Short-Range Correlations and High-Momentum Dynamics: Implications on the Equation of State of Dense Matter
Bao-Jun Cai, Bao-An Li, Yu-Gang Ma

Abstract
Nucleon short-range correlations (SRCs) and their associated high-momentum tails (HMTs) in the single-nucleon momentum distribution n 𝐤 = n ⁡ ( k ) n_{\mathbf{k}}=n(k) have emerged as key manifestations of strong, short-range dynamics in nuclear many-body systems. Despite substantial recent progress, our understanding of these correlations, and their implications for finite nuclei, nuclear reactions, and dense matter, remains incomplete and continues to evolve. In this review, we offer a necessarily selective overview of several aspects of SRC physics that directly influence the Equation of State (EOS) of dense matter, particularly in regimes of large isospin asymmetry and high baryon density. We first summarize the empirical and theoretical features of the momentum distribution n 𝐤 = n ⁡ ( k ) n_{\mathbf{k}}=n(k) , including its isospin dependence, microscopic origins, and representative parameterizations. Special emphasis is placed on the strong neutron–proton (np) tensor force at intermediate momenta, which drives the dominance of correlated np pairs and enhances the minority-species HMT in asymmetric nuclei and nuclear matter. We further discuss connections to nucleon effective masses, quasi-deuteron components, and orbital entanglement entropy, providing a broader microscopic foundation that links SRCs to single-particle and two-body structure. We then examine how SRC-induced HMTs modify the EOS of asymmetric nuclear matter within both non-relativistic and relativistic frameworks. The depletion of low-momentum states and the repopulation of high-momentum components alter kinetic and potential contributions to the EOS. We additionally consider generalizations to arbitrary spatial dimensions and estimates involving very high-momentum components, which help clarify the sensitivity of the EOS to the detailed structure of n ⁡ ( k ) n(k) . Particular attention is devoted to the softening of the kinetic symmetry energy and to deviations from the standard parabolic approximation of isospin-asymmetric nuclear matter EOS, effects that grow increasingly important with isospin asymmetry. In the context of heavy-ion reactions, we summarize the influence of SRCs on isospin-sensitive observables including particle yields, nuclear collective flows, and neutron-proton bremsstrahlung gamma rays. These effects arise from both modified initial momentum distributions and the increased availability of high relative-momentum np pairs, which can strongly affect threshold behavior and transport dynamics. We also briefly comment on experimental probes of high-momentum nucleon components, including electron- and proton-induced knockout reactions and meson production channels in heavy-ion reactions. Finally, we discuss implications for neutron-star matter, wherein extreme densities and large isospin asymmetries amplify many SRC-induced effects known from finite nuclei. Topics include consequences for mass–radius relations, tidal deformabilities, proton fractions, Migdal–Luttinger Z Z -factors, cooling processes, and the core-crust transition. We also highlight potential connections between SRC-modified nucleon momentum distributions and dark-matter interactions in dense astrophysical environments. Keywords: short-range correlations; high-momentum tails; momentum distribution n ⁡ ( k ) n(k) ; asymmetric nuclear matter; symmetry energy; Equation of State; heavy-ion collisions; neutron stars; tidal deformability; Migdal–Luttinger jump.



Notations of main quantities used in this review:

FFG: free Fermi gas

HMT: high-momentum tail

SRC: short-range correlation

NS: neutron star

TOV: Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff

\rho_{J}
: number density of nucleon
J=\mathrm{n,p}

\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{n}}+\rho_{\mathrm{p}}
: total number density of ANM

\delta=(\rho_{\mathrm{n}}-\rho_{\mathrm{p}})/(\rho_{\mathrm{n}}+\rho_{\mathrm{p}})
: isospin asymmetry between neutrons and protons

k_{\mathrm{F}}=(3\pi^{2}\rho/2)^{1/3}
: nucleon Fermi momentum in SNM

: Fermi momentum of nucleon
J

\tau_{3}^{\mathrm{n}}=+1
and
\tau_{3}^{\mathrm{p}}=-1

E_{0}(\rho)\equiv E(\rho,0)
: EOS of symmetric nuclear matter (SNM)

E_{\mathrm{n}}(\rho)\equiv E(\rho,1)
: EOS of pure neutron matter (PNM)

E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
: nuclear symmetry energy

E^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho,\delta)
: kinetic EOS encapsulating the momentum distribution effects

E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
: the kinetic part of the symmetry energy

: energy density as a function of density and isospin asymmetry

P(\rho,\delta)
: pressure as a function of density and isospin asymmetry

a_{2}(A)
: probability of finding a high-momentum nucleon pair in nucleus
A
relative to that in deuteron

n_{\mathbf{k}}=n(k)
: single nucleon momentum distribution function (suppressing the
\rho
- and

-dependence)

C_{J}
: strength of the HMT of nucleon
J=\mathrm{n,p}

: high-momentum fraction of

in nucleus

§1 Introduction

The Equation of State (EOS) of dense nucleonic matter lies at the heart of contemporary nuclear physics and astrophysics
[
452
,
124
,
115
,
191
,
192
,
193
,
44
,
480
,
15
,

,

,

,

,

,

,
419
,
280
,
17
,
464
,
350
,
345
,
446
,
74
,
155
,
16
,
13
,
269
,
46
,
41
,
348
,
287
,
149
,
274
,
108
]
. It governs the thermodynamic, transport, and microscopic properties of matter across a vast range of densities and isospin asymmetries, from terrestrial heavy-ion reactions
[
280
,
13
]
to the interiors of neutron stars (NSs)
[
406
,
211
,
46
,
41
,
17
,
464
,
350
,
345
,
446
]
. Despite decades of progress, major uncertainties persist, especially in the high-density behavior of EOS, owing to the complex interplay between short-range nuclear interactions, many-body correlations, and the nonperturbative nature of quantum chromodynamics (QCD) in the strongly coupled regime
[
68
]
. Among these ingredients, short-range correlations (SRCs) and the associated high-momentum tail (HMT) in the single nucleon momentum distribution
n_{\mathbf{k}}=n(k)
have emerged as indispensable components in developing an accurate, microscopically grounded EOS for dense matter.

FIG. 1
:
(Color Online). Upper: schematic electron energy-loss spectrum, showing elastic scattering, excitation of discrete nuclear levels, excitation of giant resonances, quasi-elastic scattering, pion production and
\Delta(1232)
formation,
\mathrm{N}^{\ast}
resonance formation and the deep inelastic region. Figure taken from Ref.
[
196
]
. Lower: sketch of the nucleon momentum distribution
n(k)
in finite nuclei or in ANM. Majority nucleons dominate the
n(k)
below the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
, while a relatively larger fraction of minority nucleons reside in the HMT above
k_{\mathrm{F}}
where neutron-proton pairs spatially close, having zero total momentum but large relative momentum are formed by SRCs. Figure taken from Ref.
[
219
]
.

SRCs originate from the strong short-range repulsion and tensor components of the nucleon-nucleon interaction
[
56
]
. They deplete the Fermi sea, generate a substantial population of high-momentum nucleons, and give rise to a characteristic power-law tail
n(k)\sim k^{-4}
at large
k
[
424
,
426
,
425
]
, as shown in the lower part of FIG.
1
, and observed in nuclei
[
219
,
422
]
, nuclear matter
[
51
]
, and even universal cold-atom systems
[
202
,
60
]
.
In finite nuclei or asymmetric nuclear matter (ANM), majority nucleons dominate the occupation of
n(k)
below the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
, while above
k_{\mathrm{F}}
in the HMT induced by SRCs, minority nucleons are preferentially populated relative to their respective total populations as it takes equal numbers of neutrons and protons to make SRC pairs
[
219
]
. This inversion of the usual occupation pattern is a distinctive feature of SRC-driven dynamics. Moreover, the correlated nucleon pairs responsible for the HMT are characterized by a large relative momentum while maintaining a comparatively small center-of-mass momentum, a hallmark of the tensor-dominated short-range interaction
[
219
]
;
in other words, they are predominantly back-to-back, see FIG.
2
for the sketch of such a configuration in finite nuclei
[
361
]
.
Because the EOS depends sensitively on the momentum distribution, even modest SRC-induced changes can significantly modify the kinetic contribution to the EOS and the symmetry energy
[
284
]
, the validity of the parabolic approximation of ANM EOS
[
65
,
86
,
401
,
203
,
366
]
, the pressure of neutron-rich matter, and the composition of dense matter.
The isospin dependence of SRCs in ANM further leads to a proton-neutron fractional population imbalance in the HMT, influencing effective masses
[
279
]
, single-particle properties
[
151
]
and the overall isovector response.
It is well known that nuclear force has a tensor component that is attractive at intermediate range due do pion exchange and a repulsive one at short distance due to
\rho
-meson exchange.
The SRC-driven features, rooted largely in strong spin-isospin dependence of neutron-proton tensor correlations tied to in-medium pion and rho-meson dynamics
[
167
]
, also play a central role in phenomena such as the European Muon Collaboration (EMC) effect
[
38
]
. The latter refers to the finding that the structure function (quark momentum distribution function) in nucleons depends on whether the nucleon is in free-space or in a nuclear medium. In fact, it has been found that only the momentum distributions of quarks in SRC neutron-proton pairs are modified when a local temporal high-density is formed in nuclei
[
467
]
. Moreover, because a larger fraction of protons populate the HMT in neutron-rich nuclei,
the structure function of protons are modified more compared to neutrons in neutron-richer nuclei
[
397
]
.

The richness of the nuclear medium effects can be revealed by not only the traditional electron-nucleus and proton-nucleus scatterings
[
63
]
(upper panel of FIG.
1
) but also nucleus-nucleus scatterings (some involving inverse kinematics in reactions with radioactive beams) over a wide range of energies
[
196
]
. These experiments have largely verified the approximately
k^{-4}
behavior of HMT with momentum up to about
2k_{\mathrm{F}}
[
219
]
. Further investigating the microscopic origin and properties of SRCs, their connections with EMC effects as well as quantifying their broad impact in nuclear physics and astrophysics, e.g., nuclear EOS, therefore remain key goals of several future experimental programs
[
218
,
220
]
, see, e.g., SRC proposals
[
436
,
217
]
at the electron-ion collider (EIC) at BNL in the US
[
62
]
, those by the R
3
B Collaboration
[
370
,
367
]
at GSI-FAIR (Facility for Anti-proton and Ion Research in Europe) in Germany
[
177
]
, as well as plans at HIAF (High-Intensity Heavy-Ion Accelerator Facility) and EicC (Electron-Ion Collider in China)
[
505
,
19
]
. Moreover, ongoing SRC experiments at facilities like the Jefferson Lab (JLab), GSI, JINR (Joint Institute for Nuclear Research) and IMP/Lanzhou (Institute of Modern Physics) are continuously producing excitingly new results, see, e.g., Refs.
[
255
,
34
,
185
,
253
]
.

FIG. 2
:
(Color Online). Back-to-back configuration of a SRC pair in finite nuclei. Figure taken from Ref.
[
361
]
.

The relevance of the EOS extends far beyond nuclear structure. In heavy-ion reactions
[
279
]
, where high densities, large isospin asymmetries, and transient nonequilibrium conditions are created, the EOS dictates collective flows, particle production thresholds, meson yield ratios, bremsstrahlung signals, and sub-threshold production phenomena. SRC-induced HMTs modify the initial nucleon momentum distributions and reaction dynamics, leading to observable consequences such as enhanced high-energy particle emission, altered isospin-sensitive observables, and novel coexistence patterns between coordinate-space neutron skins and momentum-space proton skins
[
90
]
. These observables are actively used to constrain the symmetry energy near and above saturation density
[
280
,
487
,
295
,
94
,
460
,
459
,
503
]
, making SRC physics a vital link between microscopic nuclear dynamics and experimental probes of the EOS
[
287
]
.
In astrophysics, the EOS of superdense matter under strong-field gravity governs nearly every macroscopic property of NSs
[
446
,
46
,
98
]
. SRC-induced changes in the nucleon momentum distribution influence the pressure-energy density relation, the proton fraction, Urca thresholds, superfluid gaps, core-crust transition densities, and the emergence of complex nuclear structures in low-density regions. Consequently, they affect the mass-radius (M-R) relation, tidal deformabilities, cooling curves, neutrino opacities, and the thermal and compositional evolution of NSs
[
496
]
. The discovery of massive NSs
[
174
,
171
,
429
,
172
,
169
,
116
,
376
,
168
,
175
,
176
,
173
]
, together with multimessenger observations from gravitational-wave detections
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
, has elevated the importance of understanding dense-matter EOS effects with unprecedented precision. SRC-HMT physics enriches this picture by providing a microscopic mechanism that tightens the connection between terrestrial experiments, modern nuclear many-body theories, and astrophysical observables of dense matter.

This review aims to provide a coherent examination of how SRC-induced nucleon high-momentum structures in
n(k)
modify the EOS of dense matter and how these modifications propagate into nuclear reactions and astrophysical systems. Section
§2
lays the microscopic foundation by discussing the structure, strength, and isospin dependence of
n(k)
, including its effects on nucleon effective masses, nuclear energy density functionals, and entanglement properties. Section
§3
focuses on how the SRC-HMT affects the EOS in relativistic and non-relativistic frameworks, examines kinetic contributions to the symmetry energy, and generalizes these concepts to arbitrary dimensions. Section
§4
explores the broad impact of SRC-HMT physics on isospin-sensitive observables in heavy-ion collisions and scattering experiments. Section
§5
examines NS properties under the influence of SRC-modified EOSs, covering macroscopic structure, cooling, composition, and the potential presence of exotic components. Finally, Section
§6
provides a broader perspective and outlines potential problems for future exploration.

Through this unified treatment, the review highlights how SRCs and the HMT in
n(k)
serve as a microscopic bridge connecting the EOS of dense matter to its manifestations in both laboratory experiments and NSs. Understanding these connections is essential for constraining the behavior of strongly interacting matter under extreme conditions and for advancing the global effort to determine the dense-matter EOS with high fidelity.
As this review addresses only selected aspects related to the EOS of dense matter, it is necessarily incomplete and limited; for other studies on SRCs and HMTs, we refer the reader to comprehensive reviews, e.g., Refs.
[
221
,
150
,
186
,
33
,
32
,
35
,
134
,
75
,
39
,
189
,
188
,
144
,
190
]
.
In addition, this review is intended to be introductory in nature. We include essential formulas along with detailed and pedagogical explanations, with the hope that it may serve as a helpful starting point for newcomers to this exciting field.

§2 Nucleon Momentum Distribution
n_{\mathbf{k}}=n(k)
with a HMT in Nuclear Matter

In this section, we review several foundational aspects of the single-nucleon momentum distribution function
n_{\mathbf{k}}=n(k)
. Subsection
2.1
outlines the basic definitions of the characteristic quantities of the EOS of ANM. Subsection
2.2
discusses
n_{\mathbf{k}}
in detail, including its functional form, the strength of the nuclear contact, and its isospin structure from both theoretical and experimental perspectives. Subsection
2.3
examines the nucleon E-effective mass, with emphasis on the
Z
-factor and its physical implications. Subsection
2.4
briefly introduces the quasi-deuteron picture used to study SRCs within relativistic energy density functional approaches. Finally, Subsection
2.5
presents the orbital entanglement entropy associated with SRCs and highlights its connection to the nuclear contact.

2.1 Characteristics of EOS of ANM at Zero Temperature

The EOS of ANM at zero temperature is defined as the energy per nucleon and is written as
E(\rho,\delta)
, where
\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{n}}+\rho_{\mathrm{p}}
is the total nucleon density and
\delta\equiv(\rho_{\mathrm{n}}-\rho_{\mathrm{p}})/\rho
is the isospin asymmetry associated with the neutron and proton densities
\rho_{\mathrm{n}}
and
\rho_{\mathrm{p}}
. Since
-1\leq\delta\leq 1
, the EOS of ANM can be naturally expanded around
\delta=0
, and isospin (neutron-proton exchange) symmetry ensures that no odd powers of
\delta
appear
[
280
,
279
]
,

E(\rho,\delta)\approx E_{0}(\rho)+E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\delta^{2}+E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\delta^{4}+\cdots,

(2-1)

where
E_{0}(\rho)\equiv E(\rho,0)
is the EOS of symmetric nucleonic matter (SNM). The functions
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
and
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}(\rho)
, the second- and fourth-order symmetry energies, are defined by

E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\equiv\left.\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}E(\rho,\delta)}{\partial\delta^{2}}\right|_{\delta=0},~E_{\mathrm{sym},4}(\rho)\equiv\left.\frac{1}{24}\frac{\partial^{4}E(\rho,\delta)}{\partial\delta^{4}}\right|_{\delta=0},

(2-2)

and represent the corresponding coefficients in the Taylor expansion of
E(\rho,\delta)
over
\delta
.

The EOS of SNM, the symmetry energy, and the fourth-order symmetry energy may in turn be expanded around a reference density
\rho_{\mathrm{rf}}
, commonly chosen as the saturation density
\rho_{0}
, giving
[
280
,
279
]

\displaystyle E_{0}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle E_{0}(\rho_{0})+\frac{K_{0}}{2!}\chi^{2}+\frac{J_{0}}{3!}\chi^{3}+\frac{I_{0}}{4!}\chi^{4}+\mathcal{O}(\chi^{5}),

(2-3)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})+L\chi+\frac{K_{\mathrm{sym}}}{2!}\chi^{2}+\frac{J_{\mathrm{sym}}}{3!}\chi^{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{I_{\mathrm{sym}}}{4!}\chi^{4}+\mathcal{O}(\chi^{5}),

(2-4)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym},4}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})+L_{\mathrm{sym,4}}\chi+\frac{K_{\mathrm{sym,4}}}{2!}\chi^{2}+\frac{J_{\mathrm{sym,4}}}{3!}\chi^{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{I_{\mathrm{sym,4}}}{4!}\chi^{4}+\mathcal{O}(\chi^{5}),

(2-5)

where the dimensionless expansion variables are
\chi\equiv\chi_{0}=({\rho-\rho_{0}})/{3\rho_{0}}
and
\chi_{\mathrm{rf}}=({\rho-\rho_{\mathrm{rf}}})/{3\rho_{\mathrm{rf}}}
.
The coefficients appearing in Eq. (
2-4
) are

\displaystyle K_{\mathrm{sym}}

\displaystyle=\left.9\rho_{0}^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)}{\partial\rho^{2}}\right|_{\rho=\rho_{0}},~J_{\mathrm{sym}}=\left.27\rho_{0}^{3}\frac{\partial^{3}E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)}{\partial\rho^{3}}\right|_{\rho=\rho_{0}},

(2-6)

and analogous definitions apply to
L
,
I_{\mathrm{sym}}
,
K_{0}
,
J_{0}
,
I_{0}
,
K_{\mathrm{sym},4}
,
J_{\mathrm{sym},4}
and
I_{\mathrm{sym},4}
.
For nucleon in ANM, the free Fermi gas (FFG) model single-nucleon momentum distribution function takes the step function
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(\rho,\delta)=\Theta(k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}-|\mathbf{k}|)
, where
J=\mathrm{n,p}
. Consequently the relation between the nucleon density
\rho_{J}
and the corresponding Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
could be obtained,

k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}=k_{\mathrm{F}}\left(1+\tau_{3}^{J}\delta\right)^{1/3},

(2-7)

where
\tau_{3}^{\mathrm{n}}=+1
and
\tau_{3}^{\mathrm{p}}=-1
.
For a pure neutron matter (PNM),
\delta=1
.

2.2 Form, Strength and the Isospin Structure of
n_{\mathbf{k}}=n(k)

In ANM, the single-nucleon momentum distribution
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
exhibits a HMT above the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
and a corresponding depletion below it, originating from the repulsive short-range core (or the SRCs) and the tensor force in nuclear interactions
[
327
,
56
,
353
,
354
]
, as sketched in FIG.
3
[
221
]
.
Over the past two decades, extensive theoretical and experimental studies have quantified the nucleon HMT in ANM
[
223
,
219
,
164
,
408
,
466
,
259
,
186
,
362
,
472
]
. As an illustration, the upper panel of FIG.
4
presents calculated momentum distributions for
{}^{2}\mathrm{H}
,
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{16}\mathrm{O}
, and (symmetric) nuclear matter
[
51
]
, where the distributions
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}
(with the superscript “0” denoting SNM) for the light nuclei were obtained using the Argonne
v(ij)
and model VII
v(ijk)
, while those for SNM
[
180
]
were calculated using the Urbana
v(ij)
supplemented with density-dependent terms that effectively incorporate
v(ijk)
. A key feature of all these results is the universal scaling of the HMT, highlighting its common short-range origin in all nuclear systems.
As another example, the
n(k)
for
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
nucleus using the tensor-optimized high-momentum antisymmetrized molecular dynamics (TO-HMAMD) is shown in the lower panel of FIG.
4
.
In their calculation
[
314
]
, they found that the correlated terms contribute to about 23% of the total wave function. Furthermore, the contribution of tensor correlation term is about 12%, which is roughly double as compared to the central contribution; the many-body contribution of about 4% is comparably small.
In particular, Ref.
[
314
]
found that the high-momentum components (
k>2\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
) of
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
nucleus are mostly contributed by the tensor and the short-range correlated terms, where the tensor correlation dominates around
k\approx 2\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
and the short-range repulsion dominates around
k\approx 4\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
, as denoted by the blue and yellow regions in the figure, respectively.

FIG. 3
:
(Color Online). A cartoon on the
n_{\mathbf{k}}
in finite nuclei, figure taken from Ref.
[
221
]
.

FIG. 4
:
(Color Online). Upper: the nucleon momentum distribution in
{}^{2}\mathrm{H}
,
{}^{4}{\mathrm{He}}
,
{}^{16}\mathrm{O}
and that in infinite SNM; figure taken from Ref.
[
51
]
.
Lower: the component of
n(k)
for
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
obtained from the tensor-optimized high-momentum antisymmetrized molecular dynamics (TO-HMAMD). Figures taken from Ref.
[
314
]
.

The characteristic SRC length scale in finite nuclei is roughly about
1\,\mathrm{fm}
(the size of nucleon).
In FIG.
5
, we show an example of nucleon–nucleon correlation functions

F\sim\rho(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})/\rho(\mathbf{x})\rho(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
from a microscopic calculation
[
131
]
.
The Fourier transform of the two-body density gives the single-nucleon momentum distribution,

n_{\mathbf{k}}\sim\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\,e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}\rho(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, so that
short-distance features of
F(r)
translate directly into high-momentum components of
n_{\mathbf{k}}
.
The pronounced bump of
F(r)
around
r\sim 1\,\mathrm{fm}
thus reflects the spatial scale at which SRCs
are most active.
As can be seen from the figure, the correlation functions for
{}^{16}\mathrm{O}
and

{}^{40}\mathrm{Ca}
share a very similar global pattern, indicating that the dominant features of
SRCs are largely nucleus-independent across these medium-mass systems.
At the same time, a clear isospin dependence emerges at short distances (
r\lesssim 1.5\,\mathrm{fm}
), where the difference between proton-proton and proton-neutron
pairs becomes noticeable. The proton-neutron pairs exhibit stronger correlations,
a consequence of the dominant spin-triplet components in that channel.
The isospin-dependence of the two-body pair density is produced by short-ranged interactions, driven by the two-nucleon tensor force
[
131
]
.
In these calculations, protons and neutrons are treated symmetrically; the correlations for
proton-proton and neutron-neutron pairs are identical in both
{}^{16}\mathrm{O}
and
{}^{40}\mathrm{Ca}
.

FIG. 5
:
(Color Online). An example on nucleon-nucleon correlation functions for
{}^{16}\mathrm{O}
and
{}^{40}\mathrm{Ca}
for different coupled channels. Figure taken from Ref.
[
131
]
.

Motivated by both microscopic calculations and several well-known earlier predictions from nuclear many-body theories
[
27
]
, together with recent experimental findings
[
472
,
223
,
219
]
, one may describe the single-nucleon momentum distribution in ANM as
[
91
]

n^{J}_{\mathbf{k}}(\rho,\delta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{J}+\beta_{J}{I}\left(\displaystyle\frac{|\mathbf{k}|}{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\right),&0<|\mathbf{k}|<k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J},\\
\displaystyle{C}_{J}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}{|\mathbf{k}|}\right)^{4},&k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}<|\mathbf{k}|<\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}.\end{array}\right.

(2-8)

Here, the depletion at zero momentum
\Delta_{J}
is defined relative to the free Fermi gas (FFG), whereas
\beta_{J}
encodes the strength of the momentum dependence
I(|\mathbf{k}|/k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J})
[
50
,
132
,
395
]
near (and at) the Fermi surface; constraints on the function
I(x)
as well as its impact on the EOS is discussed in Ref.
[
91
]
. The jump
Z^{J}_{\mathrm{F}}
at
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
, namely the renormalization (strength) function, contains rich information about the nucleon effective E-mass via the Migdal–Luttinger theorem
[
327
,
312
]
and also reflects its nontrivial isospin dependence
[
245
,
318
]
; the related issue on the E-mass, e.g., the effects of the function
I(x)
, will be discussed in the Subsection
2.3
, see FIG.
6
for a sketch of the
n_{\mathbf{k}}
in ANM
[
92
]
. The parameters
{C}_{J}
and
\phi_{J}
determine the HMT fraction through

\boxed{x_{J}^{\mathrm{HMT}}=\left.\int_{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}^{\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\right/{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}}=3C_{{J}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{{J}}}\right).}

(2-9)

The normalization condition

\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}n^{J}_{\mathbf{k}}(\rho,\delta)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}=\rho_{J}=\frac{(k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J})^{3}}{3\pi^{2}}

(2-10)

implies that among the four parameters
\beta_{J}
,
{C}_{J}
,
\phi_{J}
, and
\Delta_{J}
, only three are independent; one may, for instance, choose
\beta_{J}
,
{C}_{J}
, and
\phi_{J}
to be independent and then determine
\Delta_{J}
from
*1
*1

*1

In Ref.
[
224
]
, the normalization condition is set as

[2/(2\pi)^{3}]\int_{0}^{\infty}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}=\rho
, differs by a factor 2 from the definition of Eq. (
2-10
),
from which the corresponding relation reads
[4/(2\pi)^{3}]\int_{0}^{\infty}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}=\rho
for SNM.

\Delta_{J}=1-\frac{3\beta_{J}}{(k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J})^{3}}\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}{I}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\right)k^{2}\mathrm{d}k-3{C}_{J}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{J}}\right).

(2-11)

FIG. 6
:
A sketch of the single-nucleon momentum distribution function
n_{\mathbf{k}}
in ANM. Figure taken from Ref.
[
92
]
.

Since the self-consistent Green’s function (SCGF)
[
382
,
381
,
156
]
and the Brueckner–Hartree–Fock (BHF)
[
506
,
498
]
calculations both indicate that
\Delta_{J}
varies approximately linearly with
\delta
but in opposite directions for neutrons and protons, it was further assumed that all four parameters in Eq. (
2-8
) can be expanded as
Y_{J}=Y_{0}(1+Y_{1}^{J}\delta)
[
91
]
. This assumption can be justified by examining the resulting average kinetic energy per nucleon in ANM,

\boxed{E^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho,\delta)=\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{J=\mathrm{n,p}}\int_{0}^{\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(\rho,\delta)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k},}

(2-12)

which, under Eq. (
2-8
), acquires a linear term in
\delta
:

\displaystyle E_{1}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\equiv

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial E^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho,\delta)}{\partial\delta}\right|_{\delta=0}=\frac{3}{5}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\Bigg[\frac{5}{2}C_{0}\phi_{0}(\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{n}}+\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{p}})

\displaystyle+\frac{5}{2}C_{0}(\phi_{0}-1)(C_{1}^{\mathrm{n}}+C_{1}^{\mathrm{p}})+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{0}(\Delta_{1}^{\mathrm{n}}+\Delta_{1}^{\mathrm{p}})

\displaystyle+\frac{5\beta_{0}(\beta_{1}^{\mathrm{n}}+\beta_{1}^{\mathrm{p}})}{2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{5}}\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}}I\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{F}}}\right)k^{4}\mathrm{d}k\Bigg].

(2-13)

Imposing neutron-proton exchange symmetry of the EOS by requiring
E_{1}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=0
leads directly to

\Delta_{1}^{\mathrm{n}}=-\Delta_{1}^{\mathrm{p}}
,

\beta_{1}^{\mathrm{n}}=-\beta_{1}^{\mathrm{p}}
,

{C}_{1}^{\mathrm{n}}=-{C}_{1}^{\mathrm{p}}
,
and
\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{n}}=-\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{p}}
,
i.e., each parameter satisfies

Y_{J}/Y_{0}=1+Y_{1}^{J}\delta.

(2-14)

a_{2}(A)
for several typical nuclei, i.e.,
{}^{3}\mathrm{He}
,

.

FIG. 8
:
(Color Online). Top: Measured per-nucleon
(\mathrm{e},\mathrm{e}^{\prime})
cross-section ratios
(\sigma_{{}^{4}\mathrm{He}}/4)/(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}/2)

.

As shown in the upper panel of FIG.
4
, the nucleon HMT exhibits an approximate scaling from the deuteron to nuclear matter
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{A}=a_{2}(A)n_{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}
, consistent with the observations
[
180
,
363
,
119
]
, here
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{A}
and
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}
denote the high-momentum components of the nucleon momentum distribution for a nucleus with mass number
A
and for deuterium
[
189
,
454
]
, respectively. The factor
a_{2}(A)
is independent of momentum and represents the probability of finding a high-momentum nucleon pair in nucleus
A
relative to that in the deuteron
[
188
]
. Experimentally,
a_{2}(A)
is determined from the plateau in the per-nucleon inclusive
(\mathrm{e},\mathrm{e}^{\prime})
cross-section ratios for heavy nuclei relative to deuteron in the Bjorken scaling region
1.5\lesssim x_{\mathrm{Bj}}\lesssim 1.9
[
33
]
, i.e.,

\boxed{a_{2}(A)=\frac{\sigma_{A}/A}{\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}/2}.}

(2-15)

Representative experimental
a_{2}(A)
values for nuclei such as
{}^{3}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{9}\mathrm{Be}
,
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
,
{}^{56}\mathrm{Fe}
(
{}^{63}\mathrm{Cu}
), and
{}^{197}\mathrm{Au}
are displayed in FIG.
7
[
184
,
466
,
164
,
187
]
. Extrapolating these results to infinite nuclear matter leads to
a_{2}(\infty)\approx 7\pm 1
[
321
,
118
,
224
,
133
]
.
In a recent study, the relative abundances of SRC nucleon pairs have been investigated using inclusive electron scattering and the generalized contact formalism (GCF) with various nuclear interaction models. GCF calculations successfully reproduce the observed scaling of cross-section ratios
a_{2}
at high
x_{\mathrm{B}}=x_{\mathrm{Bj}}
and large momentum transfer
Q^{2}
. They found that ratios of similar-mass isotopes, such as
{}^{40}\mathrm{Ca}
and
{}^{48}\mathrm{Ca}
, are influenced not only by the isospin dependence of SRCs but also by low-energy nuclear structure. Consequently, the empirical extraction of SRC pair abundances from measured
a_{2}
values is accurate only to about 20%, and further improvement requires cross-section calculations that consistently incorporate both nuclear structure and relativistic effects.
GCF is an important framework for investigating SRC-related issues, see Refs.
[
473
,
130
,
128
,
298
,
299
,
470
,
471
,
474
,
500
]
for more discussions.
It should be noted, as emphasized in Ref.
[
221
]
, that the substantial uncertainties in the SRC ratios reported in Ref.
[
187
]
are mainly due to combining data obtained at different experimental kinematics. Additionally, the SRC ratios in Ref.
[
164
]
were used in the original EMC-SRC correlation analysis of Ref.
[
466
]
. Despite these efforts, a satisfactory theoretical understanding of the nearly linear correlation between the slope of the EMC effect and the
a_{2}
factor
[
466
]
is lacking
[
461
,
30
]
.

:

a_{2}(A)
data with predictions using the SCGF theory by Ref.
[
380
]
(upper) and
a independent particle model (IPM) by Refs.
[

,

]
(lower).

a_{2}
obtained from the low-order correlation operator approximation (LCA) adopting two estimate schemes. Figure taken from Ref.
[

.

:
(Color Online). SRC scaling factors
a_{2}
for nuclei from
A=3
to
A=40
, shown relative to the deuteron (upper) and
3
He (lower). Results are obtained using Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) calculations based on chiral interactions within chiral effective field theory (ChPT), with cutoffs
R_{0}=10\,\mathrm{fm}
(blue squares) and
R_{0}=12\,\mathrm{fm}
(red circles). Predictions using the AV18+UIX (green upward triangles) and simplified AV4+UIX
c
(green downward triangles) phenomenological potentials are also shown. Experimental data are indicated by black stars. Gray bands denote the expected saturation region inferred from measurements on heavier nuclei. Dark error bars represent Monte Carlo statistical uncertainties, while lighter bands show combined systematic uncertainties from chiral truncation and phenomenological fits.
Figure taken from Ref.
[

.

:
(Color Online). Upper: the EMC-SRC relation calculated using the LCA method; figure taken from Ref.

Four theoretical calculations are compared with the experimental
a_{2}(A)
in FIGs.
9
-
11
[
380
,
443
,
442
,
387
,
313
]
. In Ref.
[
380
]
,
a_{2}
was estimated as
a_{2}=\langle n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}/n_{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}\rangle_{|\mathbf{k}|=400\sim 550\,\mathrm{MeV}}
,
and empirically parameterized as
a_{2}(\rho)\approx b_{1}\rho^{b_{2}}
with
b_{1}\approx 7\sim 10
and
b_{2}\approx 0.4\sim 0.5
.
Considering that SCGF calculations generally predict a saturation density
\rho_{0}^{\mathrm{SCGF}}
higher than the empirical value, the extrapolated
a_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathrm{SCGF}})\approx 5\pm 1
underestimates the data, but remains roughly consistent with
a_{2}\approx 7\pm 1
given in the above, see the upper panel of FIG.
9
. The relatively low high-momentum cutoff used in SCGF calculations (400-550 MeV) also contributes to this underestimation, since the experimental momentum window for two-body SRC pairs spans approximately 300-600 MeV
[
219
]
.
In contrast, Refs.
[
443
,
442
]
evaluated
a_{2}
within the independent-particle model (IPM, lower panel of FIG.
9
):

a_{2}(A/\mathrm{d})=\frac{2}{A}N_{\mathrm{pn}(S=1)}(A,Z)\int_{\mathrm{PS}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{P}_{12}F^{\mathrm{pn}}(P_{12}),

(2-16)

where
N_{\mathrm{pn}(S=1)}(A,Z)
counts the number of correlated pn pairs in the spin-triplet state, the integral runs over the relevant center-of-mass phase space, and
F^{\mathrm{pn}}
is the center-of-mass distribution of correlated pairs. Ref.
[
442
]
found that
a_{2}(A/\mathrm{d})
increases nearly linearly with
A
for
A\lesssim 40
, and then saturates for heavier nuclei. Their predictions for
{}^{3}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{9}\mathrm{Be}
,
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
,
{}^{27}\mathrm{Al}
,
{}^{40}\mathrm{Ca}
,
{}^{48}\mathrm{Ca}
,
{}^{56}\mathrm{Fe}
,
{}^{108}\mathrm{Ag}
, and
{}^{197}\mathrm{Au}
are compared with the experimental data in FIG.
7
.
Similarly, Ref.
[
387
]
estimates the
a_{2}(A)
factor by using the low-order correlation operator approximation (LCA).
For the studied sample of fifteen nuclei, the total SRC scaling factor is roughly in the range 4.05-5.14, of which roughly 3 can be attributed to pn correlations, and the SRC scaling factors receive sizable contributions from pp and nn correlations; their results are shown in FIG.
10
where the upper and lower panels adopt two different schemes for estimating
a_{2}(A)
:

a_{2}(A)\approx\frac{\int_{p>2\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}}\mathrm{d}pP^{A}(p)}{\int_{p>2\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}}\mathrm{d}pP^{\mathrm{d}}(p)};\quad\text{and }~a_{2}(A)\approx\lim_{\mathrm{high}\,p}\frac{P^{A}(p)}{P^{\mathrm{d}}(p)},

(2-17)

here
P^{A}(p)=p^{2}n^{A}(p)/A
is the probability distribution normalized by the condition
\int\mathrm{d}pP^{A}(p)=1
[
387
]
.

In Ref.
[
313
]
, the scaling factor
a_{2}
is extracted using Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) methods combined with chiral effective field theory (ChPT). The authors exploit these advances to confirm the linear EMC-SRC correlation in light nuclei up to
12
C by comparing ab initio calculations with existing experimental data. They then extended the analysis to additional light systems (
6
He,
6
Li, and
16
O) as well as the medium-mass nucleus
40
Ca. The prediction is shown in FIG.
11
.
For
a_{2}(A/d)
, the gray band at large
A
represents the expected saturation range, determined from the difference (including uncertainties) between the measurements in
197
Au and
63
Cu. This gives an estimate
\lim_{A\to\infty}a_{2}(A/d)\approx 4.94
-
5.41
. The lower panel shows the corresponding results for
a_{2}(A/^{3}\mathrm{He})
from
4
He to
40
Ca, where the saturation region is guided by the experimental value from
56
Fe.
Overall, the calculated SRC scaling factors show excellent agreement with experiment wherever data exist. Using chiral interactions at N
2
LO with the cutoff
R_{0}=1.0
fm, the relative deviations from experiment for
a_{2}(A/d)
are
0.0\%
,
4.4\%
, and
1.7\%
for
3
He,
4
He, and
12
C, respectively. The softer cutoff
R_{0}=1.2
fm yields values typically larger by
5
-
10\%
, but still consistent with the estimated systematic uncertainties. Interestingly, the predicted
a_{2}
values for the
A=6
nuclei lie below that of
4
He, placing them between
3
He and
4
He along the fitted trend. This supports the picture that the local nuclear density, rather than the overall mass number, controls both the size of the EMC effect and the height of the SRC scaling plateau.
As further discussed in Ref.
[
313
]
, these predictions are experimentally testable. For
6
Li, inclusive
(\mathrm{e,e^{\prime}})
measurements at Jefferson Lab in quasi-elastic kinematics already offer a direct path for verification. For
6
He, inverse-kinematics experiments at future rare-isotope facilities such as FAIR, using
(\mathrm{p,2p})
reactions with a
6
He beam, provide a promising route toward direct measurement.
The slope of the EMC effect and the
a_{2}
factor calculated using the LCA and QMC methods are shown in FIG.
12
.
Specifically, it was pointed out that the obtained theory-experiment
comparisons for the EMC slopes cannot shed light
on underlying mechanisms that are due to non-SRC related
medium modifications
[
387
]
.

Experimental data show that reproducing the measured deuteron quadrupole moment and the spin correlation parameter requires approximately
4
-
5\%
tensor-force induced D-wave contribution
[
188
]
. Combining this with the extrapolated
a_{2}(\infty)
, the HMT fraction in infinite SNM is estimated as
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%\pm 4\%
[
223
,
219
,
164
,
362
,
408
,
466
,
259
,
224
]
. According to Ref.
[
422
]
, roughly
80\%
of nucleons in
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
behave independently (or as described by the shell model), while the remaining
20\%
form correlated pairs, of which
90\pm 10\%
are pn SRC pairs and
5\pm 1.5\%
are pp pairs. This indicates an approximate 20-fold excess of pn over pp pairs
[
164
,
422
]
, a trend that persists in heavier nuclei from C to
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
[
219
]
(see FIG.
13
, lower panel). By isospin symmetry, nn SRC pairs are also estimated to account for
5\pm 1.5\%
, yielding an HMT fraction in PNM of
x_{\mathrm{PNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 1.5\%\pm 0.5\%
[
223
,
219
,
164
,
362
,
408
,
466
,
259
,
224
]
. The observed dominance of pn over pp pairs reflects the spin-isospin dependence of underlying tensor correlations, resulting in approximately equal numbers of protons and neutrons in the HMT. The prediction on the pp-to-pn SRC pairs using the LCA approximation
[
387
]
is shown in FIG.
14
. In line with the data,
the LCA predictions for the number of pp-to-pn SRC pairs
are fairly constant among the fifteen nuclei in their sample
[
387
]

and increase with increasing nucleon momentum. For a fixed
momentum range, the variation in the predicted pp-to-pn SRC
pair ratios across nuclei is of the order of few percent, in line
with the experimental observations
[
158
]
.

FIG. 15
:
(Color Online). High-momentum nucleon fractions in heavy nuclei and nuclear matter. Figure taken from Ref.
[
279
]
.

FIG. 16
:
(Color Online). Upper: the proton high-momentum fraction relative to that of
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
as a function of
\delta
using a phenomenological model connecting the quenching of spectroscopic factors with the JLab experimental data; figure taken from Ref.
[

]
. Lower: relative high-momentum fractions for neutrons and protons using the quasi-elastic knock-out data
[

.

Consequently, in neutron-rich matter, the fraction of protons in the HMT exceeds that of neutrons
[
219
,
393
]
. The isospin dependence of
x_{J}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
is illustrated in FIG.
15
(blue band for neutrons, red band for protons) based on parameters from Ref.
[
91
]
.
For finite nuclei, the high-momentum fraction can be approximated by
[
393
,
164
]

\boxed{x_{J}^{\mathrm{HMT}}(A,y)\approx\frac{1}{2x_{J}}a_{2}(A,y)\int_{k_{\mathrm{F}}}^{\infty}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k},}

(2-18)

where
a_{2}(A,y)
is taken from Refs.
[
164
,
321
,
187
]
,
x_{\mathrm{p}}=Z/A
,
x_{\mathrm{n}}=N/A
, and
y=|x_{\mathrm{p}}-x_{\mathrm{n}}|
. As FIG.
15
shows, the fractional proton-neutron imbalance in the HMT grows with increasing isospin asymmetry. For instance, in
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
, the fraction of high-momentum protons exceeds that of neutrons by roughly
55\%
; see also Ref.
[
356
]
for a phenomenological study on the high-momentum fraction using the quasi-elastic knock-out data
[
159
]
as shown in FIG.
16
; the overall linear dependence of the fraction on either
\delta
or
N/Z
is obvious.

FIG. 17
:
(Color Online). Upper: ratios of pn to nn pairs for
{}^{3}\mathrm{H}
and pn to pp
pairs for
{}^{3}\mathrm{He}
as function of the relative momentum
k
, from which the enhancement factor could be calculated; figure taken from Ref.
[
324
]
. Lower: the estimated scaling factor
a_{2}
in ground states of

{}^{3}\mathrm{H}
,
{}^{3}\mathrm{He}
and
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
by no-core shell-model (NCSM) calculations using the N
4
LO chiral
nuclear force; figure taken from Ref.
[
404
]
.

While heavy nuclei are dominated by np SRCs, precise measurements in light mirror nuclei (
3
H and
3
He) reveal a significant deviation from np dominance, indicating unexpected structure in their high-momentum wavefunctions
[
289
,
398
]
.
The experimental data show that the ratio of pn-SRCs to pp-SRCs over the pair-counting prediction
P_{\mathrm{np}/\mathrm{pp}}=NZ/[Z(Z-1)/2]
for
A=3
nuclei is about
2.17^{+0.25}_{-0.20}
(the enhancement factor), which is much smaller
than that in heavy nuclei; these results provide new insight into the short-range part of the nucleon-nucleon interaction.
For example, in Ref.
[
324
]
, the wave functions of
3
H and
3
He were calculated using the Gaussian expansion method to solve the three-body Schrödinger equation. Their analysis of one- and two-nucleon momentum distributions shows that np-SRC pairs are significantly more prevalent than nn- or pp- pairs, with an enhancement factor of about 1.6, in agreement with experimental observations, see the upper panel of FIG.
17
for the ratios of pn- to nn-pairs for
{}^{3}\mathrm{H}
and pn to pp
pairs for
{}^{3}\mathrm{He}
as function of the relative momentum
k
, from which the enhancement factor could be calculated
[
324
]
.
Similarly, Ref.
[
404
]
employed a fifth-order N
4
LO chiral nuclear force without softening, enabling the isolation of nuclear-state effects with different quantum numbers on SRCs. Notably, the
a_{2}
values are reduced and nearly equal in triplet isobaric analog states of neighboring nuclei, demonstrating that SRC abundances cannot be reliably estimated from mean-field shell structures alone. This is attributed to specific nuclear states suppressing the formation of deuteron-like components, offering insights into the connection between high-energy partonic properties and low-energy nuclear structure, see the lower panel of FIG.
17
.
The scaling factors of different nuclear states in

{}^{6}\mathrm{Li}
was also calculated in Ref.
[
404
]
.

FIG. 18
:
(Color Online). Upper: the ultra-cold atomic Fermi gas and the atomic nuclei share very similar behavior although the densities of these two systems are different by 25 orders of magnitude. Lower: the scaled momentum distribution,
k^{\prime 4}n_{k^{\prime}}
where
k^{\prime}=|\mathbf{k}|/k_{\mathrm{F}}
for (a) deuteron and (b) atomic systems. Figures taken from Ref.
[
223
]
.

We now discuss briefly the
1/|\mathbf{k}|^{4}
form of the HMT as well as the strength of contact of the tail, and suggest interested reader to Ref.
[
279
]
for more detailed discussions on related issues. The
1/|\mathbf{k}|^{4}
scaling of the HMT is a universal feature in quantum many-body systems
[
202
,
60
]
.
For example, the upper panel of FIG.
18
shows that the ultra-cold atomic Fermi gas and the atomic nuclei share the very similarity although the densities of these two systems are different by 25 orders of magnitude
[
223
]
.
For deuterons, on the other hand, variational many-body calculations reproduce this behavior within
\sim 10\%
, consistent with
\mathrm{d(e,e^{\prime}p)}
data
[
223
]
. In SNM at
\rho_{0}
, the HMT magnitude is

C_{\mathrm{SNM}}=C_{0}=R_{\mathrm{d}}a_{2}(\infty)k_{\mathrm{F}}\rho/k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}
, where
R_{\mathrm{d}}
characterizes the plateau shown in the lower panel of FIG.
18
. Moreover,
\mathrm{d(e,e^{\prime}p)}
cross sections in the region
1.3k_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim|\mathbf{k}|\lesssim 2.5k_{\mathrm{F}}
also follow the
|\mathbf{k}|^{-4}
scaling. From these theoretical and experimental analyses,
C_{0}\approx 0.15\pm 0.03
is obtained
[
223
]
, after correcting for normalization differences.
Independent photonuclear absorption studies further support the
C/|\mathbf{k}|^{4}
behavior and connect
C_{0}
to the Levinger constant
L
:
C_{0}=\rho\pi L/a_{\mathrm{t}}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}=2L/3\pi k_{\mathrm{F}}a_{\mathrm{t}}
,
with
a_{\mathrm{t}}
being the scattering length for the
{}^{3}\mathrm{S}_{1}
channel. Using
1/k_{\mathrm{F}}a_{\mathrm{t}}\approx 0.15
and
L\approx 5.50\pm 0.21
[
472
]
gives
C_{0}\approx 0.172\pm 0.007
[
472
]
, consistent with previous determinations. Averaging these results yields
C_{0}\approx 0.161\pm 0.015
[
91
,
92
]
. Combining this
C_{0}
with
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
, the HMT cutoff in SNM is then obtained as
\phi_{0}=\left(1-x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}/3C_{0}\right)^{-1}\approx 2.38\pm 0.56
, which is
well within the SRC momentum range of
\sim 300
-
600\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
To fix all isospin-dependent parameters in Eq. (
2-8
), as boundary conditions one also needs information about the EOS and single-neutron momentum distribution in PNM.
Some of the information can be obtained from the state-of-the-art microscopic many-body calculations at sub-saturation densities, see Ref.
[
279
]
for some detailed discussion on this issue, here we only list the main results.
In particular, we have
C_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}\approx 0.12
, consequently a value of

C_{1}\approx-0.25\pm 0.07
is obtained. Similarly, the high momentum
cutoff parameter for PNM is found to be about

\phi_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}\equiv\phi_{0}(1+\phi_{1})=(1-x_{\mathrm{PNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}/3C_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{PNM}})^{-1}\approx 1.04\pm 0.02
.
It is easy to understand that the
\phi_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}
is very
close to unity, since the high momentum neutron fraction in PNM is only about 1.5%.
Finally, inserting the
\phi_{0}
obtained earlier for SNM,

\phi_{1}\approx-0.56\pm 0.10
is found.

FIG. 19
:
(Color Online). Upper: the proton momentum distributions
in all nuclei from
A=2-12
calculated by QMC simulations at
T=0
; figure taken from Ref.
[
481
]
. Lower: the un-normalized population of nucleons in
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
up to momentum
k
(blue) from the prediction shown in the upper panel.

It is worth commenting on the uncertainties associated with the HMT fractions, particularly in PNM. While various many-body approaches consistently predict the qualitative effects of SRCs on the HMT in agreement with experimental observations, the predicted magnitude of the HMT depends on both the model and the nuclear interaction employed. For instance, SCGF calculations using the AV18 interaction yield an HMT fraction of (11-13)% for SNM at saturation density
\rho_{0}
[
382
,
380
]
. In contrast, recent BHF results span a wider range, from roughly 10% with the N
3
LO450 interaction to over 20% with AV18, Paris, or Nij93 interactions
[
296
]
, whereas variational Monte Carlo (VMC) calculations for
12
C indicate an HMT of about 21% in SNM
[
481
]
; see FIG.
19
for the predictions.
Direct experimental information on the isospin dependence of the HMT remains scarce. As discussed earlier, np SRC pairs occur roughly 18-20 times more frequently than pp pairs
[
219
,
422
]
, leading to an estimated HMT in PNM of approximately 1-2%
[
223
]
. However, some theoretical studies, such as SCGF calculations, suggest a significantly larger HMT in PNM, around 4-5%
[
382
,
380
]
. This uncertainty in the HMT magnitude for PNM affects certain quantitative predictions in model calculations. Future calculations of the HMT in PNM using the same models and interactions applied to the PNM EOS are highly desirable to improve accuracy.
A better understanding of the HMT in PNM is crucial. For example, in addition to the HMT-exp parameter set described in the above, the HMT-SCGF parameter set is estimated as
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 12\%
,
x_{\mathrm{PNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 4\%
,
\phi_{0}\approx 1.49
,
\phi_{1}\approx-0.25
,
C_{0}\approx 0.121
and
C_{1}\approx-0.01
[
90
]
.
We will use these HMT sets (namely the HMT-exp and HMT-SCGF) in the following discussions.

As illustrated in FIG.
18
, the HMT of nucleons exhibits a
{1}/{|\mathbf{k}|^{4}}
behavior that closely resembles that observed in two-component (spin-up and spin-down) cold atomic Fermi gases. For the latter systems, this behavior was first predicted by Tan
[
424
,
426
,
425
]
and subsequently confirmed experimentally
[
202
]
. It is important to note that Tan’s general result applies to all two-component Fermi systems with a contact interaction, provided the s-wave scattering length
a
is much larger than the inter-particle distance
d\sim\rho^{-1/3}
, which itself must greatly exceed the effective interaction range
r_{\mathrm{eff}}
, i.e.,
r_{\mathrm{eff}}\ll d\ll a
. In the unitary limit,
k_{\mathrm{F}}a\rightarrow\pm\infty
, Tan’s prediction becomes universal for all Fermi systems.
In nuclei and SNM, however, the HMT is predominantly generated by tensor-force-induced np pairs with
a\approx 5.4\,\mathrm{fm}
and
d\approx 1.8\,\mathrm{fm}
at
\rho_{0}
, as noted in Refs.
[
223
,
472
]
. Consequently, the stringent conditions required for unitary Fermi gases are not fully satisfied in ordinary nuclei and SNM. Nevertheless, the nearly identical
{1}/{|\mathbf{k}|^{4}}
behavior observed in both nuclear systems and cold atomic Fermi gases suggests the presence of underlying universal physics, which merits further theoretical and experimental exploration
[
223
]
.

FIG. 20
:
Scaling function in a dilute Fermi gas for different values of
m
in the asymptotics of the momentum
distribution
n(k)\sim 1/k^{4+m}
given in comparison with the relativistic Fermi gas (RFG) result; grey area shows experimental data from Ref.
[
154
]
. Figure taken from Ref.
[
26
]
.

FIG. 21
:
(Color Online). Nucleon momentum distributions
n(k)
of
{}^{3}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{9}\mathrm{Be}
and
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
extracted from the latest inclusive electron scattering data using
′
-scaling (red squares),
y
-scaling (circles), and results from Ref.
[
118
]
(blue solid squares). Figure taken from Ref.
[
300
]
.

As discussed in Ref.
[
223
]
, although the density of ANM differs from that of ultra-cold Fermi gases by nearly 25 orders of magnitude, the contact that quantifies the probability of finding particle pairs turns out to be surprisingly similar. In a two-component Fermi gas, the two-body correlation function has the general structure
\langle{}_{1}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime}){}_{2}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{0}){}_{2}(\mathbf{0}){}_{1}(\mathbf{r})\rangle=\sum_{i}{}_{i}\phi_{i}(\mathbf{r})\phi_{i}^{\ast}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})
,
treating it as a Hermitian operator in
\mathbf{r}
and
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
. For separations
r\ll d
, where
d
denotes the inter-particle distance, the functions
\phi_{i}
, which act as s-wave Jastrow factors describing the short-distance structure of the many-body wave function, are determined by two-body physics. They take the form
\sin[kr+\delta_{0}(k)]/r
, where
\delta_{0}(k)
is the s-wave phase shift and
k
is the wave vector of the scattering state. The s-wave scattering amplitude

f_{0}(k)=[-k\cot\delta_{0}(k)+ik]^{-1}

(2-19)

is independent of the scattering angle and, in the low-energy limit
k\to 0
, approaches
f_{0}(k\to 0)=-a
, with
a
being the s-wave scattering length that governs low-energy scattering
[
202
]
. Including contributions up to order
k^{2}
in the small-
k
expansion of
\delta_{0}(k)
leads to

f_{0}(k)=-\frac{1}{a^{-1}-k^{2}r_{\mathrm{eff}}/2+ik},

(2-20)

where
r_{\mathrm{eff}}
is the effective range of the interaction. In the unitary limit
a\to\infty
, and for wave vectors
k\ll r_{\mathrm{eff}}^{-1}
, the above expression reduces to the universal form
f_{0}(k)=i/k
, independent of the interaction details. Under these conditions, the wave function
\sin(kr+\delta_{0})/r
simplifies to
\sin\delta_{0}\chi(r)/r
with
\chi(r)=1-r/a
, and thus one obtains

\langle{}_{1}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime}){}_{2}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{0}){}_{2}(\mathbf{0}){}_{1}(\mathbf{r})\rangle=C({\chi(r)}/{r})^{2}=C(r^{-1}-a^{-1})^{2}
,
where
C
is Tan’s contact parameter. At unitarity, the Fourier transform of this short-distance structure generates a high-momentum tail proportional to
|\mathbf{k}|^{-4}
in the momentum distribution.
Many interesting physical issues on Tan’s contact theory and implications are given in Refs.
[
202
,
60
,
365
]
.

While it is intriguing that both nuclei and cold atomic systems near the unitary limit exhibit similar
{1}/{|\mathbf{k}|^{4}}
HMT shapes, it should be emphasized that, for the nuclear case, most supporting evidence remains theoretical. Only limited indirect experimental indications exist so far, in clear contrast to the situation in ultra-cold atomic gases. As noted earlier, a more thorough investigation of the HMT shape in nuclei and nuclear matter, both theoretically and experimentally, remains necessary.
In fact, the study of the form and range of the HMT for Fermions with
k>k_{\mathrm{F}}
has a long history, see, e.g., Refs.
[
327
,
50
,
395
,
132
,
312
,
438
]
. These early works, however, focused almost exclusively on SNM. In such analyses, the HMT of
n(k)=n_{\mathbf{k}}
is often obtained by expanding relevant many-body quantities for a dilute gas of hard spheres in powers of
ak_{\mathrm{F}}
[
235
,
277
]
. In general, the asymptotic momentum distribution is related to the Fourier transform
\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{NN}}(k)
of the nucleon-nucleon interaction via

n(k)\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}\left[\frac{\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{NN}}(k)}{k^{2}}\right]^{2}.

(2-21)

However, whether the required condition for this asymptotic behavior should be
k\to\infty
or
k/A\to\infty
with
A
being the mass number remains unclear
[
26
,
23
,
22
,
25
,
24
,
28
,
29
]
. Moreover, the spin-isospin structure of the short-range NN interaction and the resulting isospin dependence of the HMT are still not well understood.
When a contact interaction is assumed (so
\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{NN}}(k)\to\mathrm{const.}
), the asymptotic tail naturally reduces to
n(k)\sim 1/k^{4}
[
18
]
, consistent with the behavior in ultra-cold atoms near the unitary limit or with the HMT in SNM within the np dominance picture using a zero-range nuclear force. Systematic analyses of scaling functions, such as
y
-scaling and superscaling, in the quasi-elastic (QE) region of inclusive electron-nucleus scattering
[
26
]
indicate that the momentum distribution follows
n(k)\sim k^{-4-m}
with
m\approx 4\sim 5
for
k
up to
(1.59\sim 1.97)k_{\mathrm{F}}
, where
k_{\mathrm{F}}\approx 250\,\mathrm{MeV}/c
is the Fermi momentum. The corresponding NN potentials extracted via inverse Fourier transformation behave as
V_{\mathrm{NN}}(r)\sim 1/r
for
m=4
and
V_{\mathrm{NN}}(r)\sim(1/r)^{1/2}
for
m=5
.

As an illustration, FIG.
20
compares scaling functions calculated for different values of
m
with both the relativistic Fermi Gas (RFG) model and the experimental QE scaling function from Ref.
[
26
]
. A good description of the experimental data is obtained with
m\approx 4.5
, implying a momentum distribution of the form
n(k)\sim k^{-8.5}
. This example demonstrates how studying the HMT can provide insights into the short-range behavior of nuclear forces.
In a recent study, the authors of Ref.
[
300
]
extracted the nucleon momentum distributions for the deuteron,
{}^{3}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{4}\mathrm{He}
,
{}^{9}\mathrm{Be}
and
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
using the
′
-scaling approach and the latest high-precision data. Their analysis indicates that, for all these nuclei, the
′
-scaling method yields results consistent with modern
ab initio
calculations up to
3.5\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
. See FIG.
21
for the results for
n(k)
obtained in this way.

FIG. 22
:
(Color Online). Proton momentum distributions for
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
and
{}^{16}\mathrm{O}
calculated in the LDA for
q>0.6\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
. It used AV18 to evolve
the operator setting
\lambda=1.35\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
using the similarity renormalization group (SRG), and divided each distribution by the proton number
Z
. The gray-shaded sections are where
q<\lambda
. Black
dots correspond to AV18 QMC calculations
[
482
]
. Figure taken from Ref.
[
434
]
.

FIG. 23
:
(Color Online). Upper: the deuteron single nucleon momentum distributions
calculated using the OPE-Pionless EFT approach. Lower: the relative difference between
\rho_{\mathrm{d}}(k)
from the OPE-EFT
and the AV18 potential at large momenta. Figures taken from Ref.
[
511
]
.

An alternative interpretation, however, arises from renormalization-group (RG) evolution to low RG resolution: while SRCs at high resolution appear as large relative-momentum components in the nuclear wave function, RG evolution shifts this physics into the reaction operators without altering observables. This scale separation leads to wave-function factorization, enabling descriptions based on the generalized contact formalism or the low-order correlation operator approximation. At low resolution, the same SRC-driven experimental signatures can be reproduced using simple two-body operators and local-density approximations (LDA) built from uncorrelated wave functions, with the underlying dynamics traced to well-established features of the nucleon-nucleon interaction, such as the tensor force. As an illustration, FIG.
22
shows the LDA proton momentum distributions obtained with the AV18 potential using proton and neutron densities from the Skyrme functional, and compared with QMC results
[
482
]
. For momenta above the gray-shaded region, the LDA describes the high-momentum tails reasonably well, particularly at the largest
q
, while deviations appear below
q=0.6\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
where the approximations break down. The nearly identical high-momentum behavior across nuclei reflects the universal nature of the HMT, whereas the sharp cutoff near
k_{\mathrm{F}}
is expected to be smoothed once higher-order density-matrix-expansion terms and long-range correlations are included
[
434
]
.
In a very recent work, the authors of Ref.
[
511
]
applied the operator product expansion (OPE) within the framework of pionless effective field theory (EFT) to investigate the short-range structure of nuclei. By matching the OPE to selected nuclear potentials for nucleon-nucleon scattering states, the corresponding Wilson coefficients are extracted. The nucleon momentum distribution in the deuteron is subsequently employed to benchmark the OPE predictions against those of these nuclear potentials. To enable a more systematic separation of short- and long-range dynamics, this study further discusses how the OPE approximation can be refined through the inclusion of higher-order EFT interactions and higher-dimension local operators. The upper panel of FIG.
23
shows the deuteron single nucleon distribution function obtained using the OPE-EFT approach, while the lower panel is the relative deviation
[
511
]
:

\left|{\delta\rho(k)}/{\rho_{18}(k)}\right|\equiv\left|{\rho_{\mathrm{OPE}}(k)}/{\rho_{18}(k)}-1\right|,

(2-22)

where
\rho_{18}(k)
is the
\rho_{\mathrm{d}}(k)
evaluated directly with the AV18 potential.
This comparison provides a concise benchmark of the OPE-EFT method, illustrating how accurately the extracted Wilson coefficients reproduce the deuteron momentum distribution. Importantly, because the Wilson coefficients are independent of the external states, the success of this approach demonstrates that short-range information obtained from two-body scattering states can be systematically applied to many-body bound systems. In this way, the OPE-EFT framework provides a controlled and model-independent method to separate short-range nuclear dynamics from long-range structure, enabling predictive calculations of nucleon momentum distributions in larger nuclei while maintaining consistency with underlying NN interactions.

FIG. 24
:
(Color Online). Differential cross sections
\mathrm{d}^{2}M/\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}\Omega
for proton emission, multiplied by
k^{4}
and plotted against

k/k_{\mathrm{F}}
. Figure taken from Ref.
[
212
]
.

FIG. 25
:
(Color Online). Comparison of
k^{4}n(k)
, for five different wave functions with
n(k)≡\widetilde{\phi}^{2}(k)
. Square well (red) solid, Hulthein (blue) dashed, zero-range (black) dot-dashed, surface delta (black) solid, and exponential (black). Figure taken from Ref.
[
328
]
.

We have previously discussed that the high-momentum fraction in SNM is experimentally found to be about
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\pm 4\%
[
219
]
.
Correspondingly, the high-momentum cutoff parameter
\phi_{0}
is constrained as
\phi_{0}\approx 2.4
through the relation
\phi_{0}=(1-x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}/3C_{0})^{-1}
. This implies that high-momentum nucleons are confined to the momentum region
k_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim k\lesssim 2.4k_{\mathrm{F}}
.
As shown in (
2-12
), when calculating the kinetic contribution to the EOS, the integral formally diverges due to the
k^{-4}
form of the HMT, i.e.,

\int^{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}k^{-4}k^{2}\,d\mathbf{k}\sim\int^{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}dk\sim\phi_{0}
,
which diverges linearly as
\phi_{0}\to\infty
. This divergence motivates the introduction of the cutoff
\phi_{0}
. Another motivation is the tension between the experimentally extracted fraction
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
and the contact
C
, since taking
\phi_{0}\to\infty
would yield
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}=3C_{0}
, inconsistent with constraints on
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
. Furthermore, at momenta beyond about
2.4k_{\mathrm{F}}
, three-nucleon correlations may become relevant, though these are significantly more complex than two-nucleon interactions.
Beyond the cutoff
\phi_{0}
, several issues arise concerning the
k^{-4}
form of the HMT:

(a)

Although
\phi_{0}\approx 2.4
derived from
\phi_{0}=(1-x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}/3C_{0})^{-1}
is consistent with some experimental analyses, studies of scaling functions such as
y
-scaling in the QE region of inclusive electron-nucleus scattering suggest that
n_{\mathbf{k}}=n(k)\sim k^{-4-m}
as we discussed earlier
[
26
]
.
This indicates that approximating the HMT as
k^{-4}
over
k_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim k\lesssim 2.4k_{\mathrm{F}}
is not fully accurate. Naturally, using a
k^{-4-m}
form would resolve the divergence issue in the EOS.

(b)

Recent analyses of proton energy spectra from reactions with 47 MeV/u projectiles on Sn and Au targets provide experimental support for the
1/k^{4}
behavior of the momentum distributions, and the observed plateau in the differential cross section only extends from
k\gtrsim k_{\mathrm{F}}
to about
1.3k_{\mathrm{F}}
[
212
]
. Data for higher momenta are currently unavailable, see FIG.
24
.

(c)

The
k^{-4}
form originates from the zero-range assumption of the two-body interaction. However, the momentum distribution cannot be approximated by a zero-range model for

k>k_{\mathrm{eff}}=(ar_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2})^{-1/3}
,
where
a\approx 5.4\,\mathrm{fm}
is the
s
-wave scattering length and
r_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx m_{\phi}^{-1}
is the effective range of the interaction
[
328
]
. For example,
r_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx 1.42\,\mathrm{fm}
for
m_{\pi}\approx 139\,\mathrm{MeV}
or
r_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx 0.39\,\mathrm{fm}
for
m_{\phi}\approx 500\,\mathrm{MeV}
. FIG.
25
illustrates that
k^{4}n(k)
exhibits a plateau in which the contact
\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{4}n(k)
is approximately constant. Beyond this plateau,
k^{4}n(k)
decreases, implying that
n(k)
decays faster than
k^{-4}
.

(d)

Comparisons with ultra-cold atomic Fermi gases show that
k^{4}n(k)
maintains a plateau for
k_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim k\lesssim 2.5k_{\mathrm{F}}
. In contrast, nuclear matter with realistic interactions (e.g.,
\mathrm{N}^{3}\mathrm{LO}
chiral forces) exhibits a plateau only for smaller
k
, i.e.,
k^{4}n(k)
decreases for
k\gtrsim\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}
with
\phi_{0}\approx 1.5
–2
[
223
]
(see FIG.
18
). This highlights that, unlike ultra-cold atoms tuned to the unitary limit with the Feshbach technique
[
114
]
, nuclear matter generally cannot be treated as unitary, and the relevant HMT cutoff
\phi_{0}
must be smaller.

All these considerations indicate that the
k^{-4}
HMT is only an approximate description valid for momenta moderately above
k_{\mathrm{F}}
, but not for arbitrarily large
k
. A natural question then arises: what is the effective form of the HMT at very high momenta? In principle, this requires detailed microscopic calculations incorporating all types of nucleon-nucleon interactions and many-body correlations.
As a first-order estimate, the upper limit of contributions to
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
from higher-momentum nucleons can be obtained from the Lippmann–Schwinger equation
[
328
]
:

n(k)=\frac{8\pi a^{3}}{(k^{2}a^{2}+1)^{2}}\left(1-\Lambda k^{2}r_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}\right),

(2-23)

where
\Lambda\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
. Writing
a^{-1}=\xi k
and
k=\xi^{\prime}r_{\mathrm{eff}}^{-1}
, for
a^{-1}\ll k\ll r_{\mathrm{eff}}^{-1}
we have
0<\xi,\xi^{\prime}\ll 1
, yielding

n(k)\approx\frac{8\pi a^{3}}{k^{4}a^{4}}\cdot\left(1-\Lambda\xi^{\prime 2}-2\xi^{2}+2\Lambda\xi^{2}\xi^{\prime 2}\right)\approx\frac{8\pi a^{3}}{k^{4}a^{4}}\sim\frac{1}{k^{4}},

(2-24)

which recovers the conventional
k^{-4}
form. At even higher
k
,
\xi^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
, giving

\displaystyle n(k)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{8\pi a^{3}}{k^{4}a^{4}}\cdot\left[1-\Lambda\xi^{\prime 2}+2(\Lambda\xi^{\prime 2}-1)\xi^{2}+3(1-\Lambda\xi^{\prime 2})\xi^{4}\right]

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\frac{\xi^{2}}{k^{4}a^{4}}\sim\frac{1}{k^{6}},\quad\xi=\frac{1}{ka},

(2-25)

indicating that for
k\sim r_{\mathrm{eff}}^{-1}
, the HMT can be reasonably modeled as
k^{-6}
. Higher-order corrections scale as
k^{-8},k^{-10},\cdots
.

Finally, the contact coefficient
C_{0}
exhibits universality in both nuclear systems and ultra-cold gases, and it is reasonable to assume that
C_{0}
is independent of the cutoff
\phi_{0}
. QMC simulations in 3D and 2D using various interactions also support this universality
[
365
]
. The zero-momentum depletion
\Delta_{0}
can in principle be computed via Monte Carlo or other theoretical methods; in this review, it is determined via
\Delta_{0}=1-x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
,
directly linking it to the experimental
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%
. Recent studies further investigated the relation between the contact and mean-field distributions, showing that SRCs are present even if
n(k)
does not scale as
1/k^{4}
, regardless of whether the system is superfluid or a Fermi-like liquid
[
72
,
73
]
.
In Subsection
3.5
we estimate the contribution of a
k^{-6}
HMT to the kinetic EOS of nucleonic matter.

2.3 Nucleon E-effective Mass and its Isospin Splitting

In this subsection, we discuss the nucleon E-effective mass (or E-mass for brevity). The concept of nucleon effective mass
M_{\mathrm{N}}^{*}
was originally developed by Brueckner
[
69
]
to describe equivalently the motion of nucleons in a momentum-dependent potential with the motion of a quasi-nucleon of mass

M_{\mathrm{N}}^{\ast}
in a momentum-independent potential. It has been generalized later and widely used to characterize the momentum and/or energy dependence of the single-nucleon potential or the real part of the nucleon self-energy in nuclear medium
[
245
,
318
]
. Basically, the nucleon effective mass reflects leading effects of the space-time non-locality of underlying nuclear interactions and the Pauli exchange principle. It is a fundamental quantity characterizing the nucleon’s propagation in nuclear medium
[
242
,
411
,
338
,
339
]
. In particular, the so-called k-mass and E-mass describe, respectively, the momentum and energy dependence of the single-nucleon potential while the total effective mass is the product of the two
[
279
]
.

FIG. 26
:
(Color Online). Spectroscopic factor as a function of target mass. Taken from Ref.
[
272
]
.

FIG. 27
:
(Color Online). Upper: momentum distribution function in a FFG model (left panel) and the one including interactions (right panel). Figure taken from Ref.
[
122
]
.
Lower: spectral function in an ideal Fermi gas and in a Fermi liquid;
notation conversion:
A\leftrightarrow S
,
\xi_{\mathbf{k}}\leftrightarrow\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}
,
{}_{\mathbf{k}}\leftrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}}
.
Figure taken from Ref.
[
160
]
.

FIG. 28
:
(Color Online).
Left: a typical nucleon spectral function
S(\mathbf{k},E)
; figure taken from Ref.
[
52
]
.
Right: spectral function
S(\mathbf{p},E)
(upper) and the momentum distribution
n(p)
(lower) of
{}^{56}\mathrm{Fe}
. In panel (a), the logarithm of
S(\mathbf{p},E)
is presented to highlight the NN-SRC part, and the region enclosed by a curve describes the mean-field part. The open squares represent the
n(p)
obtained from
y
-scaling analyses on
\mathrm{(e,e^{\prime})}
cross sections; figure taken from Ref.
[
344
]
.

FIG. 29
:
(Color Online).
The
\mathrm{(e,e^{\prime})}
cross sections on
{}^{56}\mathrm{Fe}
using the
S(\mathbf{p},E)
of right panel of FIG.
28
.
Solid line: total inclusive cross sections; dotted line: contributions of mean field part; dashed line: contributions of SRCs.
Figure taken from Ref.
[
344
]
.

The E-mass is directly related to the discontinuity of the single-nucleon momentum distribution at the Fermi surface of nuclear matter
[
245
,
312
,
132
,
395
]
, that is
[
92
]

\frac{M^{\ast,\mathrm{E}}_{J}}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}=1-\frac{\partial U_{J}}{\partial E}\equiv\frac{1}{Z_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}},\text{ with }Z_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}\equiv n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(k^{J}_{\mathrm{F}-0})-n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(k^{J}_{\mathrm{F}+0}),

(2-26)

is the Migdal–Luttinger jump
[
327
,
312
]
of the
single-nucleon momentum distribution
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
at the Fermi
momentum
k^{J}_{\mathrm{F}}
, see FIG.
6
.
The
Z
-factor is closely related to the NS cooling properties, as to be discussed in Subsection
5.2
.
Over the past decades, the nuclear physics community has devoted significant effort to probing the depletion of the nucleon Fermi sphere, mainly through transfer, pick-up, and
\mathrm{(e,e^{\prime}p)}
reactions. These measurements, usually expressed in terms of nucleon spectroscopic factors, provide direct constraints on
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(k_{\mathrm{F}-0}^{J})
[
245
,
318
,
242
,
272
]
. As shown in FIG.
26
, where the spectroscopic factors are displayed as a function of target mass, it is well established that mean-field theory typically overpredicts the data by about 20
\sim
40%, reflecting its neglect of dynamical correlations.
In parallel, more recent analyses of inclusive and exclusive electron-nucleus scattering
[
223
,
219
,
123
,
164
,
408
,
466
,
259
,
186
]
, nucleon-nucleus reactions
[
427
,
362
]
, and medium-energy photon-nuclear absorption
[
472
]
have provided quantitative information on the size, shape, and isospin dependence of the HMT above the Fermi surface, as discussed in detail in Subsection
2.2
, thereby constraining
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(k_{\mathrm{F}+0}^{J})
. Taken together, these constraints, on both the depletion below
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
and the HMT above it, offer an important empirical determination of the Migdal–Luttinger jump and hence of the nucleon E-mass in neutron-rich nucleonic matter.

The
Z
-factor is a fundamental quantity in many-body physics and is closely related to the spectral function, which provides a direct link to experimental data
[
441
]
. For instance, the reaction
(\mathrm{e},\mathrm{e^{\prime}}\mathrm{p})
can probe the hole spectral function in typical finite nuclei. Because the electron interacts weakly with nucleons, it serves as an ideal probe for studying nuclear structure. In electron scattering, the dominant excitation operators are primarily one-body in nature. When the incident electron has sufficient energy, the knocked-out proton can be energetic enough for the impulse approximation to be applicable. If the electron transfers a substantial amount of energy, the resulting excited state is expected to be dominated by a simple particle-hole configuration, corresponding to an outgoing particle influenced by the surrounding medium and a valence hole selected by the reaction kinematics. Additional interactions between this particle and the hole can then be neglected. In the impulse approximation, the energy
\omega
and momentum
\mathbf{q}
lost by the electron are transferred to a proton with binding (missing) energy
E_{\mathrm{m}}
and (missing) momentum
-\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{m}}
, which exits the nucleus with momentum
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{m}}
and kinetic energy
T_{\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}
. The proton knockout cross section is proportional to the spectral function,

\mathrm{d}\sigma\sim S(\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{m}},E_{\mathrm{m}})
,
where
S(\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{m}},E_{\mathrm{m}})
is the hole spectral function, i.e., the probability of finding a proton with binding energy
E_{\mathrm{m}}
and momentum
\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{m}}
in the parent nucleus. The spectroscopic factor of the last valence orbit across different nuclei is shown in FIG.
26
, indicating a roughly 30% global reduction of single-particle strength for these valence holes in most nuclei. This significant deviation from mean-field predictions requires a detailed explanation based on nuclear correlations. In quantum field theory, the cross section can similarly be computed from time-ordered correlation functions (
S
-matrix) under appropriate conditions.
The nucleon (or other fermions like electrons) momentum distribution can be derived from the Green’s function or spectral function as
[
151
]
*2
*2

*2

Spin and other degrees of freedom are suppressed, so
n_{\mathbf{k}}\to n_{\mathbf{k}\sigma}
, etc.

n_{\mathbf{k}}=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\pi}{S(\mathbf{k},\omega)},

(2-27)

where
\omega
is measured below the Fermi surface.
The spectral function in nuclear system is discussed in details in Ref.
[
381
]
.

For a FFG, the momentum distribution reduces to the step function
\Theta(\mu-E_{\mathbf{k}})
. When interactions are included, a sharp quasi-particle peak appears in the spectral function
[
122
,
151
]
,

\displaystyle{S(\mathbf{k},\omega)}/{2\pi}=

\displaystyle{\pi}^{-1}\mathrm{Im}\,G(\mathbf{k},\omega-i0^{+})=Z_{\mathbf{k}}\delta(\omega-\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})+\cdots,

(2-28)

where
\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}=E_{\mathbf{k}}-\mu
and
Z_{\mathbf{k}}
is the wave-function renormalization, interpreted as the residue of the single-nucleon pole in the exact propagator. In a FFG, the spectral function is a sharp delta function at
\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}
(lower-left panel of FIG.
27
) while in an interacting Fermi liquid the quasi-particle forms a broadened peak of width
\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}}
at
\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}
(lower-right panel of FIG.
27
). At the Fermi momentum, this peak becomes infinitely sharp, corresponding to a long-lived quasi-particle on the Fermi surface
[
254
]
. At the Fermi momentum, the peak becomes infinitely sharp, corresponding to a long-lived quasi-particle. The quasi-particle peak weight is
Z_{\mathbf{k}}^{-1}\sim M_{\mathrm{N}}^{\ast,\mathrm{E}}
, with
M_{\mathrm{N}}^{\ast,\mathrm{E}}
the nucleon E-effective mass. The nucleon momentum distribution then reads

n_{\mathbf{k}}=Z_{\mathbf{k}}\Theta(-\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})+\text{smooth background}.

(2-29)

Finite temperature induces a continuous HMT in
n_{\mathbf{k}}
(upper-left panel of FIG.
27
), while interactions at zero temperature produce a discrete HMT (upper-right panel of FIG.
27
)
[
122
]
. Although interactions smear the momentum distribution, the jump at
k_{\mathrm{F}}
survives in a reduced form, which is just the Migdal–Luttinger jump
[
327
,
312
]
. In a Landau Fermi liquid, the Fermi surface volume measures the particle density, and since quasi-particle and unrenormalized particle Fermi surfaces coincide, the Fermi surface volume is an adiabatic invariant under interactions, known as the Luttinger theorem (Luttinger sum rule)
[
312
,
311
]
.
A typical nucleon spectral function is shown in the left panel of FIG.
28
, from which one can see its peaked structure around certain energies and momenta.
Shown in the right panel of FIG.
28
is the spectral function
S(\mathbf{p},E)
(upper panel) and the momentum distribution
n(p)
(lower panel) of
{}^{56}\mathrm{Fe}
using the light-front dynamics (LFD). The correlated part of the spectral function
S_{\mathrm{corr}}(p)\sim n_{\mathrm{corr}}(p)
with the latter being deduced from the LFD wave functions
[
104
]
.
In panel (a), the logarithm of
S(\mathbf{p},E)
is presented to highlight the NN-SRC part, and the region enclosed by a curve describes the mean-field part. The open squares represent the
n(p)
obtained from
y
-scaling analyses of
\mathrm{(e,e^{\prime})}
cross sections.
One can see that the “RMF+LFD” prediction for the
n(p)
is quite consistent with the
y
-scaling result.
NN-SRC nucleons account for about
25\%
of nucleons, but contribute
\sim 16\%
to the
\mathrm{(e,e^{\prime})}
cross section at the kinematics of FIG.
29
; their contribution grows at higher
Q^{2}
, making such kinematics ideal for extracting SRC strength.
