We now discuss the numerical implication on the
Z
-factor and related E-mass.
Most phenomenological models approximate the depletion of the Fermi sea by introducing a constant reduction factor
\Delta_{J}
, as illustrated in FIG.
6
. To explore the role of the two parameters entering
\beta_{J}=\beta_{0}(1+\tau_{3}^{J}\beta_{1}\delta)
in the single-nucleon momentum distribution
n^{J}_{\mathbf{k}}(\rho,\delta)
of Eq. (
2-8
), we first recall that these parameters encode how the depletion behaves at finite momenta below and near the Fermi surface, thereby influencing the E-mass obtained from the Migdal–Luttinger theorem. A convenient way to estimate their impact is to adopt a widely used parametrization of the momentum distribution e.g., from Ref.
[
119
]
, which is based on microscopic many-body calculations. For momenta
|\mathbf{k}|\lesssim 2\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
, the function
I
behaves approximately as
\exp[{-\alpha|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}]
where
\alpha\approx 0.12\,\mathrm{fm}^{2}
. Since
\alpha k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\approx 0.21\ll 1
at
\rho_{0}
, this allows one to employ
\exp[{-\alpha|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}]\approx 1-\alpha|\mathbf{k}|^{2}+\mathcal{O}(|\mathbf{k}|^{4})
for
0<|\mathbf{k}|<k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
, making the quadratic form

{I}\left({|\mathbf{k}|}/{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\right)=\left({|\mathbf{k}|}/{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\right)^{2}

(2-30)

a reasonable and effective representation of the depletion. The constants in the parametrization of Ref.
[
119
]
are then absorbed into
\Delta_{J}
and
\beta_{J}
, and Eq. (
2-11
) gives
\Delta_{J}=1-3\beta_{J}/5-3{C}_{J}(1-1/\phi_{J})
. This leads in SNM to
\beta_{0}=(5/3)[1-\Delta_{0}-x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}]
; using the predicted
\Delta_{0}\approx 0.88\pm 0.03
[
506
,
354
,
180
]
together with
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 0.28\pm 0.04
yields
\beta_{0}\approx-0.27\pm 0.08
. Furthermore, demanding
\beta_{J}=\beta_{0}(1+\beta_{1}\tau_{3}^{J}\delta)<0
so that
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
decreases monotonically towards
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
imposes
|\beta_{1}|\leq 1
.
A nonzero
\beta_{J}
directly influences the Migdal–Luttinger “renormalization function”
Z^{J}_{\mathrm{F}}
. For SNM, one has
Z_{\mathrm{F}}^{0}=1+2\beta_{0}/5-C_{0}-x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 0.45\pm 0.07
(or
0.56\pm 0.04
when
\beta_{0}
is omitted).

To apply the Migdal–Luttinger theorem
[
327
,
312
]
and extract the E-mass, the nucleon momentum distribution must be known both below and above the Fermi surface. In the previous subsection we focused on the contact and HMT contributions around and above
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
; here we first summarize the depletion below the Fermi surface and then assemble all information for the E-mass extraction.
The depletion parameter
\kappa_{J}
, which quantifies the occupation deficit below
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
, provides a useful indicator of the extent to which the HVH theorem
[
239
]
and independent-particle pictures may fail. Larger depletion corresponds to stronger deviations
[

,

,

,
143
]
. The sensitivity of
\kappa_{J}
to the tensor force was established long ago
[

,
374
]
. For the parametrization in Eq. (
2-8
), the average depletion at
\rho_{0}
in ANM is
[
92
]

\displaystyle\kappa_{J}=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{15}\beta_{J}+3C_{J}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{J}}\right).

(2-31)

In SNM, one finds
\kappa\equiv\kappa_{0}=4\beta_{0}/15+x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 0.21\pm 0.06
. FIG.
30
shows the
k_{\mathrm{F}}
-dependence of the depletion
\kappa
(upper) and the corresponding

-factor (lower) calculated within the BHF approach using a separable representation of the Paris interaction
[
43
]
. Our extracted Fermi-surface discontinuity
Z_{\mathrm{F}}^{0}
and the depletion
\kappa
are found to be very close to the result of Ref.
[

]
.
Similarly, FIG.

shows the
Z
-factor obtained in the SCGF approach for the three interactions considered (CDBonn, AV18, and N
3
LO), see panels (d)-(f). Overall,
Z(k)
reaches its maximum near

with values of approximately

-

, decreases toward a minimum around

, and then rises again to approach

at high momenta; see Ref.
[

]
for more related discussions.
In contrast, the situation in ANM is more uncertain, primarily because the isovector parameter
\beta_{1}
remains poorly constrained, despite its direct impact on both
Z_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}
and the neutron-proton E-mass splitting.
The result is shown in the upper panel of FIG.

, and the decrease (increase) of neutron (proton) depletion with isospin asymmetry
\delta
follows an approximate linear trend, in agreement with microscopic studies
[

,

[

The splitting

\kappa_{\mathrm{n}}-\kappa_{\mathrm{p}}\approx[8\beta_{0}\beta_{1}/15+6C_{0}\phi_{1}/\phi_{0}+6C_{0}C_{1}(1-\phi_{0}^{-1})]\delta\approx(-0.37\pm 0.16)\delta
.

Using the parametrization of Eq. (

),

Z_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}=\Delta_{J}+\beta_{J}-C_{J}=1+2\beta_{J}/5-4C_{J}+3C_{J}\phi_{J}^{-1}
.
In SNM, this becomes

Z_{\mathrm{F}}^{0}=1+2\beta_{0}/5-C_{0}-x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
,
which leads to
M_{0}^{\ast,\mathrm{E}}/M\approx 2.22\pm 0.35
when combined with the empirical values of
\beta_{0}
,

,

, and

. The dependence on
\beta_{0}
is modest, as shown in the upper panel of FIG.

. Comparisons with a broad spectrum of earlier microscopic and phenomenological results are also displayed there
[

,

,

]
.
The term

directly reflects the SRC strength; thus stronger SRC increase the E-mass. This is consistent with the contrast between BHF (with SRC via ladder diagrams and TBF) and RHF (without explicit SRC) predictions
[
278
]
. In PNM, the corresponding jump

Z_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}=1+2\beta_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}/5-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}-C_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}

is reduced mainly by
C_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}\approx 0.12
, because
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}^{\mathrm{PNM}}\approx 1.5\%
is extremely small.

In neutron-rich matter, the neutron-proton E-mass splitting can be written as
({M_{\mathrm{n}}^{\ast,\mathrm{E}}-M_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast,\mathrm{E}}})/{M_{\mathrm{N}}}=s_{\mathrm{E}}\delta+t_{\mathrm{E}}\delta^{3}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{5})
.
The dependence of
s_{\mathrm{E}}
and
t_{\mathrm{E}}
on
\beta_{1}
is displayed in the lower panel of FIG.
33
. For
\beta_{1}=-1,0,1
, one finds, respectively,

s_{\mathrm{E}}(\beta_{1}=-1)\approx-3.29\pm 1.23
,
t_{\mathrm{E}}(\beta_{1}=-1)\approx-1.49\pm 1.47
;

s_{\mathrm{E}}(\beta_{1}=0)\approx-2.22\pm 0.84
,
t_{\mathrm{E}}(\beta_{1}=0)\approx-0.41\pm 0.42
;

s_{\mathrm{E}}(\beta_{1}=1)\approx-1.16\pm 0.64
,
t_{\mathrm{E}}(\beta_{1}=1)\approx-0.09\pm 0.05
.
A robust qualitative conclusion is that in neutron-rich matter one always finds
M_{\mathrm{n}}^{\ast,\mathrm{E}}<M_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast,\mathrm{E}}
, consistent with predictions of most microscopic many-body models. However, the magnitude of the splitting is quite sensitive to the isovector parameter
\beta_{1}
, which remains experimentally unconstrained. Future measurements of the isospin dependence of SRC, via proton or electron scattering on isotopic chains, and of nucleon spectroscopic factors using radioactive beams have strong potential to constrain
\beta_{1}
and thus to pin down the neutron-proton E-mass splitting more precisely.

2.4 Quasi-deuteron: Embedding SRCs into Relativistic Density Functionals

Recently, Burrello and Typel develop an extended relativistic energy density functional (EDF) to embed SRCs directly into the nuclear EOS
[
77
]
. The motivation stems from a fundamental inconsistency: modern electron-scattering experiments show that a significant fraction of nucleons (approximately
\gtrsim 20\%
) participate in strongly correlated np pairs even at and above the saturation density
\rho_{0}
, while standard EDFs based on mean-field theory predict that all light clusters, including the deuteron, should dissolve as
\rho_{0}
is approached. In other words, traditional EDFs assume that nuclear matter becomes an uncorrelated Fermi gas at
\rho_{0}
, whereas experiments demonstrate that SRCs remain an important and persistent mode of many-body correlation at these densities.

To resolve this conflict, the authors reinterpret SRC pairs as
in-medium quasi-deuterons
, effective composite degrees of freedom introduced into the functional alongside nucleons.
Quasi-deuterons
immersed in dense matter are used as surrogate for correlations.
The key innovation is the parametrization of the
in-medium deuteron binding energy (mass shift)
, which is allowed to evolve with density such that correlated np pairs do not disappear at saturation density, instead they survive in dense matter as SRC-like quasiparticles. The mass shift is constrained by (i) microscopic few-body calculations at low densities, (ii) empirical SRC fractions near
\rho_{0}
, and (iii) physical requirements such as maintaining a positive Dirac effective mass for nucleons at high densities.
A central quantity in the formulation is the meson–cluster coupling scaling factor
\chi
, which controls the interaction strength between the quasi-deuterons and the background mean fields. The authors demonstrate that conventional choices (
\chi=1
) lead to unphysical behavior, such as the impossibility of defining a unique deuteron fraction across the full density range, thereby forcing the model toward the traditional result where clusters disappear below or near saturation. However, for more realistic values (
\chi\leq 1/\sqrt{2}
), the model supports smooth and physically consistent deuteron fractions for all densities, allowing SRC-like correlations to persist naturally in the EOS.

The presence of quasi-deuterons modifies the macroscopic properties of nuclear matter. Near
\rho_{0}
, the additional attraction from correlated pairs increases the binding energy compared with standard mean-field functionals. At higher densities, the stiffness of the EOS and the behavior of the symmetry energy become sensitive to the value of
\chi
, leading to a range of physically plausible scenarios, as shown in FIG.
34
for the symmetry energy (the solid red line is the conventional
model without the quasi-deuteron mechanism).
Some differences emerge among the parameterizations
which account for the presence of the deuterons
below
\rho_{0}
.
Moreover, the model recovers the correct low-density limit: as
\rho\to 0
, the energy per nucleon approaches one half of the vacuum deuteron binding energy
[
77
]
, unlike traditional mean-field EDFs that neglect clustering.
Overall, this work represents the first systematic attempt to incorporate SRC physics not by heuristic adjustments to single-nucleon momentum distributions, but by embedding the correlations directly into the EDF through modified composite degrees of freedom. This framework may provide a starting point for future extensions, including momentum-dependent mass shifts, heavier cluster species, and applications to NS matter.

FIG. 35
:
(Color Online). Upper: the ratio between the nuclear contacts of pp and np channels and the ratio of the SRC orbital entanglement entropies.
Nuclear contacts are extracted from the two-body wave function in
k
-space (left) and
r
-space (right)
[
468
]
.
Lower: calculated ratios of reduced nuclear contacts extracted from the
k
-space (left) and from the
r
-space (right) nuclear two-body density distribution as a function of the nuclear mass number. The blue dashed line indicates a value of 2 on the vertical axis.
Figures taken from Ref.
[
261
]
.

2.5 Orbital Entanglement Entropy of SRC

In this subsection, we briefly discuss the orbital entanglement entropy associated with SRCs in quantum many-body systems. A key new insight is the connection between the strength of SRCs, the emergence of high-momentum components in single-particle occupations, and the resulting entanglement structure
[
72
,
73
]
. The universal SRC-induced HMT
n(k)\sim C/k^{4}
tails, observed in nuclei and cold atomic gases, significantly alter the standard relation between kinetic energy and occupation probabilities far from the Fermi surface, particularly in dynamical situations. Consequently, SRCs increase the orbital entanglement entropy of fermionic systems
[
72
]
, with important implications for nonequilibrium phenomena such as heavy-ion collisions and nuclear fission.
The orbital entanglement entropy provides a well-defined and intrinsic measure of complexity of many-fermion wave functions, vanishing for a single Slater determinant and increasing with correlations and superpositions of multiple determinants. Its evolution in nonequilibrium processes, such as nuclear fission, illustrates the localization of the many-body wave function in Fock and Hilbert space, and captures both short-range and long-range correlations. Thus, orbital entanglement entropy offers a natural and quantitative link between SRC-induced high-momentum components and the complexity of strongly interacting quantum systems
[
72
]
.

Building on the preceding discussion of SRCs, we now focus on their impact on single-particle momentum distributions and entanglement in quantum many-body systems. Even for momenta near or slightly above the local Fermi momentum, SRCs significantly modify the standard mean-field occupation probabilities,

n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k)=\frac{1}{1+\exp[\beta\epsilon(k)]},

(2-32)

where
\beta=1/k_{\mathrm{B}}T
,
\epsilon(k)=E(k)-\mu=k^{2}/2M_{\mathrm{N}}-\mu
is the single nucleon kinetic energy, where
\mu
is the chemical potential. While this formula applies in a narrow energy window around the Fermi surface for quasi-equilibrium and weakly interacting systems, decades of studies in nuclei and cold atomic gases demonstrate that SRCs require more sophisticated treatments.

Recent work has emphasized the intimate link between SRCs and quantum entanglement
[
73
]
: particles interacting at distances shorter than the average interparticle separation naturally become entangled, as in paradigmatic examples such as the EPR scenario or Young’s double-slit experiment. Consequently, many-body systems with SRCs are entangled across all energy scales, independent of equilibrium. The corresponding entanglement entropy directly influences the momentum distribution and nonequilibrium dynamics of the system. For isolated quantum systems in a pure state, entanglement entropy evolves in time and, in the long run, can describe equilibration processes in a manner analogous to Boltzmann entropy in dilute, weakly interacting systems. Thus, entanglement entropy provides a unified measure capturing both mean-field and SRCs in strongly interacting many-body systems.

In Ref.
[
73
]
, the nucleon momentum distribution accounting for SRCs was parametrized as

n(k)=\eta(k_{0})\begin{cases}n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k),&k\leq k_{0},\\
n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k_{0})\left(\dfrac{k_{0}}{k}\right)^{4},&k_{0}<k<\Lambda,\end{cases}

(2-33)

where
n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k)
is the mean-field distribution,
k_{0}
marks the transition to the HMT, and
\eta(k_{0})
is a normalization factor reflecting the depletion of the Fermi sea due to correlations. The HMT is characterized by the Tan’s contact
[
424
,
426
,
425
]

C(k_{0})=\eta(k_{0})\,n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k_{0})k_{0}^{4},

(2-34)

which measures the strength of SRCs, while the total distribution is normalized to the saturation density:

\rho_{0}=A/V=g\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}n(k)=\frac{gk_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}}{6\pi^{2}},

(2-35)

here
g
is the degeneracy of the system.
The factor
\eta(k_{0})
is obtained from particle number conservation
[
73
]

\eta(k_{0})=\frac{\int_{0}^{\Lambda}dk\,k^{2}n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k)}{k_{0}^{3}n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k_{0})+\int_{0}^{\Lambda}dk\,k^{2}n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k)}.

(2-36)

Assuming that
\Lambda=\infty
and
\rho_{0}=g\int[{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}}/{(2\pi)^{3}}]n_{\mathrm{mf}}(k)
,
a lower limit for
\eta(k_{0})
can be obtained in the case of a FFG at zero temperature by choosing
k_{0}=k_{\mathrm{F}}
. By putting the HMT part of the momentum distribution into the (orbital) entanglement entropy
[
73
]
, one then obtains
[
358
]

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{SRC}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{g}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}n(k)\ln n(k)+\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\left[1-n(k)\right]\ln\left[1-n(k)\right]\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-\frac{g}{2\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{C}{k_{\mathrm{F}}}\ln\left(\frac{C/k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}{1-C/k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}\right)+k_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}\ln\left(1-\frac{C}{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3\rho_{0}\left[\frac{C}{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}\ln\left(\frac{C/k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}{1-C/k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}\right)+\ln\left(1-\frac{C}{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}\right)\right],

(2-37)

where
C/k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}=c_{\mathrm{np}}
is the reduced np contact. Consequently
[
358
]

\boxed{S_{\mathrm{SRC}}\approx-3\rho_{0}\left[c_{\mathrm{np}}\ln\left(\frac{c_{\mathrm{np}}}{1-c_{\mathrm{np}}}\right)+\ln\left(1-c_{\mathrm{np}}\right)\right],}

(2-38)

which clearly shows that the orbital entanglement entropy is proportional to the mass number
A
, since
\rho_{0}=A/V
. The nuclear structure orbital entanglement entropy of SRCs is calculated based on the nuclear scale separation, specifically considering the entanglement between the SRC orbitals and the rest of the system. It should be emphasized that this is a single-nucleon entanglement entropy, not a pairwise entanglement between a proton and a neutron. The entanglement arises from the probability for a nucleon to occupy a momentum state above the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
. The nuclear momentum space separates into two parts: nucleons occupy either the mean-field part of the wave function, i.e., the Fermi sea, or the high-momentum SRC part. The orbital entropy is computed between these two subspaces, where one contains all low-momentum Fermi sea states and the other contains the high-momentum SRC states, treated as a distinct SRC “orbital” that can be multiply occupied. Since the occupation probability of a single SRC is given by the nuclear contact, a general expression for the SRC orbital entanglement entropy can be derived using the GCF
[
473
,
130
,
128
,
298
,
299
,
470
,
471
,
474
,
500
]
. The GCF approach allows one to obtain the scaling of the entropy with the mass number, and it shows that, unlike typical entanglement entropies in quantum systems which scale with the surface area
\sim A^{2/3}
, the SRC orbital entanglement entropy in large nuclei is extensive and scales linearly with
A
[
358
]
.

SRC increase the orbital entanglement entropy because nucleons acquire a finite probability of occupying the high-momentum states above the Fermi sea. Without SRCs, all nucleons reside in the low-momentum Fermi sea, resulting in minimal entropy. The presence of SRCs introduces uncertainty about whether a nucleon occupies a Fermi sea or an SRC state, directly enhancing the entanglement entropy. This contribution scales with the nuclear density and thus grows linearly with the mass number, making the entropy extensive in large nuclei. SRC-induced HMT of single-particle occupation probabilities not only raise the entanglement entropy of fermionic systems but may also influence the dynamics of nuclear reactions such as heavy-ion collisions and fission
[
73
]
.

Using Eq. (
2-38
), one can define a ratio that describes the relative SRC entanglement entropies and reduced nuclear contacts in different channels
[
261
]
:

R(c_{\mathrm{pp}},c_{\mathrm{np}})=\frac{S_{\mathrm{SRC}}^{\mathrm{pp}}/S_{\mathrm{SRC}}^{\mathrm{np}}}{c_{\mathrm{pp}}/c_{\mathrm{np}}},

(2-39)

where for the
\mathrm{pp}
channel one considers the contribution from spin-0 pairs, while for the
\mathrm{np}
channel the total contribution from both spin-0 and spin-1 pairs are included. The nuclear contacts of symmetric nuclei were extracted in Ref.
[
468
]
.
The study of Ref.
[
261
]
reveals that the entanglement entropies and nuclear contacts for pp and np SRC pairs follow a scaling relation. In particular, the proportionality of entanglement entropy between pp and np pairs is directly related to the ratio of the corresponding nuclear contacts, which is found to be approximately 2.0, as shown in FIG.
35
. Although the exact reason why this ratio converges to 2 in all examined nuclei is not yet understood, the introduction of entanglement entropy provides an additional constraint on the values of nuclear contacts. The authors suggest that this scaling behavior is universal for all symmetric nuclei and provide a possible explanation for this phenomenon
[
261
]
.

§3 Effects of SRC-HMT on Cold Dense Matter EOS

This section discusses the SRC-HMT effects on the EOS of cold ANM. In Subsection
3.1
, we introduce the relativistic free Fermi gas (RFFG) model and emphasize its behavior in a general spatial dimension
d
. In Subsection
3.2
, we examine the reduction of the kinetic symmetry energy caused by the SRC-induced HMT and also provide the relativistic corrections to the kinetic symmetry energies; in addition, the validity of the parabolic approximation for the EOS of ANM is analyzed at both the kinetic level and the full level. In Subsection
3.3
, the SRC-induced HMT is incorporated into the Walecka model (using the nonlinear version as an example) and into the modified Gogny-type energy density functional, and the resulting overall reduction (softening) of the nuclear symmetry energy at supra-saturation densities is highlighted. In Subsection
3.4
, we generalize the EOS of ANM to a general dimension
d
, following the strategy used in Subsection
3.1
and employing the HVH theorem as the main tool; in particular, we examine the EOS in 2D and outline several general features. We also explore the
\epsilon
-expansion of the EOS around a reference dimension and highlight several interesting implications. Finally, we estimate the impact of an additional HMT of the form
k^{-\sigma}
with
\sigma>4
on the kinetic part of the nuclear EOS in Subsection
3.5
; since such a tail has been suggested by microscopic studies, our estimate provides a useful reference for assessing its potential significance.

3.1 EOS of a Relativistic Free Fermi Gas

In this subsection, we briefly review the non-relativistic and relativistic FFG models, providing insights relevant for later discussions. As a reminder of the basic formalisms and notations used in the present work, we first recall the predictions of the non-relativistic FFG model in a 3D coordinate space.
The average kinetic energy per nucleon in ANM is given by
[
96
]

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho,\delta)=

\displaystyle\left.\left[\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mathrm{n}}}\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}+\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mathrm{p}}}\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\right]\right/2\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{5}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\frac{1}{2}\left[(1+\delta)^{5/3}+(1-\delta)^{5/3}\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{10M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\delta^{2}+\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{162M_{\mathrm{N}}}\delta^{4}+\frac{7k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{4374M_{\mathrm{N}}}\delta^{6}.

(3-1)

From this, one obtains
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)={k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}/{162M_{\mathrm{N}}}
and
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=7k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/4374M_{\mathrm{N}}
besides the well-known expressions
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/10M_{\mathrm{N}}
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)={k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}/{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}
.
Using
k_{\mathrm{F}}=(3\pi^{2}\rho/2)^{1/3}\approx 263\,\mathrm{MeV}
corresponding to the saturation density
\rho_{0}\approx 0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, one finds
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 22.2\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 12.3\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 0.45\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 0.12\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
Beside, we obtain:

\Psi\equiv{E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)}/{E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)}=27^{-1}.

(3-2)

These predictions serve as useful references for evaluating effects of relativistic kinematics, SRCs, and nuclear interactions missing in the model. For instance, to reproduce the empirical binding energy of about
-16\,\mathrm{MeV}
at
\rho_{0}
, an interaction contribution of about
-38.2\,\mathrm{MeV}
is required. Similarly, with a kinetic symmetry energy of about 12.3 MeV, an interaction contribution of
\sim 20
MeV is needed to reproduce the total empirical nuclear symmetry energy of 32 MeV
[
279
]
.
While the FFG model predicts a fourth-order kinetic symmetry energy of
\sim 0.45
MeV, several microscopic theories and phenomenological models estimate empirically a total
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})
below
\sim 2
MeV
[
276
,
65
,
420
,
86
,
401
,
203
,
366
]
. There is, however, no consensus on the empirical value of
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})
, and the situation is even less certain for the sixth-order symmetry energy
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(\rho_{0})
. Thus, we cannot determine whether the relative kinetic contribution to the total symmetry energy decreases from
\delta^{2}
to higher-order terms.

The relativistic kinetic energy
T(\mathbf{k})=\sqrt{\mathbf{k}^{2}+M_{\mathrm{N}}^{2}}-M_{\mathrm{N}}
can be expanded as

\displaystyle T(\mathbf{k})\approx\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(1-\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}^{2}}+\frac{\mathbf{k}^{4}}{8M_{\mathrm{N}}^{4}}-\frac{5\mathbf{k}^{6}}{64M_{\mathrm{N}}^{6}}+\frac{7\mathbf{k}^{8}}{128M_{\mathrm{N}}^{8}}\right),

(3-3)

to order
\mathbf{k}^{10}
. Considering ANM in a coordinate space of arbitrary dimension
d
, define

\displaystyle\langle k^{\sigma}(\rho,\delta)\rangle\equiv

\displaystyle\left.\left[\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mathrm{n}}}k^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}^{d}\mathbf{k}+\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mathrm{p}}}k^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}^{d}\mathbf{k}\right]\right/2\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\mathbf{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Upsilon\left[(1+\delta)^{1+\sigma/d}+(1-\delta)^{1+\sigma/d}\right],

(3-4)

where
\Upsilon
is independent of
\delta
, and
\sigma/d
controls the expansion. Here
\sigma=2
is the non-relativistic kinetic energy,
\sigma=4
the first relativistic correction, and
\sigma=6
the second, etc. The Fermi momentum in ANM is
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}=k_{\mathrm{F}}(1+\tau_{3}^{J}\delta)^{1/d}
, with
k_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho)=[\rho 2^{d-2}\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(d/2+1)]^{1/d}\sim\rho^{1/d}
.
Expanding around
\delta=0
gives

\displaystyle\langle k^{\sigma}(\rho,\delta)\rangle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}k_{\mathrm{sym},2j}^{\sigma}(\rho)\delta^{2j}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle k_{0}^{\sigma}(\rho)+k_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\sigma}(\rho)\delta^{2}+k^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\delta^{4}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{6}),

(3-5)

with
k_{0}^{\sigma}(\rho)\equiv k_{\mathrm{sym},0}^{\sigma}(\rho)
and
k_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\sigma}(\rho)\equiv k_{\mathrm{sym,2}}^{\sigma}(\rho)
. The ratio of quartic to quadratic coefficients is

\displaystyle{}^{\sigma}_{d}(j=2)\equiv\frac{k^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)}{k^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)}=\frac{1}{12}\left(\frac{\sigma}{d}-2\right)\left(\frac{\sigma}{d}-1\right).

(3-6)

Depending on the value of
\sigma/d
, this ratio is not necessarily as small as the
E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)/E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=1/27
in the FFG model in 3D. For example, if one takes
\sigma=4
and
d=1
, i.e., the average of the momentum to the fourth power (corresponding to the first relativistic correction) in dimension 1, one obtains
{}^{4}_{1}(j=2)=1/2
.
As indicated by Eq. (
3-6
), the
ratio
E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)/E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
is zero in both 1D and 2D (in fact the quartic terms in 1D and 2D are identically zero), while it is
1/27
in 3D as normally used in preparing the EOS for modeling NSs and various studies of heavy-ion reactions. Studies of nuclear EOSs in 1D and 2D and their observational effects in collectively moving sub-systems in heavy-ion reactions and/or NSs might be interesting, we will come back to discuss this issue in Subsection
3.4
.

FIG. 36
:
(Color Online). The ratio
{}_{d}^{\sigma}(j)=k^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sym},2j+2}/k^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sym},2j}
as a function of
j
for different
d
. Here, panels (a-f) have
\sigma=2
, panels (e-h) have
\sigma=4
, panels (i-l) have
\sigma=6
. The vertical dashed line corresponds to
j=1
.
Figure taken from Ref.
[
96
]
.

As
d\to\infty
while keeping
\sigma
fixed, the isospin dependence of
\langle k^{\sigma}(\rho,\delta)\rangle
becomes weak (approaching zero) as indicated by Eq. (
3.1
). However, the isospin-expansion terms are not strictly zero. Specifically, one finds

k_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\sigma}(\rho)=\Upsilon\left(\frac{g}{6}-\frac{g^{2}}{12}-\frac{g^{3}}{6}+\frac{g^{4}}{12}\right),\quad k_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\sigma}(\rho)=\Upsilon(g+g^{2}),

(3-7)

with
g=\sigma/d
. Both quantities approach zero as
g\to 0
in the infinite-
d
limit, yet their ratio remains finite:

{k_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\sigma}(\rho)}/{k_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\sigma}(\rho)}\to 6^{-1},

(3-8)

i.e., in an imagined space of infinite dimensions the ratio
\Psi
takes the value of
1/6\approx 16.7
%, which is independent of the power
\sigma
and is about five times the FFG model prediction in 3D.
More generally, the ratio of adjacent coefficients is

\displaystyle{}_{d}^{\sigma}(j)=\frac{1}{(2j+1)(2j+2)}\left(\frac{\sigma}{d}-2j+1\right)\left(\frac{\sigma}{d}-2j\right),

(3-9)

shown in FIG.
36
for various
\sigma
and
d
. The minimum typically occurs at
j=1
, a special point for
\sigma
and
d
. As
j\to\infty
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}{k^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sym},2j+2}}/{k^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sym},2j}}=1,

(3-10)

indicating a convergence radius
R_{\delta}=1
[
475
]
, irrespective of the integer
\sigma
.
For
\sigma=0
, the expression (
3-9
) gives a nonzero value, i.e.,
\lim_{\sigma\to 0}{}_{d}^{\sigma}(j)=(2j-1)(2j)/(2j+1)(2j+2)
, although in this case both the
k_{\mathrm{sym},2j+2}^{\sigma}
and
k_{\mathrm{sym},2j}^{\sigma}
are approaching zero (but their ratio is not necessarily zero).

The relativistic kinetic energy per nucleon in ANM in 3D can be straightforwardly and similarly written as:

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho,\delta)=\left[2\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\right]^{-1}\cdot\sum_{J=\mathrm{n,p}}\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\left[\sqrt{\mathbf{k}^{2}+M_{\mathrm{N}}^{2}}-M_{\mathrm{N}}\right],

(3-11)

where the nucleon dispersion relation changes from the non-relativistic
\mathbf{k}^{2}/2M_{\mathrm{N}}
to the relativistic form
\sqrt{\mathbf{k}^{2}+M_{\mathrm{N}}^{2}}-M_{\mathrm{N}}
.
For small deviations
\delta x
, useful mathematical approximations are

\displaystyle\sum_{\Delta=\pm 1}\mathrm{arcsinh}\,(x+\Delta\delta x)\approx 2\mathrm{arcsinh}\,x-\frac{x}{(x^{2}+1)^{3/2}}\delta x^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{1}{4}\frac{x(2x^{2}-3)}{(x^{2}+1)^{7/2}}\delta x^{4}-\frac{1}{24}\frac{x(8x^{4}-40x^{2}+15)}{(x^{2}+1)^{11/2}}\delta x^{6},

(3-12)

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+\delta x}\approx 1+\frac{1}{2}\delta x-\frac{1}{8}\delta x^{2}+\frac{1}{16}\delta x^{3}-\frac{5}{128}\delta x^{4}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{7}{256}\delta x^{5}-\frac{21}{1024}\delta x^{6}.

(3-13)

Using these expansions, the kinetic energy and its symmetry components can be expressed analytically as

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{3M_{\mathrm{N}}}{8\nu^{2}}\left[\left(1+2\nu^{2}\right)\sqrt{1+\nu^{2}}-\frac{\mathrm{arcsinh}\,\nu}{\nu}\right]-M_{\mathrm{N}},

(3-14)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{6}\frac{\nu^{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu^{2}}},

(3-15)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{648}\frac{\nu^{2}(10\nu^{4}+11\nu^{2}+4)}{(1+\nu^{2})^{5/2}},

(3-16)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{34992}\frac{\nu^{2}(176\nu^{8}+428\nu^{6}+477\nu^{4}+260\nu^{2}+56)}{(1+\nu^{2})^{9/2}},

(3-17)

with the dimensionless parameter
\nu=k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{\mathrm{N}}
.

Non-relativistic limit with
\nu=k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{\mathrm{N}}\ll 1
.
Expanding for small
\nu
, one recovers

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{10M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(1-\frac{5}{28}\nu^{2}+\frac{5}{72}\nu^{4}-\frac{25}{704}\nu^{6}\right),

(3-18)

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\nu^{2}+\frac{3}{8}\nu^{4}-\frac{5}{16}\nu^{6}\right),

(3-19)

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{162M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{4}\nu^{2}-\frac{25}{32}\nu^{6}\right),

(3-20)

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{7k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{4374M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{7}\nu^{2}-\frac{5}{112}\nu^{6}\right),

(3-21)

where the prefactors correspond to the familiar non-relativistic FFG expressions.
Notably, the first-order relativistic correction to the kinetic symmetry energy,
-k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}/(12M^{3})
, was previously given in Ref.
[
194
]
and is about
-0.48\,\mathrm{MeV}
at
\rho_{0}
.

Ultra-relativistic limit with
\nu=k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{\mathrm{N}}\gg 1
.

In the opposite extreme, defining
\mu=\nu^{-1}
, one obtains

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{0}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{3k_{\mathrm{F}}}{4}\left(1-\frac{4\mu}{3}+\mu^{2}+\frac{1-4\ln(2/\mu)}{8}\mu^{4}\right),

(3-22)

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\mu^{2}+\frac{3}{8}\mu^{4}\right),

(3-23)

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{5k_{\mathrm{F}}}{324}\left(1-\frac{7}{5}\mu^{2}+\frac{81}{40}\mu^{4}\right),

(3-24)

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{11k_{\mathrm{F}}}{2187}\left(1-\frac{91}{44}\mu^{2}+\frac{729}{176}\mu^{4}\right).

(3-25)

The density dependence is distinct in the two limits. For example, in the non-relativistic regime
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\sim\rho^{2/3}\ll k_{\mathrm{F}}
, while in the ultra-relativistic limit
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\sim\rho^{1/3}\gg M_{\mathrm{N}}
.

The ratio of fourth- to second-order kinetic symmetry energies is then given by:

\Psi=\frac{E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)}{E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)}=\frac{1}{108}\frac{4+11\nu^{2}+10\nu^{4}}{1+2\nu^{2}+\nu^{4}}.

(3-26)

It is noteworthy that in the ultra-relativistic regime, the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
becomes the dominant scale since the nucleon rest mass
M_{\mathrm{N}}
is much smaller than
k_{\mathrm{F}}
and can be safely neglected. In this limit, the kinetic symmetry energies simplify to
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx k_{\mathrm{F}}/6
and
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx 5k_{\mathrm{F}}/324
, indicating that relativistic effects enhance the quartic term relative to the conventional quadratic contribution. Specifically, this reduces to the familiar FFG result
{}_{\mathrm{NR}}=1/27
in the non-relativistic limit, and to
{}_{\mathrm{UR}}=5/54
in the ultra-relativistic limit; hence
1/27\leq\Psi\leq 5/54
.
The enhancement of
\Psi
reflects relativistic effects on the quartic contribution relative to the quadratic kinetic symmetry energy. For conventional nuclear densities (
\rho\lesssim 10\rho_{0}
), the system remains effectively non-relativistic, providing a partial explanation for the smallness of
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}
in the conventional FFG model.
Furthermore, if one roughly considers
\nu=k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 1
as the transition point between non-relativistic and relativistic behavior, this corresponds to a density of
\rho\approx 45\rho_{0}
.

FIG. 37
:
(Color Online). Dependence of the kinetic symmetry energy and the isospin-quartic term on the fractions of high-momentum nucleons in SNM (
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
) and PNM (
x_{\mathrm{PNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
).
Figure taken from Ref.
[
279
]
.

FIG. 38
:
(Color Online). Upper: symmetry energy with/without tensor contribution obtained in the RHF model. Lower: different contributions of the the tensor part. Figures taken from Ref.
[
248
]
.

FIG. 39
:
(Color Online). Kinetic part, the isospin-single and the isospin-triplet parts of the symmetry energy obtained in the RHF theory. Figure taken from Ref.
[
521
]
.

FIG. 40
:
(Color Online). Symmetry energy obtained in the RHF theory via the HVH theorem. Figure taken from Ref.
[
330
]
.

3.2 Reduction of Kinetic Symmetry Energy and the Parabolic Approximation Issue

The SRC-induced HMT modifies the single-nucleon momentum distribution
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
, which has direct consequences on the kinetic EOS of ANM
[
91
]
. In this subsection, we discuss these effects. The kinetic EOS can be expanded in powers of
\delta
:

E^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho,\delta)\approx E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)+E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\delta^{2}+E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\delta^{4}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{6}).

(3-27)

Using the
n^{J}_{\mathbf{k}}(\rho,\delta)
in Eq. (
2-8
) with
\beta_{J}=0
, the coefficients from Eq. (
2-12
) are

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{5}E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho)\left[1+{C}_{0}\left(5\phi_{0}+\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}-8\right)\right],

(3-28)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho)\Bigg[1+{C}_{0}\left(1+3{C}_{1}\right)\left(5\phi_{0}+\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}-8\right)

\displaystyle+3{C}_{0}\phi_{1}\left(1+\frac{3}{5}{C}_{1}\right)\left(5\phi_{0}-\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}\right)+\frac{27{C}_{0}\phi_{1}^{2}}{5\phi_{0}}\Bigg],

(3-29)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{81}E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho)\Bigg[1+{C}_{0}(1-3{C}_{1})\left(5\phi_{0}+\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}-8\right)

\displaystyle+3{C}_{0}\phi_{1}(9{C}_{1}-1)\left(5\phi_{0}-\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{81{C}_{0}\phi_{1}^{2}(9\phi_{1}^{2}-9{C}_{1}\phi_{1}-15\phi_{1}+15{C}_{1}+5)}{5\phi_{0}}\Bigg],

(3-30)

where
E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho)=k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/2M_{\mathrm{N}}
is the nucleon Fermi energy in SNM.
Because
\phi_{0}>1
in the presence of HMT, one has

\Phi_{0}\equiv 5\phi_{0}+\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}-8>0.

(3-31)

Consequently, the kinetic EOS of SNM is enhanced relative to the FFG prediction
[
489
,
219
]
. This enhancement arises naturally since neutron-proton SRC dominates in SNM, and the HMT significantly increases the kinetic energy through the
k^{4}
factor in Eq. (
2-12
). In the absence of HMT, i.e., the FFG case with
\phi_{0}=1
and
\phi_{1}=0
,
\Phi_{0}=0
, and the expressions reduce to the well-known results

E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{0}(\rho)=3E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho)/5
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho)/3
, as well as

E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho)/81
.
For interacting nucleons in ANM, using the momentum distribution parameters, it was found
[
92
]
that

E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 40.45\pm 8.15\,\mathrm{MeV}
,

E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx-13.90\pm 11.54\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and

E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 7.19\pm 2.52\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
The relativistic corrections to these quantities would be briefly discussed in the end of this subsection.
Compared to FFG values, the isospin-dependent HMT significantly increases
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
while reducing
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
to a negative value, consistent qualitatively with several recent studies using phenomenological and microscopic many-body approaches
[
380
,
489
,
445
,
308
,
103
,
102
]
.
The validity of the empirical isospin parabolic law for the kinetic EOS in the presence of isospin-dependent HMT was previously unknown, but it was found to be seriously broken
[
92
]
. Since the corrections in the square brackets for
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
and
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
are not small, the parabolic approximation of the EOS of ANM at the kinetic level is expected to fail. Quantitatively, the ratio

|E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})/E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})|\approx 52\%\pm 26\%

is much larger than the FFG value of
3.7\%
[
92
]
. The large quartic term is mainly due to the strong isospin dependence of the HMT cutoff parameter
\phi_{1}
. Setting
\phi_{1}=0
yields

E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})\approx 14.68\pm 2.80
MeV and

E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym},4}(\rho_{0})\approx 1.12\pm 0.27
MeV, close to the FFG values.

FIG.
37
illustrates that a large quartic term arises when the difference between
x_{\mathrm{SNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
and
x_{\mathrm{PNM}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
is large. Previous studies that assumed the parabolic approximation evaluated
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
as the difference between the kinetic energies of PNM and SNM, effectively summing the kinetic symmetry and quartic energies:

E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin,app}}(\rho_{0})\approx E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})+E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx-6.71\pm 9.11
MeV.
This explains the quantitative consistency with
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
reported in Ref.
[
224
]
under the parabolic approximation.
Using the HVH theorem
[
239
]
at the mean-field level, an appreciable isospin-quartic term in the EOS may arise from high-order derivatives of the momentum-dependent symmetry potential or differences between nucleon isoscalar and isovector effective masses. SRC modifies the nucleon momentum distribution, producing quasi-particles whose distribution differs from the FFG step function. Since SRC is mainly tensor-force induced, the reduction in kinetic symmetry energy and enhancement of the quartic term can also be attributed to the tensor force, which does not contribute at the lowest order of the mean-field. These SRC-induced modifications can be considered additions to the mean-field EOS, though further quantitative studies are needed.
The enhancement of the kinetic quartic term is also observed in relativistic Hartree-Fock (RHF) theories. In Ref.
[
305
]
, the authors further showed that the Fock terms, particularly those from the isoscalar meson coupling channels, significantly affect the kinetic contribution to
S_{4}(\rho)\equiv E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)
, systematically leading to an increased kinetic term in RHF especially for
\rho\gtrsim 0.2\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, see the upper panel of FIG.
41
.
The authors of Ref.
[
248
]
found that including the tensor correlations reduces the symmetry energy as a function of density, see the upper panel of FIG.
38
where the three interaction sets namely PKA1, PKO1, and PKO3 are considered.
Although the tensor correlations contributes very little at/around the normal density, its effect could be sizable at large densities to be an amount of 10-20
\%
of the total symmetry energy compared with other channels. Moreover, based on the interaction set PKA1, it was found that the tensor component in the
\sigma
-S coupling channel dominates the tensor contributions to
the symmetry energy, followed by the
\omega
-V couplings, while
those in the
\pi
- and
\rho
- and exchanges are relatively small. This result can be well understood from the tensor coupling constants shown in the lower panel of the lower part of FIG.
38
namely that the tensor coupling constants in
\sigma
-S and
\omega
-V channels tend to certain values at high density, whereas due to the
exponential density-dependent behavior of
g_{\rho},f_{\rho}
and
f_{\pi}
, those
from the isovector
\rho
-V,
\rho
-T and
\pi
-PV channels vanish at the supra-normal density region where the tensor effects become notable.
Based on the RHF framework, the kinetic-potential decomposition of the symmetry energy was studied in Ref.
[
521
]
, and the kinetic part of the symmetry energy was found to be reduced, see FIG.
39
for the decomposition of the symmetry energy.
For example the kinetic symmetry energy in the PKO1 (PKA1) with the tensor correlations included is found to be about 3.7 MeV (0.5 MeV), which is much smaller than the one no tensor correlations is included, i.e., the 8.0 MeV in TW99 (8.1 MeV in PKDD).
Parallel to the work given in Ref.
[
248
]
, the Fock contribution to the symmetry energy was recently investigated in Ref.
[
330
]
in the RHF framework via the HVH theorem.
See FIG.
40
for the density dependence of the symmetry energy where the reduction on the symmetry energy from the RHF set is obvious, here the
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
in different models (i.e., RH, RHF, ERHF (low) and ERHF (high)) is fixed at 32.5 MeV. The two sets namely ERHF(low) and ERHF(high) are designed to well describe the single-nucleon potential obtained from optical models at low energies and high energies, respectively.
In the RHF theory the potential part is decomposed into two parts
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{pot}}(\rho)=E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{pot,dir}}(\rho)+E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{pot,ex}}(\rho)
where the direct term is simply given by
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{pot,dir}}(\rho)=g_{\rho}^{2}\rho/2Q_{\rho}
with
Q_{\rho}=m_{\rho}^{2}+g_{\rho}^{2}g_{\omega}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}^{2}
, and once the negative Fock contribution to the symmetry energy is included the direct term decreases to a very small value, and the final density dependence of the symmetry energy is competition result from these difference channels.

FIG. 41
:
(Color Online). Upper: the nuclear fourth-order symmetry energy, its potential part and kinetic part as functions of the baryon density. The results are calculated with RHF interaction sets PKA1 and PKO1, in comparison with the RMF ones PKDD and TW99; figure taken from Ref.
[
305
]
.
Lower: the isospin quartic term obtained in the chiral effective theory; figure taken from Ref.
[
256
]
.

FIG. 42
:
(Color Online). Symmetry energy (upper) and the EOS of PNM (lower) obtained in QCDSR. Figures taken from Ref.
[
88
]
.

Although the parabolic approximation for the EOS may break down at the kinetic level, the full EOS of ANM, including the potential part, is still expected to satisfy this approximation (especially near the saturation density), as illustrated, for example, by the RHF calculations shown in the upper panel of FIG.
41
. The parabolic behavior of the full EOS of ANM has also been confirmed by chiral effective field theory studies; for instance, Ref.
[
256
]
found that
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 1.5\,\mathrm{MeV}
(see the lower panel of FIG.
41
), consistent with other investigations
[
86
,
401
,
203
,
366
]
.
One exception comes from the QCD sum rules (QCDSR) calculations for the EOS of ANM
[
87
,
88
]
.
In QCDSR, the EOS of PNM and the symmetry energy are well described, see, e.g., FIG.
42
[
88
]
.
In order to estimate the contributions of higher-order terms in the EOS, we write out the EOS of SNM and that of PNM as
[
88
]
,

\displaystyle E_{0}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(\rho)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}\rho}{\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}}

\displaystyle\times\left[\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{N}}}{2m_{\mathrm{q}}}\left(1+\frac{16\pi^{2}f}{3}\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}}{\mathscr{M}^{4}}\right)-4\right],

(3-32)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{n}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(\rho)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}\rho}{\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}}

\displaystyle\times\left[\left(1-\xi\right)\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{N}}}{2m_{\mathrm{q}}}\left(1+\frac{16\pi^{2}f}{3}\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}}{\mathscr{M}^{4}}\right)-5\right],

(3-33)

here

\xi\approx 0.1
[
88
]
is the
coefficient characterizing the isospin splitting of the chiral condensate.
Since the EOS of PNM at very low densities (say densities smaller than
0.01\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
) could be
determined very accurate by simulations or microscopic calculations, there exists a relation between several fundamental quantities and the model parameters in QCDSR,
such as the four-quark effective parameter
f
[
121
]
, the nucleon-sigma term
\sigma_{\mathrm{N}}
[
199
]
, the chiral condensate
\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}
in vacuum
[
121
]
, the light quark mass
m_{\mathrm{q}}
, and the Borel mass
\mathscr{M}
[
121
,
88
]
.

The symmetry energy obtained in the parabolic approximation is roughly given by

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{para}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym,para}}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(\rho)-\frac{1}{2}\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}\rho}{\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}}

\displaystyle\times\left[\frac{\xi\sigma_{\mathrm{N}}}{2m_{\mathrm{q}}}\left(1+\frac{16\pi^{2}f}{3}\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}}{\mathscr{M}^{4}}\right)+1\right].

(3-34)

The kinetic symmetry energy
in the parabolic approximation is

E_{\mathrm{sym,para}}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(\rho)=(2^{2/3}-1)E_{0}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(\rho)\approx 0.59E_{0}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(\rho)
.
If one takes
f=0
in Eq. (
3-33
), then the
E_{\mathrm{n}}(\rho)
could be written as
[
87
]

\displaystyle\boxed{E_{\mathrm{n}}(\rho)\approx E_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(\rho)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}\rho}{\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}}\left[\left(1-\xi\right)\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{N}}}{2m_{\mathrm{q}}}-5\right],}

(3-35)

which depends only on the fundamental quantities such as
m_{\mathrm{q}}
,
\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}
,
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{N}}
, and not on the effective parameters
f
and
\mathscr{M}^{2}
.
Despite its simplicity, Eq. (
3-35
) has already the power of quantitative predictions at very low densities.
The high-order (HO) term of the EOS is simply obtained as
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)=E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{para}}(\rho)-E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
.
The numerical results on the
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
and
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)
using the QCDSR equations are shown in the upper part of FIG.
43
.
For example, the high order effects in the EOS at
\rho_{0}=0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
are found to be about 8.8 MeV, 7.4 MeV, and 6.4 MeV for
f=0
, 0.25 and 0.5, respectively; clearly showing that the high order term
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)
at the saturation density
\rho_{0}
are generally not small (e.g.,
\lesssim 2\,\mathrm{MeV}
)
[
86
,
203
,
366
,
401
]
.

FIG. 43
:
(Color Online). Upper: the
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
and
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)
as functions of density with different
f
using the QCDSR. Lower: high order EOS obtained in QCDSR for three different calculation schemes. Figures taken from Ref.
[
88
]
.

It is useful to generally analyze the origin of the possible breakdown of the parabolic approximation of the EOS of ANM in QCDSR framework.
In the simplest QCDSR situation, the density dependence of the chiral condensates has the structure of

\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\rho,\delta}^{\mathrm{u,d}}=\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}+a(1\mp\xi\delta)\rho
,
where
a=\sigma_{\mathrm{N}}/2m_{\mathrm{q}}
.
The nucleon Dirac effective mass and the vector self-energy are given as
M_{J}^{\ast}\sim\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\rho,\delta}^{\mathrm{u,d}}

and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{V}}^{J}\sim\langle q^{{\dagger}}q\rangle_{\rho,\delta}^{\mathrm{u,d}}
, respectively.
Then the
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)
could be estimated as
[
88
]
:

E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)\sim-2^{-1}a\xi\rho\left(1-{M_{0}^{\ast}}/{{E}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}\right),

(3-36)

where the overall factor is unimportant for the qualitative demonstration.
At low densities,
E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}=(M_{0}^{\ast,2}+k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2})^{1/2}\approx k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/2M_{0}^{\ast}+M_{0}^{\ast}\approx M_{0}^{\ast}
, indicating that the expression in the parentheses in Eq. (
3-36
) is small.
As density increases, the
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)
by Eq. (
3-36
) increases eventually, leading to the increasing of the
high order effects eventually. For example, keeping only the leading order term in
(k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{0}^{\ast})^{2}
, one obtains the high order effect
as
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)\sim-a\xi k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\rho/4M_{0}^{\ast,2}
,
which is negative.
When considering high order terms in density in the
chiral condensates, e.g.,
\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\rho,\delta}^{\mathrm{u,d}}=\langle\overline{q}q\rangle_{\mathrm{vac}}+a(1\mp\xi\delta)\rho+b(1\mp\zeta\delta)\rho^{\vartheta}
with
\vartheta>1
,
and assuming other quark/gluon condensates remain
unchanged, one may obtain
[
88
]
:

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{HO}}^{\vartheta\mathrm{\,term}}(\rho)\approx\frac{1}{2}b\zeta\rho^{\vartheta}\frac{\vartheta-1}{\vartheta+1}-\frac{1}{4}b\zeta\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}\right)^{2}\rho^{\vartheta}.

(3-37)

It is obvious from this expression the
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)
will not be small even at low densities, and
the high order effects become more and more important as density increases.
By adopting the three fitting schemes, the EOS of ANM is systematically calculated
[
88
]
and the corresponding results are shown in the lower part of FIG.
43
.
We find, for example, the
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)
in QCDSR-2
is about 7.1 MeV at
\rho_{0}
, and an even larger value about 10.9 MeV in the QCDSR-3 scheme could be inferred.
Similarly in the QCDSR-1 set the fourth-order symmetry at
\rho_{0}
is about 1.1 MeV.
As a concise summary, in QCDSR calculations, the high-order term
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho)
is found to be sizable at densities
\lesssim\rho_{0}
, implying that the traditional parabolic approximation for the EOS of ANM may be violated. At
\rho_{0}
, the full QCDSR calculation yields
E_{\mathrm{HO}}(\rho_{0})\approx 1.1\sim 11.9\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
88
]
, demonstrating a much larger uncertainty than in phenomenological models. Nevertheless, caution is required when treating these higher-order contributions, as emphasized in Ref.
[
475
]
, where it was noted that the expansion in
\delta
may converge slowly, become nonanalytic, or even display reversed term magnitudes in complex scenarios.

When the QCDSR case is set aside, the quartic term’s small size may seem somewhat surprising in light of finite nuclei calculations.
In particular, the fourth-order term
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)
extracted from nuclear mass formulas has been found to be sizable. Specifically, values of
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)\approx 3.28\pm 0.50
MeV and
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)\approx 8.33\pm 1.21
MeV were reported in Refs.
[
246
]
and
[
430
]
, respectively. Furthermore, in Ref.
[
247
]
, the fourth-order symmetry energy of finite nuclei was analyzed by fitting nuclear mass data using the nuclear mass formula with two different forms of the Wigner energy, resulting in a constraint of
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)\approx 3.91\pm 0.10
MeV. These relatively large values of
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)
have led to suggestions that the full
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}(\rho_{0})
in infinite nuclear matter might also be large.

A careful analysis revisiting the relation between the fourth-order symmetry energy of nuclear matter at saturation density and its counterpart
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)
in finite nuclei shows the importance of the high-order isospin-dependent surface tension contribution. In Ref.
[
99
]
, the full expression for
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)
, explicitly including high-order surface tension effects, was derived. It was found that
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})
cannot be reliably extracted from measured
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)
without proper knowledge of the high-order surface tension. These results indicate that large
a_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)
values of several MeV obtained from nuclear mass analyses can be fully compatible with the empirical constraint
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 2\,\mathrm{MeV}
from chiral effective field theory
[
256
]
and mean-field models
[
86
,
401
,
203
,
366
]
, and do not necessarily imply a large
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})
as previously inferred when high-order surface tension effects were neglected.
Specifically, one has

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{sym}}(A)=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{1+(\alpha/\beta)A^{-1/3}},

(3-38)

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(A)=

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})}{\beta A^{1/3}}\right)^{-4}

\displaystyle\times\left(E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})-\frac{L^{2}}{2K_{0}}+\frac{4\theta\kappa E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{4}(\rho_{0})}{\beta A^{1/3}}\right),

(3-39)

as well as the surface contribution:

\displaystyle\boxed{a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(A)=\frac{4\theta\kappa E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{4}(\rho_{0})}{\beta A^{1/3}}\cdot\left(1+\frac{E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})}{\beta A^{1/3}}\right)^{-4},}

(3-40)

here
\alpha\equiv E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
is the nuclear matter symmetry energy at
\rho_{0}
,
\beta
denotes the surface symmetry energy, and
\theta
and
\kappa
appear in the surface tension expressed as
\sigma\approx\sigma_{0}-\gamma\mu_{\mathrm{a}}^{2}-{}^{\prime}\mu_{\mathrm{a}}^{4}
or equivalently
\sigma/\sigma_{0}\approx 1+y+\kappa y^{2}
with
y=-\theta\mu_{\mathrm{a}}^{2}
, where
\theta=\gamma/\sigma_{0}
and
\kappa=-{}^{\prime}\sigma_{0}/{}^{2}
; evidently,
\kappa
represents a higher-order effect.

FIG. 44
:
(Color Online). The symmetry energy
a_{\mathrm{sym}}(A)
and the surface fourth-order symmetry energy
a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(A)
of finite nuclei as functions of nuclear mass number
A
. Here
\alpha/\beta=2
and
\kappa=0.5
are adopted for illustration,
\alpha\equiv E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
. Figure taken from Ref.
[
99
]
.

In FIG.
44
, we show the
A
-dependence of the symmetry energy
a_{\mathrm{sym}}(A)
(blue dash-dot line) and the surface fourth-order symmetry energy
a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(A)
(magenta line) of finite nuclei, adopting
\kappa=0.5
. For illustration, we further use
\alpha\approx 30\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
\beta\approx 15\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
99
]
. Within the considered range of
A
, the surface contribution to the fourth-order symmetry energy exhibits only a weak dependence on the mass number.
In the small-
A
limit, one finds
a_{\mathrm{sym}}(A)\to\beta A^{1/3},a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)\to 4\kappa\theta\beta^{3}A
, and
a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)/a_{\mathrm{sym}}(A)\to 4\kappa\theta\beta^{2}A^{2/3}
, all independent of the bulk coefficient
\alpha
and vanishing as
A\to 0
. For heavier nuclei, the parameter
\varphi=\alpha/\beta A^{1/3}
is generally smaller than unity, leading to the approximation
a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(A)\approx 4\alpha^{3}\theta\kappa\varphi={\alpha^{3}A^{2/3}\kappa\varphi}/{S\beta\sigma_{0}}
. Notably, this approximation shows that
a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(A)
is proportional to
\varphi
, which approaches zero in the infinite matter limit, i.e.,
\lim_{A\to\infty}a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(A)=0\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
The vanishing of
a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)
for very large
A
is expected, as it represents only the surface part of the fourth-order symmetry energy. Physically, the surface disappears in the limit
A\to\infty
, so the associated contribution naturally goes to zero, as illustrated in the inset of FIG.
44
. However, this does not imply that the fourth-order symmetry energy of infinite matter vanishes
[
99
]
. Hence, the finite-nucleus fourth-order symmetry energy discussed here is still characterized by
\alpha
and
\beta
, rather than
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})
(see Ref.
[
456
]
).
The mass number at which
a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)
reaches its maximum can be determined from
\partial a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A)/\partial A=0
, giving
A_{\max}=27\alpha^{3}/\beta^{3}\approx 216
. At this point,
a^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{sym},4}(A_{\max})=27\alpha^{3}\theta\kappa/64\approx 7.5\,\mathrm{MeV}
, as indicated by the black dashed line in the inset of FIG.
44
. Interestingly, the empirical ratio
\alpha/\beta
(around 2-3) coincidentally predicts that the fourth-order surface symmetry energy peaks near
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
. This highlights the fundamental difference between finite nuclei (
A\sim 208
) and infinite matter with
A\to\infty
[
456
]
.

Similarly, if one includes higher-order isospin-dependent terms in the surface tension coefficient as
\sigma/\sigma_{0}\approx 1+y+\kappa y^{2}+sy^{3}
, with
s
representing an effective parameter beyond
\kappa
, the sixth-order symmetry energy
a_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(A)
of finite nuclei can be directly obtained as a function of
A
, incorporating both volume and surface contributions
[
99
]
:

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(A)=

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})}{\beta A^{1/3}}\right)^{-6}\cdot\Bigg[E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(\rho_{0})-L_{\mathrm{sym,4}}\left(\frac{L}{K_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{K_{\mathrm{sym}}}{2}\left(\frac{L}{K_{0}}\right)^{2}-\frac{J_{0}}{6}\left(\frac{L}{K_{0}}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{16\theta^{2}E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{6}(\rho_{0})}{\beta A^{1/3}}\left(\frac{4\kappa^{2}}{1+\beta A^{1/3}/E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})}-s\right)\Bigg].

(3-41)

The surface contribution in Eq. (
3.2
) (the last line) originates from two sources: a higher-order term derived from the lower-order coefficient
\kappa
(proportional to
\kappa^{2}
) and a higher-order term from the coefficient
s
. Naturally, this surface term vanishes in the limit
A\to\infty
.

Parallel to the discussion in the previous paragraphs, it remains very challenging to extract the sixth-order symmetry energy of infinite matter,
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(\rho_{0})
, from this expression, even if the coefficient
a_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(A)
appearing in the mass formula is constrained, because the coefficient
s
can be adjusted to make
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(\rho_{0})
small or large without significantly affecting finite nuclei properties such as the nuclear surface tension. For instance, neglecting the surface term, a “small”
a_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(A)\approx 1\,\mathrm{MeV}
could correspond to an
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(\rho_{0})
around 7-8 MeV, potentially conflicting with empirical constraints on the EOS of ANM.
In this regard, the surface contribution to
a_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(A)
is essential. Furthermore, by imposing conditions such as
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 2\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
a_{\mathrm{sym,6}}(A)\lesssim 2\,\mathrm{MeV}
, Eq. (
3.2
) establishes a connection among several characteristics with sizable magnitudes, which could be used to explore correlations among them
[
99
]
.

FIG. 45
:
(Color Online). Kinetic symmetry energy and the isospin-quartic term including the SRC-induced HMT in comparison with predictions by other theories. Figure taken from Ref.
[
279
]
.

The SRC-induced reduction of the kinetic symmetry energy relative to the FFG value is a common feature in many-body theories that include SRC effects. As shown in FIG.
45
, comparisons with microscopic many-body and phenomenological models, including SCGF (N
3
LO, AV18, Nij1, CD Bonn)
[
103
]
, BHF with AV18 plus Urbana IX three-body-force (TBF)
[
445
]
, the Fermi hypernetted chain (FHNC) model
[
308
]
, and a phenomenological parametrization
[
224
]
, all confirm qualitatively that SRC reduces the kinetic symmetry energy, though quantitative differences exist; the relativistic prediction on the reduction of the kinetic symmetry energy will be discussed in Subsection
3.3
.
The SRC-reduced kinetic symmetry energy affects not only the understanding of the origin of symmetry energy but also isovector observables, such as free neutron/proton and
\pi^{-}/\pi^{+}
ratios in heavy-ion collisions
[
224
,
284
]
, and properties of NSs, where isospin asymmetry is large, see Section
§5
. To date, possible effects of a large quartic term on heavy-ion collisions remain unexplored due to the small asymmetry in such reactions.
However, in neutron-rich systems such as rare isotope reactions or peripheral collisions of heavy nuclei with thick neutron skins, the quartic term may play a significant role.
Typically, the EOS of neutron-rich nucleonic matter is parameterized as the sum of a FFG kinetic term and a potential energy up to the isospin-quadratic term. Findings in Ref.
[
91
]
indicate the importance of including the isospin-quartic term in both kinetic and potential parts. Accurate extraction of the potential isospin-quartic term
E^{\mathrm{pot}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\delta^{4}
requires using the kinetic EOS of quasi-particles with reduced symmetry energy and enhanced quartic term due to the isospin-dependent HMT.
For isovector observables in heavy-ion collisions, the nucleon isovector potential is critical. Beyond the Lane potential
\pm 2\rho E^{\mathrm{pot}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\delta
, the
E^{\mathrm{pot}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\delta^{4}
term adds
\pm 4\rho E^{\mathrm{pot}}_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\delta^{3}
.
In neutron-rich systems, such as nuclear reactions induced by rare
isotopes and peripheral collisions between two heavy nuclei having thick neutron-skins, the latter may play a significant role in understanding the isovector observables or extracting the sizes of neutron-skins of the neutron-rich nuclei involved.
On the NS side, the kinetic part as well as the potential part of the symmetry energy were found to separately affect the formation density of the
\Delta(1232)
state, e.g.,

\displaystyle\rho_{\Delta^{-}}^{\mathrm{crit}}:\;

\displaystyle k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mathrm{n},2}/2M_{\mathrm{D}}^{\ast}\approx\Phi_{\Delta}+g_{\sigma\mathrm{N}}(1-x_{\sigma})\overline{\sigma}-g_{\omega\mathrm{N}}(1-x_{\omega})\overline{\omega}_{0}

\displaystyle-6(1-x_{\rho})E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{pot}}(\rho)\delta-4E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\delta

(3-42)

for the formation density of
\Delta^{-}
[
89
]
,
where
\Phi_{\Delta}\approx 293\,\mathrm{MeV}
is the mass difference between the
\Delta(1232)
and the nucleon,
M_{\mathrm{D}}^{\ast}
is the Dirac nucleon effective mass,
g_{\sigma\mathrm{N}},g_{\omega\mathrm{N}},g_{\rho\mathrm{N}}
are the meson-nucleon coupling constants and
x_{\sigma},x_{\omega}
and
x_{\rho}
characterize the couplings between the
\Delta
’s and the mesons.
The relation (
3.2
) is obtained using the nonlinear RMF model incorporating the couplings between the
\Delta
’s and the mesons.
In this sense one could study the separable effects of the kinetic part and the potential part of the symmetry energy on the
\rho_{\Delta^{-}}^{\mathrm{crit}}
.
It could be possible to investigate the effects of the large kinetic quartic term if one could find similar relations in NSs.

We offer an explanation for the reduction of the kinetic symmetry energy, from a mathematical viewpoint. To illustrate this, it is convenient to consider the difference of the symmetry energy at a reference density
\rho_{\mathrm{rf}}\neq\rho_{0}
, defined as

\mathcal{O}\equiv E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{\mathrm{rf}})[\mathrm{FFG}]-E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{\mathrm{rf}})[\mathrm{HMT}]
,
under the constraints that
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
and
L
are kept fixed. If
\mathcal{O}>0
, the SRC-induced HMT reduces the symmetry energy, whereas if
\mathcal{O}<0
, the symmetry energy is enhanced.
For simplicity and to highlight the essential features, one may parametrize
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
as

E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,\Phi)=\Phi\left(\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{\nu_{0}}+A\left(\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{\nu_{1}}+B\left(\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{\nu_{2}},

(3-43)

where
\nu_{0}=2/3
corresponds to the FFG prediction
[
288
]
for the density dependence of the kinetic symmetry energy, representing the smallest power in
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
. The exponents
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are larger than
2/3
, and without loss of generality, we take
\nu_{2}>\nu_{1}
. Moreover,
\Phi=(1/6M_{\mathrm{N}})(3\pi^{2}/2)^{2/3}\Upsilon_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{NR}}
, with
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{NR}}
defined in Eq. (
3-29
). The coefficients
A
and
B
are determined by the constraints that
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
and
L
remain fixed.
After straightforward calculations, one finds

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\approx

\displaystyle\delta\Phi\left(\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{rf}}}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{\nu_{2}}\cdot\frac{\nu_{1}-\nu_{0}}{\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}}\cdot\Pi\left(\rho_{\mathrm{rf}},\nu_{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\mbox{higher-order terms in }\left(\frac{\nu_{0}}{\nu_{2}}\right)\mbox{ or
}\left(\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\right),

(3-44)

where
\delta\Phi=\Phi_{\mathrm{FFG}}-\Phi_{\mathrm{HMT}}
and

\Pi\left(\rho,\nu_{2}\right)=1-\left(\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{-\nu_{2}}\left[1+\nu_{2}\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}\right)\right].

(3-45)

The function
\Pi
increases monotonically with
\nu_{2}
at fixed density, and for fixed
\nu_{2}
,
\Pi
increases (decreases) for
\rho/\rho_{0}>1
(
0<\rho/\rho_{0}<1
). Therefore,
\Pi(\rho,\nu_{2})>\Pi(\rho,0)=0
and
\Pi(\rho,\nu_{2})>\Pi(\rho_{0},\nu_{2})=0
, i.e.,
\Pi
is positive-definite. The pre-factor of
\Pi(\rho_{\mathrm{rf}},\nu_{2})
in Eq. (
3.2
) is also positive because
\delta\Phi>0
, implying that
\mathcal{O}>0
. Consequently, the symmetry energy is softened (hardened) at supra- (sub-) saturation densities.
Furthermore, as the difference between the high-momentum nucleon fractions in SNM and PNM increases (for example, comparing the HMT-exp set with the HMT-SCGF set), the factor
\Phi_{\mathrm{HMT}}
can become much smaller or even negative, thereby enhancing
\delta\Phi
. This leads to a more pronounced softening (hardening) of the symmetry energy at supra- (sub-) saturation densities.

As shown in Eq. (
3-3
), the first four relativistic corrections to the nucleon kinetic energy are “
-\mathbf{k}^{2}/4M_{\mathrm{N}}^{2}
”, “
\mathbf{k}^{4}/8M_{\mathrm{N}}^{4}
”, “
-5\mathbf{k}^{6}/64M_{\mathrm{N}}^{6}
” and “
7\mathbf{k}^{8}/128M_{\mathrm{N}}^{8}
”, respectively. In calculating the average energy per nucleon in ANM, after integrating over momentum
k
, the
k
-dependence transforms into a dependence on the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}\sim\rho^{1/3}
. Specifically, for general
\sigma
, the term
\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}/M_{\mathrm{N}}^{\sigma-1}
leads to
3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\sigma}/(\sigma+3)M_{\mathrm{N}}^{\sigma-1}
in the kinetic EOS if the step function for the momentum distribution
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
is adopted. Similarly, the corresponding term for the kinetic symmetry energy is
\sigma k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\sigma}/6M_{\mathrm{N}}^{\sigma-1}
. For example, the first-order relativistic correction contributes
-3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}/56M_{\mathrm{N}}^{3}
to the SNM kinetic EOS and
-k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}/12M_{\mathrm{N}}^{3}
to the kinetic symmetry energy
[
194
]
, see also Eqs. (
3-18
) and (
3-19
).
When the SRC-induced HMT is considered, analytical expressions for
\langle\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}\rangle
become non-trivial. The
\langle\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}\rangle
at orders
\delta^{0}
,
\delta^{2}
, and
\delta^{4}
are given (assuming
\beta_{J}=0
in Eq. (
2-8
))

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}\rangle\left(\mbox{at order }\delta^{0}\right)\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{J=\mathrm{n,p}}\int_{0}^{\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(\rho,\delta)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\right)\left(\mbox{at order }\delta^{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2[(1-3C_{0})\phi_{0}+3C_{0}]k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\sigma+1}}{(\sigma+1)\phi_{0}}+\frac{2C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\sigma+1}(\phi_{0}^{\sigma-3}-1)}{\sigma-3},

(3-46)

and similarly the quadratic and the quartic contributions for
\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}\rangle\left(\mbox{at order }\delta^{2}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{J=\mathrm{n,p}}\int_{0}^{\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(\rho,\delta)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\right)\left(\mbox{at order }\delta^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\sigma}}{\sigma+3}\Bigg[\frac{3C_{0}\phi_{1}}{\phi_{0}}(\phi_{1}-C_{1})+(\sigma+3)C_{0}C_{1}\phi_{1}e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sigma+3}{\sigma-1}C_{0}\phi_{1}^{2}(\sigma-1)(\sigma-2)e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}+(\sigma+3)C_{0}C_{1}\phi_{1}e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\left(1+\frac{\sigma}{3}\right)\Bigg[\frac{3C_{0}\phi_{1}}{\phi_{0}}+3C_{0}C_{1}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)-(\sigma+3)C_{0}\phi_{1}e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}-\frac{\sigma+3}{\sigma-1}C_{0}C_{1}\left[e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}-1\right]\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\sigma(\sigma+3)}{18}\Bigg[1-3C_{0}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)+\frac{\sigma+3}{\sigma-1}C_{0}\left[e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}-1\right]\Bigg]\Bigg],

(3-47)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}\rangle\left(\mbox{at order }\delta^{4}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{J=\mathrm{n,p}}\int_{0}^{\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(\rho,\delta)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\right)\left(\mbox{at order }\delta^{4}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\sigma}}{\sigma+3}\Bigg[\frac{3C_{0}\phi_{1}^{3}}{\phi_{0}}(\phi_{1}-C_{1})+\frac{\sigma+3}{24}C_{0}\phi_{1}^{4}(\sigma-2)(\sigma-3)(\sigma-4)e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}+\frac{\sigma+3}{6}C_{0}C_{1}\phi_{1}^{3}(\sigma-2)(\sigma-3)e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\left(1+\frac{\sigma}{3}\right)\Bigg[\frac{3C_{0}\phi_{1}^{2}}{\phi_{0}}(C_{1}-\phi_{1})+\frac{\sigma+3}{6}C_{0}\phi_{1}^{3}(\sigma-2)(\sigma-3)e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}+\frac{\sigma+3}{2}C_{0}C_{1}\phi_{1}^{2}(\sigma-2)e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\sigma(\sigma+3)}{18}\Bigg[\frac{3C_{0}\phi_{1}}{\phi_{0}}(\phi_{1}-C_{1})+\frac{\sigma+3}{2}C_{0}\phi_{1}^{2}(\sigma-2)e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}+(\sigma+3)C_{0}C_{1}\phi_{1}e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\left(\frac{\sigma}{18}-\frac{\sigma^{3}}{162}\right)\Bigg[3C_{0}\left[\frac{\phi_{1}}{\phi_{0}}+C_{1}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)\right]-(\sigma+3)C_{0}\phi_{1}e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}-\frac{\sigma+3}{\sigma-1}C_{0}C_{1}\left[e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}-1\right]\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{1}{1944}\sigma(\sigma-6)(\sigma-3)(\sigma+3)\left[1-3C_{0}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)+\frac{\sigma+3}{\sigma-1}C_{0}\left[e^{(\sigma-1)\ln\phi_{0}}-1\right]\right]\Bigg].

(3-48)

For our purposes, numerical evaluations are performed for
\sigma=2,4,6,8,10
. Sixth-order terms (
\delta^{6}
) can also be obtained numerically, though the explicit expressions are very complicated.

FIG. 46
:
(Color Online). Kinetic parts of the EOS of ANM with relativistic corrections for
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
(panel a),
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
(panel b),
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
(panel c), and
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
(panel d). Here
\rho_{0}\approx 0.16\pm 0.01\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
,
\sigma=2
is the non-relativistic term while
\sigma=4,6,8,10
correspond to the first four relativistic corrections from Eq. (
3-3
). Figures taken from Ref.
[
96
]
.

Using the parameters
\phi_{0},\phi_{1},C_{0}
and
C_{1}
introduced in Subsection
2.2
, one can evaluate the magnitudes of all contributions to the kinetic EOS as well as their relativistic corrections in the presence of SRC. At saturation density
\rho_{0}\approx 0.16\pm 0.01\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, the leading (non-relativistic) component is found to be

E_{0,\mathbf{k}^{2}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 40.47\pm 9.23\,\mathrm{MeV}
. The label “
0,\mathbf{k}^{2}
” highlights that this term arises directly from the non-relativistic kinetic energy
\mathbf{k}^{2}/2M_{\mathrm{N}}
, hence it does not represent a relativistic correction.
Proceeding to the higher orders in
\mathbf{k}^{\sigma}
, the first relativistic correction, originating from the term “
-\mathbf{k}^{4}/8M_{\mathrm{N}}^{3}
” (or equivalently “
-\mathbf{k}^{2}/4M_{\mathrm{N}}^{2}
” after dividing by
\mathbf{k}^{2}/2M_{\mathrm{N}}
in Eq. (
3-3
)), contributes

E_{0,\mathbf{k}^{4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx-1.68\pm 1.11\,\mathrm{MeV}
. The subsequent three corrections coming from “
\mathbf{k}^{6}/16M_{\mathrm{N}}^{5}
”, “
-5\mathbf{k}^{8}/128M_{\mathrm{N}}^{7}
”, and “
7\mathbf{k}^{10}/256M_{\mathrm{N}}^{9}
” provide

0.21\pm 0.25\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
-0.04\pm 0.07\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and
0.01\pm 0.02\,\mathrm{MeV}
, respectively. The smallness of the last value already indicates that including the first four relativistic corrections is sufficient to estimate the overall relativistic effects.
These results are summarized in panel (a) of FIG.
46
, which also shows for comparison the non-relativistic FFG value of about
22.10\pm 0.92\,\mathrm{MeV}
(labeled as “FFG”). The enhancement of
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}
induced by the SRC-generated HMT has long been recognized
[
91
]
.
Summing the non-relativistic term and all four relativistic corrections gives
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 38.98\pm 8.32\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
Relative to the purely non-relativistic value
40.47\,\mathrm{MeV}
, the reduction of about
1.49\,\mathrm{MeV}
corresponds to a
\sim 4\%
correction.

Turning to the quadratic kinetic symmetry energy, the leading SRC-modified non-relativistic contribution is

-13.80\pm 9.57\,\mathrm{MeV}
. The first four relativistic corrections amount to

2.11\pm 1.87\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
-0.46\pm 0.58\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
0.12\pm 0.20\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and
-0.03\pm 0.07\,\mathrm{MeV}
, respectively. Combining them yields
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx\approx-12.01\pm 8.23\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
The overall enhancement of about
1.79\,\mathrm{MeV}
corresponds to a relative correction of roughly
13\%
, shown in panel (b) of FIG.
46
. Both the uncorrected value (
-13.80\,\mathrm{MeV}
) and the corrected one (
-12.01\,\mathrm{MeV}
) are compatible with the result
-16.94\,\mathrm{MeV}
obtained from a nonlinear RMF model including the HMT, see discussions given in Subsection
3.3
.
We now consider the fourth- and sixth-order kinetic symmetry energies. Including the first four relativistic corrections in the presence of SRC gives
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 6.06\pm 1.77\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 1.07\pm 0.54\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
For
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
, the individual contributions are

7.12\pm 2.30\,\mathrm{MeV}
(non-relativistic),
-1.36\pm 0.99\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
0.37\pm 0.44\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
-0.10\pm 0.17\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and
0.03\pm 0.06\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
For
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
, they are

1.01\pm 0.51\,\mathrm{MeV}
(non-relativistic),
0.09\pm 0.08\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
-0.06\pm 0.07\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
-0.007\pm 0.05\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and
0.01\pm 0.05\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
These are displayed in panels (c) and (d) of FIG.
46
.
The net relativistic modifications to the fourth- and sixth-order symmetry energies are therefore about
-1.06\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
0.06\,\mathrm{MeV}
, respectively, corresponding to relative effects of roughly
15\%
and
6\%
. The change in the sixth-order term is thus negligible when compared with its non-relativistic magnitude.

It is also interesting to compare the ratio
\Psi
between the kinetic quartic and quadratic symmetry energies (as defined in Eq. (
3-2
)) with and without the relativistic corrections in the presence of SRC-induced high momentum nucleons. Based on the results presented above, the
\Psi
changes from
\Psi\approx-7.12/13.80\approx-51.6\%
(without) to
\Psi\approx-6.06/12.01\approx-50.4\%
(with) the relativistic corrections.
Moreover, considering the extreme case of PNM, the overall relativistic corrections can be evaluated by adding
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
,
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
and
E_{\mathrm{sym,6}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
all together. This sum is 34.8 MeV for the non-relativistic FFG model and 34.1 MeV by including the (first four) relativistic corrections, both are close to the non-relativistic FFG model prediction about
3k_{\mathrm{n}}^{2}/10M\approx 35.1\,\mathrm{MeV}
where
k_{\mathrm{n}}=(3\pi^{2}\rho)^{1/3}=2^{1/3}k_{\mathrm{F}}
