is the neutron Fermi momentum in the PNM. Thus, although the relativistic corrections to each kinetic energy term may be large or small, the overall effects are rather small (the relative effect is about 2%), since some changes are positive while others are negative.
Particularly, for the four kinetic energies, only the (quadratic) symmetry energy is reduced considering the SRC-induced HMT, while the other three higher order symmetry energies are all enhanced, compared with their FFG predictions (i.e., without HMT), see FIG.
46
. These results indicate clearly that the SRC-induced HMT is more fundamental and has much larger impacts on the ANM EOS than the relativistic corrections. Although the SRC effects are introduced through the HMT in the single nucleon momentum distribution function
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
and affect apparently only the kinetic parts of ANM EOS in the models considered here, the SRC is fundamentally due to the tensor force in the neutron-proton isosinglet interaction channel
[
219
]
.
Thus, in this sense, the finding is consistent with that in Subsection
3.3
, namely that nucleon-nucleon interactions can modify the value of
\Psi
significantly. It should also be noted that, once interaction effects beyond the FFG picture are incorporated, the ratio
\Psi
is in principle allowed to become much larger than the values obtained here.

Finally, we discuss the effects of the
\beta_{J}
on the kinetic EOS. Contributions from a finite
\beta_{J}
to the first three terms of the kinetic EOS are

\displaystyle\delta E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{5}E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{4\beta_{0}}{35},

(3-49)

\displaystyle\delta E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{4\beta_{0}(1+3\beta_{1})}{35},

(3-50)

\displaystyle\delta E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{81}E_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{4\beta_{0}(1-3\beta_{1})}{35}.

(3-51)

With
\beta_{0}\approx-0.35
(the maximum magnitude of
\beta_{0}
as discussed in Subsection
2.3
), one can find that the maximal effect of
\beta_{J}
produces negligible corrections on
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
and less than 2 MeV on
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
. Including these corrections, the final values are

E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 39.77\pm 8.13\,\mathrm{MeV}
,

E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx-14.28\pm 11.59\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and

E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 7.18\pm 2.52\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
91
]
.
We want to point out that the approach has limitations. Since the momentum distribution parameters (Eq. (
2-8
)) were fixed at saturation density using data and/or model calculations, their possible density dependence remains unexplored; the density dependence of the kinetic EOS arises solely from the Fermi energy.

3.3 Incorporating SRC-HMT into the Nonlinear Walecka RMF and Modified-Gogny HF Models

In the previous subsections, the discussion focused on the SRC-HMT effects on the kinetic EOS while the effective nucleon interactions were not included. The next step is to examine how SRC can simultaneously modify both the kinetic and potential parts of the EOS of ANM, particularly the density dependence of the nuclear symmetry energy within a nonlinear relativistic-mean-field (RMF) model (the Walecka model) and a modified-Gogny energy functional.
The applications of the SRC-HMT modified RMF model on NS physics would be discussed in Section
§5
.
To incorporate the SRC-induced HMT into RMF models, the kinetic energy density and the corresponding pressure were modified according to (without
\beta_{J}
parameter in Eq. (
2-8
))
[
93
]

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\mathrm{kin}}_{J}=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{k_{\textrm{F}}^{J}}\textrm{d}\textbf{k}\Delta_{J}\sqrt{|\textbf{k}|^{2}+{M_{J}^{\ast 2}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}^{\phi_{J}k_{\textrm{F}}^{J}}\textrm{d}\textbf{k}C_{J}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}{|\mathbf{k}|}\right)^{4}\sqrt{|\textbf{k}|^{2}+{M_{J}^{\ast 2}}},

(3-52)

\displaystyle P_{\mathrm{kin}}^{J}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{k_{\textrm{F}}^{J}}\mathrm{d}k\Delta_{J}\frac{k^{4}}{\sqrt{k^{2}+{M_{J}^{\ast}}^{2}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3\pi^{2}}\int_{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}^{\phi_{J}k_{\textrm{F}}^{J}}\mathrm{d}kC_{J}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}{k}\right)^{4}\frac{k^{4}}{\sqrt{k^{2}+{M_{J}^{\ast}}^{2}}},

(3-53)

following the general transformation for any function
f
at zero temperature when the FFG step function is replaced by
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
containing both depletion and the HMT,

\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}(\mathrm{FFG\ step\ function})f\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}\,(\mathrm{HMT})f\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}.

(3-54)

A conceptual inconsistency of this hybrid approach should be noted: the RMF framework is unable to self-consistently reproduce the phenomenological
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
in Eq. (
2-8
) that is constrained by experiment. This issue is shared by most phenomenological mean-field models. Ideally, one would adjust the Lagrangian parameters to quantitatively reproduce the empirical
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
within the same theory. Unfortunately, strong model dependence in predicting
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
, together with the limited experimental precision on the isospin dependence of SRC, continues to prevent a fully consistent description. As a result, direct implementation of the phenomenological
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}
while readjusting only the interaction sector provides a pragmatic although imperfect means to assess SRC effects on the EOS of ANM.

The kinetic symmetry energy in the RMF model with HMT is given by
[
93
]

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}\left[1-3C_{0}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)\right]-3E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}C_{0}\left[C_{1}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)+\frac{\phi_{1}}{\phi_{0}}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{9M_{0}^{\ast,4}}{8k_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}}\frac{C_{0}\phi_{1}(C_{1}-\phi_{1})}{\phi_{0}}\left[\frac{2k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}\left(\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{3/2}-\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}\left(\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}\right)^{2}+1\right)^{1/2}-\mathrm{arcsinh}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2k_{\mathrm{F}}C_{0}(6C_{1}+1)}{3}\left[\mathrm{arcsinh}\left(\frac{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}\right)-\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{M_{0}^{\ast}}{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}\right)^{2}}-\mathrm{arcsinh}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}\right)+\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{M_{0}^{\ast}}{k_{\mathrm{F}}}\right)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{3k_{\mathrm{F}}C_{0}}{2}\Bigg[\frac{(1+3\phi_{1})^{2}}{9}\left(\frac{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}{F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}-\frac{2F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}\right)+\frac{2F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}(3\phi_{1}-1)}{9\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}-\frac{1}{9}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}+\frac{4E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}{9k_{\mathrm{F}}}\Bigg]+\frac{C_{0}(4+3C_{1})}{3}\left[\frac{F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}(1+3\phi_{1})}{\phi_{0}}-E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}\right],

(3-55)

under the Lagrangian
[
93
,
403
,
333
]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Walecka}}=

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}\left[{}_{\mu}(i\partial^{\mu}-g_{\omega}\omega^{\mu}-g_{\rho}\vec{\rho}^{\mu}\cdot\vec{\tau})-(M_{\mathrm{N}}-g_{\sigma}\sigma)\right]\psi+\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\mu}\sigma\partial^{\mu}\sigma-m_{\sigma}^{2}\sigma^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu\nu}\omega^{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}m_{\omega}^{2}\omega_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3}b_{\sigma}M_{\mathrm{N}}(g_{\sigma}\sigma)^{3}-\frac{1}{4}c_{\sigma}(g_{\sigma}\sigma)^{4}+\frac{1}{4}c_{\omega}(g_{\omega}^{2}\omega_{\mu}\omega^{\mu})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}m_{\rho}^{2}\vec{{\rho}}_{\mu}\cdot\vec{{\rho}}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{4}\vec{{\rho}}_{\mu\nu}\cdot\vec{{\rho}}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}g_{\rho}^{2}\vec{\rho}_{\mu}\cdot\vec{\rho}^{\mu}\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}g_{\omega}^{2}\omega_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}.

(3-56)

In this review, we use the terms “Walecka model” and “RMF model” interchangeably.
Moreover, in (
3-55
), we define
F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}=\sqrt{M_{0}^{\ast,2}+p_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}
with
p_{\mathrm{F}}=\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}
[
93
]
.
This expression reduces to the familiar RMF result
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/6E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}
when
\phi_{0}=1
and
\phi_{1}=0
. The presence of HMT makes
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
depend solely on
M_{0}^{\ast}\equiv M_{\mathrm{D}}^{\ast}
, and the reduction relative to the FFG case is substantial across the full range of physically reasonable effective masses. For instance, at
M_{0}^{\ast}/M_{\mathrm{N}}=0.6
, the kinetic symmetry energy becomes
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx-16.94\pm 13.66\,\mathrm{MeV}
(see FIG.
47
for the
M_{0}^{\ast}
-dependence of
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
), close to the corresponding non-relativistic values, as shown in FIG.
45
. The reduction of the kinetic symmetry energy with HMT is therefore a robust feature shared by both relativistic and non-relativistic treatments.
Similarly, the slope of the kinetic symmetry energy is
[
93
]
:

\displaystyle L^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}({E}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast,2}+M_{0}^{\ast,2})}{6{E}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast,3}}+\frac{g_{\sigma}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}M_{0}^{\ast}\rho}{2E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast,3}}\frac{\partial{\overline{\sigma}}}{\partial\rho}\right]{}\left[1-3C_{0}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)\right]-\frac{9\rho}{E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}\left(\frac{\pi^{2}}{2k_{\mathrm{F}}}-g_{\sigma}M_{0}^{\ast}\frac{\partial{\overline{\sigma}}}{\partial\rho}\right){}C_{0}\left[C_{1}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)+\frac{\phi_{1}}{\phi_{0}}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{9C_{0}\phi_{1}(C_{1}-\phi_{1})}{4\pi^{2}k_{\mathrm{F}}\phi_{0}E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}{}\Bigg[\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}{}\mathrm{arcsinh}\,\theta{}\left(\frac{3M_{0}^{\ast,5}\pi^{2}}{2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}+4g_{\sigma}k_{\mathrm{F}}M_{0}^{\ast,4}\frac{\partial{\overline{\sigma}}}{\partial\rho}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\frac{\pi^{2}}{2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{}\left(2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{5}-k_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}M_{0}^{\ast,2}-3k_{\mathrm{F}}M_{0}^{\ast,4}\right)-4g_{\sigma}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}M_{0}^{\ast}E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast,2}\frac{\partial{\overline{\sigma}}}{\partial\rho}\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{2k_{\mathrm{F}}C_{0}(6C_{1}+1)}{3}{}\left[\mathrm{arcsinh}\,(\phi_{0}\theta)-\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}-\mathrm{arcsinh}\,\theta+\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle+{2k_{\mathrm{F}}\rho C_{0}(6C_{1}+1)}{}\left(\frac{M_{0}^{\ast}\pi^{2}}{2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}+g_{\sigma}k_{\mathrm{F}}\frac{\partial{\overline{\sigma}}}{\partial\rho}\right)\times\left(\frac{\phi_{0}}{F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}M_{0}^{\ast}}+\frac{M_{0}^{\ast}}{\phi_{0}^{2}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}\sqrt{1+\frac{\displaystyle 1}{\displaystyle\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}}-\frac{1}{M_{0}^{\ast,2}\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}}-\frac{1}{M_{0}^{\ast}k_{\mathrm{F}}\theta^{2}\sqrt{1+\frac{\displaystyle 1}{\displaystyle\theta^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{3k_{\mathrm{F}}C_{0}}{2}{}\left[\frac{(1+3\phi_{1})^{2}}{9}\left(\frac{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}{F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}-\frac{2F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}\right)+\frac{2F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}(3\phi_{1}-1)}{9\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}-\frac{1}{9}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}+\frac{4E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}{9k_{\mathrm{F}}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{9k_{\mathrm{F}}\rho C_{0}}{2}{}\left(\frac{M_{0}^{\ast}\pi^{2}}{2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}+g_{\sigma}k_{\mathrm{F}}\frac{\partial{\overline{\sigma}}}{\partial\rho}\right){}\left[\frac{(1+3\phi_{1})^{2}}{9}\left(\frac{\phi_{0}}{F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}M_{0}^{\ast}}-\frac{\phi_{0}^{3}\theta^{2}}{M_{0}^{\ast,2}(1+\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2})^{3/2}}+\frac{2}{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\sqrt{1+\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\left.\frac{2(3\phi_{1}-1)}{9\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\sqrt{1+\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}-\frac{M_{0}^{\ast}}{9E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast,3}}-\frac{4}{9k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle+{\rho C_{0}(4+3C_{1})}{}\left[\frac{\pi^{2}}{2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{}\left[\frac{(1+3\phi_{1})\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}\sqrt{1+\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}-\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\left.g_{\sigma}\frac{\partial{\overline{\sigma}}}{\partial\rho}{}\left[\frac{(1+3\phi_{1})\sqrt{1+\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}{\phi_{0}}-\frac{(1+3\phi_{1})\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{M_{0}^{\ast,2}\sqrt{1+\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}-\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}+\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{M_{0}^{\ast,2}\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}}\right]\right].

(3-57)

here
\theta=k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{0}^{\ast}
(which plays the role of
\nu=k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{\mathrm{N}}
in the above),
\overline{\sigma}
is the
\sigma
-field in SNM and
\partial\overline{\sigma}/\partial\rho
is given by

\frac{\partial\overline{\sigma}}{\partial\rho}=\frac{g_{\sigma}}{R_{\sigma}}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}M_{0}^{\ast}}{{E}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}+\frac{4\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(2)}}}{3\rho}+\overline{\Phi}\right),\quad\text{ with }\overline{\Phi}=C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}M_{0}^{\ast}\left(\frac{\phi_{0}}{p_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}-\frac{1}{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}E_{\mathrm{F}}}\right),

(3-58)

with

R_{\sigma}=m_{\sigma}^{2}+3g_{\sigma}^{2}\left(\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(1)}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}-\frac{\Delta_{0}\rho}{{E}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}\right)+2b_{\sigma}Mg_{\sigma}^{3}\overline{\sigma}+3c_{\sigma}g_{\sigma}^{4}\overline{\sigma}^{2}-g_{\sigma}^{2}\left(\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(2)}}}{M_{0}^{\ast}}+\overline{\Psi}\right),\quad\text{ with }\overline{\Psi}=\frac{2C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}{\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{p_{\mathrm{F}}F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}-\frac{1}{k_{\mathrm{F}}E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}\right),

(3-59)

and

\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(1)}}=\frac{\Delta_{0}M_{0}^{\ast,3}}{\pi^{2}}\left(\theta\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}-\mathrm{arcsinh}\,\theta\right),~~\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(2)}}=\frac{2C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}{\pi^{2}M_{0}^{\ast}}\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}}-\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}\right),

(3-60)

The sum of
\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(1)}}
and
\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(2)}}
gives the total scalar density:

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{s}}\equiv\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}=\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(1)}}+\rho_{\mathrm{s},0}^{\mathrm{(2)}}=\frac{\Delta_{0}M_{0}^{\ast,3}}{\pi^{2}}\left(\theta\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}-\mathrm{arcsinh}\,\theta\right)+\frac{2C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}{\pi^{2}M_{0}^{\ast}}\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}}-\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}\right).

(3-61)

In addition, the expression for the potential part of the slope parameter of the symmetry energy is given by
[
84
]
:

L^{\mathrm{pot}}(\rho)=\frac{3g_{\rho}^{2}\rho}{2(m_{\rho}^{2}+\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}g_{\omega}^{2}g_{\rho}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}^{2})}-\frac{3g_{\omega}^{3}g_{\rho}^{4}\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}\overline{\omega}_{0}\rho^{2}}{(m_{\omega}^{2}+3c_{\omega}g_{\omega}^{4}\overline{\omega}_{0})(m_{\rho}^{2}+\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}g_{\omega}^{2}g_{\rho}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}^{2})^{2}}.

(3-62)

FIG. 47
:
(Color Online). Kinetic symmetry energy as a function of Dirac effective mass of nucleons in SNM in the RMF-FFG and RMF-HMT models at saturation density. Taken from Ref.
[
93
]
.

FIG. 48
:
(Color Online). Upper: kinetic EOS of SNM defined by Eq. (
3-63
), compared with data from the np dominance analysis. Lower: ratio of average proton to neutron kinetic energies as a function of proton fraction in the IPM and LCA. Figures taken from Refs.
[
93
,
388
]
.

One may further assess the reliability of the hybrid framework used here by comparing its predictions for the kinetic EOS with those obtained in other approaches
[
393
,
388
]
. In the nonlinear RMF model, the kinetic contribution to the EOS of SNM is defined as

\boxed{E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\equiv\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{\phi_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}\sqrt{\mathbf{k}^{2}+M_{0}^{\ast,2}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}-M_{0}^{\ast},}

(3-63)

where
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}
denotes the nucleon momentum distribution in SNM. The HMT and FFG predictions for
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
are displayed in the upper panel of FIG.
48
.
Recent analyses of electron-nucleus scattering data using the np dominance picture
[
219
,
393
]
have extracted average kinetic energies of neutrons and protons for C, Al, Fe and Pb (with uncertainties) and for
7,8,9
Li,
9,10
Be and
11
B (without uncertainties). The empirical dependence of the average kinetic energy on the mass number
A
can be mapped onto a density dependence through the well-established relation
[
106
,
111
,
383
,
140
,
139
,
137
,
520
]

\rho_{A}\approx\frac{\rho_{0}}{1+\alpha/\beta A^{1/3}},

(3-64)

where
\alpha/\beta
encodes the balance between surface and volume symmetry energies (see discussion in Subsection
3.2
). Using
\alpha/\beta\approx 2.8
[
140
]
for the relevant mass range, the average kinetic energy per nucleon,
\langle T\rangle=[\langle T_{\mathrm{n}}\rangle(1+\delta)+\langle T_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle(1-\delta)]/2
,
calculated with both the HMT and FFG parameterizations is compared with the np dominance results in the upper panel of FIG.
48
. Within the parabolic approximation for ANM,
E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{ANM}}(\rho)\approx E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{0}(\rho)+\delta^{2}E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
,
even the most neutron-rich nucleus considered (
208
Pb with
\delta^{2}\approx 0.045
) satisfies
E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{ANM}}(\rho)\approx E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
,
indicating that the results shown in the upper panel of FIG.
48
effectively represent the kinetic EOS of SNM.
It is noteworthy that the RMF-HMT prediction agrees well with the np dominance extraction, whereas the FFG estimate lies about 40% lower. It is well known that pure mean-field models underestimate spectroscopic factors from
7
Li to
208
Pb by approximately 30–40% because they miss the occupation of high-momentum states generated by SRC
[
272
]
, shown in FIG.
26
. The lower nucleon kinetic energies predicted by the RMF-FFG model relative to the RMF-HMT follow from the same underlying physics.
Another robust feature
[
219
,
388
]
is that protons carry more kinetic energy than neutrons in neutron-rich nuclei, particularly in the neutron-skin regions of heavy systems
[
90
]
(to be discussed in Subsection
4.1
). The np dominance picture predicts a growing difference between
\langle T_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle
and
\langle T_{\mathrm{n}}\rangle
as the isospin asymmetry increases. Within the low-order correlation operator approximation (LCA)
[
388
]
, nucleon kinetic energies were evaluated by isolating contributions from central, spin-isospin and tensor correlations, enabling a detailed assessment of the relative strengths of nn, pp and pn pairs in the HMT. For momenta in the range
1.5\lesssim|\mathbf{k}|\lesssim 3\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
, the distribution is dominated by tensor-induced pn correlations, and the predicted pp-pn ratio in the HMT is consistent with exclusive two-nucleon knockout measurements showing np-SRC dominance
[
388
]
.
As illustrated in the lower panel of FIG.
48
, the independent particle model (IPM) yields
\langle T_{\mathrm{n}}\rangle>\langle T_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle
, but this ordering is reversed once correlations are included. Furthermore, the difference
\langle T_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle-\langle T_{\mathrm{n}}\rangle
increases approximately linearly as the proton fraction
x_{\mathrm{p}}
decreases. For the most asymmetric nucleus studied,
{}^{48}\mathrm{Ca}
, the proton kinetic energy exceeds the neutron value by about 10%, consistent with the findings in Ref.
[
219
]
; see also the magenta and green points.

FIG. 49
:
(Color Online). Total symmetry energy versus density in the HMT and FFG scenarios, compared with several recent constraints. Figure taken from Ref.
[
93
]
.

Turning back to the RMF results, the SRC-reduced kinetic symmetry energy has important consequences for the density dependence of the total symmetry energy
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
. Since
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
and
L(\rho_{0})
are both well constrained, a smaller kinetic contribution at
\rho_{0}
necessarily implies a larger potential contribution. FIG.
49
compares the results obtained by fixing
M_{0}^{\ast}
,
E_{0}(\rho_{0})
,
\rho_{0}
,
K_{0}
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
and
L(\rho_{0})
at their empirical values. The inclusion of HMT lowers
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
but makes its slope steeper at subsaturation densities; around
0.04
fm
-3
the decrease reaches
\sim 30\%
, exceeding the current uncertainty band
[
520
]
. At suprasaturation densities the reduction persists, amounting to
\sim 25\%
near
0.5
fm
-3
. Overall, the curvature of
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
is substantially reduced, leading to a more concave density dependence.
An interesting feature is that the negative kinetic symmetry energy allows the construction of very soft or even decreasing symmetry energies at high densities by varying
L(\rho_{0})
while keeping
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
fixed
[
279
]
. Such super-soft behaviors have been suggested to be favorable for explaining certain
\pi^{-}/\pi^{+}
data in heavy-ion collisions
[
487
]
.

FIG. 50
:
(Color Online). Left: the EOS of SNM and the pressure
P_{0}
of SNM as functions of density for both the FFG and HMT models.
Right: a comparison between the model pressure
P_{0}
of SNM with the experimental constraints from analyzing nuclear collective flows in heavy ion collisions. Figure taken from Ref.
[
93
]
.

Besides the reduction of the symmetry energy due to SRC-induced HMT, the EOS of SNM in the modified Walecka model was also obtained in Ref.
[
93
]
. To assess the HMT and FFG models, the left panel of FIG.
50
shows the binding energy and pressure of SNM as functions of density; here the expressions for the EOS of SNM
E_{0}(\rho)=\varepsilon_{0}/\rho-M_{\mathrm{N}}
, the pressure
P_{0}(\rho)
as well as the coefficient of incompressibility
K_{0}(\rho)
are given by
[
93
]
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{0}M_{0}^{\ast,4}}{\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{4}\theta(1+\theta^{2})^{3/2}-\frac{1}{8}\theta\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}-\frac{1}{8}\mathrm{arcsinh}\,\theta\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}{\pi^{2}}\left[V(\theta)-\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\phi_{0}^{2}\theta^{2}}}+\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}m_{\sigma}^{2}\overline{\sigma}^{2}++\frac{1}{3}b_{\sigma}M_{\mathrm{N}}g_{\sigma}^{3}\overline{\sigma}^{3}+\frac{1}{4}c_{\sigma}g_{\sigma}^{4}\overline{\sigma}^{4},

(3-65)

\displaystyle P_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}m_{\sigma}^{2}\overline{\sigma}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}b_{\sigma}M_{\mathrm{N}}g_{\sigma}^{3}\overline{\sigma}^{3}-\frac{1}{4}c_{\sigma}g_{\sigma}^{4}\overline{\sigma}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}m_{\omega}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}c_{\omega}g_{\omega}^{4}\overline{\omega}_{0}^{4}+\frac{2\Delta_{0}M_{0}^{\ast,4}W(\theta)}{3\pi^{2}}+\frac{2C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}V(\theta)}{3\pi^{2}},

(3-66)

\displaystyle K_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle-9\frac{\partial\overline{\sigma}}{\partial\rho}\Bigg[\overline{\sigma}\left(m_{\sigma}^{2}+b_{\sigma}M_{\mathrm{N}}g_{\sigma}^{3}\overline{\sigma}+c_{\sigma}g_{\sigma}^{4}\overline{\sigma}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\frac{8g_{\sigma}\Delta_{0}M_{0}^{\ast,3}W(\theta)}{3\pi^{2}}\Bigg]+12C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}V(\theta)

\displaystyle+9\left[\frac{2\Delta_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}{3\pi^{2}M_{0}^{\ast}E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}+\frac{2C_{0}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{4}}{3\pi^{2}M_{0}^{\ast}}\left(\frac{\phi_{0}}{F_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}-\frac{1}{E_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ast}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times\left(\frac{M_{0}^{\ast}\pi^{2}}{2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}+g_{\sigma}k_{\mathrm{F}}\frac{\partial\overline{\sigma}}{\partial\rho}\right)+\frac{9\rho g_{\omega}^{2}}{Q_{\omega}}-\frac{18P_{0}(\rho)}{\rho},

(3-67)

here
Q_{\omega}=\overline{\omega}_{0}^{2}+3c_{\omega}g_{\omega}^{4}\overline{\omega}_{0}
and
\partial\overline{\sigma}/\partial\rho
is given in Eq. (
3-58
), and the two functions
W(\theta)
and
V(\theta)
are
[
93
]
:

\displaystyle W(\theta)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\theta^{3}\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}-\frac{3}{8}\theta\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}+\frac{3}{8}\mathrm{arcsinh}\,\theta,

(3-68)

\displaystyle V(\theta)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{arcsinh}(\phi_{0}\theta)-\mathrm{arcsinh}\,\theta.

(3-69)

Interestingly, the HMT model predicts a stiffer EOS for SNM at supra-saturation densities compared to the FFG model, even though by construction both models share the same values of
M_{0}^{\ast}
,
\rho_{0}
,
E_{0}(\rho_{0})
, and
K_{0}
. This is primarily due to the significant contribution of high-momentum nucleons to the kinetic EOS in the HMT model. Consequently, HMT is expected to affect the higher-order characteristic coefficients of SNM at
\rho_{0}
relative to the FFG predictions.
Quantitatively, the third-order Taylor expansion coefficient of the EOS around
\rho_{0}
, i.e., the skewness
J_{0}
of SNM
changes from
J_{0}^{\mathrm{FFG}}\approx-454\,\mathrm{MeV}
in the FFG model to
J_{0}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx-266\,\mathrm{MeV}
in the HMT model. Unfortunately, the current knowledge of
J_{0}
is still too limited to provide a meaningful constraint. On the other hand, the pressure of SNM in the density range
2\rho_{0}\sim 5\rho_{0}
has been constrained experimentally via nuclear collective flows in heavy-ion collisions, shown as a cyan band in the right panel of FIG.
50
. Despite the larger skewness in the HMT model, its EOS remains consistent with these empirical constraints, indicating that the current experimental uncertainty is too broad to distinguish HMT from FFG predictions.
Since the kinetic symmetry energy depends on the nucleon Dirac effective mass, which in turn is determined by the scalar baryon density
\rho_{\mathrm{s}}
, it is instructive to examine how the SRC-modified nucleon momentum distribution affects
\rho_{\mathrm{s}}
and
M_{0}^{\ast}
. At low densities (
\theta\ll 1
), we have from Eq. (
3-61
) that

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{s}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{0}M_{0}^{\ast,3}}{\pi^{2}}\frac{2}{3}\theta^{3}+\frac{2C_{0}M_{0}^{\ast,3}}{\pi^{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)\theta^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2M_{0}^{\ast,3}\theta^{3}}{3\pi^{2}}\left[\Delta_{0}+3C_{0}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\right)\right]=\rho.

(3-70)

The next-order correction is
M_{0}^{\ast,3}\theta^{5}(1+C_{0}\Phi_{0})/5\pi^{2}
,
which is negative, implying
\rho_{\mathrm{s}}<\rho
.
At the high-density limit
\rho\to\infty
, the
\sigma
-field saturates at
\overline{\sigma}^{\infty}\equiv\overline{\sigma}(\rho=\infty)=M_{\mathrm{N}}/g_{\sigma}
, so that the Dirac effective mass approaches zero. Correspondingly, the scalar density becomes

\rho_{\mathrm{s}}^{\infty}\equiv\rho_{\mathrm{s}}(\rho=\infty)=M_{\mathrm{N}}^{3}\left[(m_{\sigma}/g_{\sigma}M_{\mathrm{N}})^{2}+b_{\sigma}+c_{\sigma}\right]
,
giving
\rho_{\mathrm{s}}^{\infty}(\mathrm{FFG})\approx 3.29\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
and
\rho_{\mathrm{s}}^{\infty}(\mathrm{HMT})\approx 2.99\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
[
93
]
. Thus,
\rho_{\mathrm{s}}
in the HMT model is always smaller than in the FFG model.
Actually, the nucleon Dirac effective masses in SNM for both FFG and HMT model are very similar, and this can be understood as follows. The effective mass is constrained at three points:
M_{0}^{\ast}(0)/M_{\mathrm{N}}=1
,
M_{0}^{\ast}(\rho_{0})/M_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 0.6
, and
M_{0}^{\ast}(\infty)/M_{\mathrm{N}}=0
. Furthermore, Eq. (
3-58
) ensures
\partial\overline{\sigma}/\partial\rho>0
, so that
M_{0}^{\ast}/M_{\mathrm{N}}
decreases monotonically over the full density range and is concave at high densities. Given these common constraints, it is not surprising that
M_{0}^{\ast}(\rho)/M_{\mathrm{N}}
behaves very similarly in both models.

In the nonlinear RMF calculations, the HMT and FFG models are constructed to share the same symmetry energy
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
and slope parameter
L
, it is instructive to examine the curvature
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
of the symmetry energy as a measure of how the HMT influences the total symmetry energy. The quantity
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
also determines the isospin dependence of the incompressibility of ANM through
K(\delta)\approx K_{0}+K_{\mathrm{sat,2}}\delta^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{4})
,
where
K_{\mathrm{sat,2}}=K_{\mathrm{sym}}-6L-J_{0}L/K_{0}
[
109
]
represents the isospin-dependent contribution. Quantitatively, one finds
K_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{FFG}}\approx-37\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
K_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx-274\,\mathrm{MeV}
. The corresponding values of the isospin coefficient of the incompressibility are
K_{\mathrm{sat,2}}^{\mathrm{FFG}}\approx-174\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
K_{\mathrm{sat,2}}^{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx-470\,\mathrm{MeV}
, with the latter in very good agreement with the empirical estimate
K_{\mathrm{sat,2}}=-550\pm 100\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
126
]
. Overall, the inclusion of the HMT makes the symmetry energy substantially more concave around saturation density, leading to a significantly stronger isospin dependence of the incompressibility of ANM compared with the FFG model.

FIG. 51
:
(Color Online). Panel (a): The
\nu
(equivalently
\rho
) dependence of the ratio
\Psi
in the relativistic FFG model (red) compared with the nonlinear RMF model prediction (blue lines).
Panel (b): results with a general RMF construction different from the FSUGold set. Here,
M\leftrightarrow M_{\mathrm{N}}
.
Figures taken from Ref.
[
96
]
.

The nonlinear RMF model allows us to study the ratio
\Psi
of the quartic over quadratic symmetry energy even without including SRC-HMT effects. Specifically, the
\Psi
obtained with the FSUGold parameter set
[
431
]
is displayed in the left panel of FIG.
51
as the blue solid line, while the relativistic FFG prediction from Eq. (
3-26
) is shown as the dashed magenta line. It is clear that nuclear interactions significantly modify the FFG prediction (indicated by red arrows in panel (a)) at both low and high densities.
In the ultra-relativistic limit, the quadratic and quartic symmetry energies can be approximated as
[
86
]
:

\displaystyle\boxed{E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\approx\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}+\frac{c_{\omega}^{2/3}\rho^{1/3}}{2\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}},~~E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\approx\frac{5k_{\mathrm{F}}}{324}+\frac{c_{\omega}^{5/3}\rho^{1/3}}{6\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}^{2}},}

(3-71)

both scaling as
\rho^{1/3}
[
86
]
. Here,
c_{\omega}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}
characterize the self-interaction of four
\omega
mesons and the
\omega
-
\rho
meson coupling in the nonlinear RMF Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Walecka}}
of Eq. (
3-56
) via
4^{-1}c_{\omega}g_{\omega}^{4}(\omega_{\mu}\omega^{\mu})^{2}
and
2^{-1}\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}g_{\rho}^{2}g_{\omega}^{2}\omega_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}\vec{\rho}_{\nu}\cdot\vec{\rho}^{\nu}
[
86
]
, with
g_{\omega}
and
g_{\rho}
as nucleon-meson coupling constants. Consequently, the ratio in the ultra-relativistic limit is

\Psi={}^{\mathrm{RMF}}_{\mathrm{UR}}\equiv\frac{5a/324+c_{\omega}^{5/3}/6\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}^{2}}{a/6+c_{\omega}^{2/3}/2\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}},

(3-72)

where
a=(3\pi^{2}/2)^{1/3}
[
86
]
. Using FSUGold parameters
c_{\omega}=0.01
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}=0.24
, one obtains
{}_{\mathrm{UR}}^{\mathrm{RMF}}\approx 7.8\%
, quickly reached for
\nu=k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{\mathrm{N}}\gtrsim 1
. In both numerator and denominator, the first term is kinetic, the second potential. The potential-to-kinetic ratios are
(c_{\omega}^{2/3}/2\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}})/(a/6)\approx 0.24
and
(c_{\omega}^{5/3}/6\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}^{2})/(5a/324)\approx 0.04
, showing kinetic dominance in FSUGold. If
c_{\omega}
or
1/\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}
is small, the limit naturally approaches the FFG prediction
5/54
.

To explore interaction effects further, consider the theoretical scenario where the potential terms dominate,
c_{\omega}^{5/3}/6\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}^{2}\gg 5a/324
and
c_{\omega}^{2/3}/2\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}\gg a/6
. Then Eq. (
3-72
) gives
\Psi\to\xi\equiv c_{\omega}/3\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}
, independent of density. In this limit,
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)
can become comparable to or even exceed
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
, demonstrating that nucleon-nucleon interactions can strongly modify the relative strengths of quadratic and quartic symmetry energies. Numerically,
\Psi
could diverge if
\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}
is extremely small, although this occurs only at extremely high, theoretically inaccessible densities.
As an illustration, the right panel of FIG.
51
shows a constructed RMF calculation for
\Psi
(blue solid line) approaching both
{}_{\mathrm{UR}}^{\mathrm{RMF}}
(black dotted line) and the constant
\xi=c_{\omega}/3\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}=4.8
. Here,
c_{\omega}=0.01
, while
\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}=c_{\omega}/3\xi\approx 7\times 10^{-4}
, much smaller than the FSUGold set
[
431
]
. This gives
c_{\omega}^{2/3}/2\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}\approx 33.42\gg a/6\approx 0.41
and
c_{\omega}^{5/3}/6\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}^{2}\approx 160.41\gg 5a/324\approx 0.04
, confirming potential dominance. This corresponds to
\nu=k_{\mathrm{F}}/M_{\mathrm{N}}\sim 40
or
\rho\sim 2.9\times 10^{6}\rho_{0}
, highlighting the theoretical nature. Thus, nucleon-nucleon interactions, combined with relativistic effects, strongly modify
\Psi
, whereas relativistic corrections alone have minimal impact.
In contrast, at ultra-low densities, the kinetic part dominates. The kinetic symmetry energy and the potential part expand as
[
86
]

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{g_{\sigma}}{m_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}\frac{\rho}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}\right]\sim b\nu^{2},

(3-73)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{pot}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{\rho}{2}\frac{g_{\rho}^{2}}{m_{\rho}^{2}}\left[1-\Lambda_{\mathrm{V}}\left(\frac{g_{\rho}}{m_{\rho}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{g_{\omega}}{m_{\omega}}\right)^{4}\rho^{2}\right]\sim b^{\prime}\nu^{3},

(3-74)

respectively,
here
b
and
b^{\prime}
are two constants;
so as
\rho\to 0
we have
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{pot}}(\rho)/E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\sim\nu\to 0
. Similarly, the fourth-order symmetry energy is dominated by its kinetic part,
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/162M_{\mathrm{N}}+\cdots\sim c\nu^{2}+\cdots
, and similarly
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}^{\mathrm{pot}}(\rho)\approx-72^{-1}(g_{\sigma}/m_{\sigma})^{2}\rho\nu^{4}+\cdots\sim c^{\prime}\nu^{7}+\cdots
, giving
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{pot}}(\rho)/E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx(c^{\prime}/c)\nu^{5}\ll 1
. Therefore, at low densities,
\Psi
is primarily determined by kinetic contributions.
