.

As an example of non-relativistic calculations with momentum-dependent interaction, shown in FIG.
52
are the results of SNM pressure and the
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
within a modified Gogny (with the MDI interaction
[
141
]
) energy density functional (EDF) using the FFG, HMT-SCGF and HMT-exp parameter sets
[
97
]
. In this study, several quantities were fixed at their empirical values, i.e.,
M_{0}^{\ast}
,
E_{0}(\rho_{0})
,
\rho_{0}
,
K_{0}
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
,
L
,
U_{0}(\rho_{0},0)

and
U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0},1\,\mathrm{GeV})
, where
U_{0}(\rho,|\mathbf{k}|)
and
U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,|\mathbf{k}|)
is the single-nucleon isoscalar and isovector potential, respectively, see TAB.
1
for the details on the parameters
[
97
]
.
The energy per nucleon is given by
[
110
,
493
,
492
]
,

\displaystyle E(\rho,\delta)=

\displaystyle\sum_{J=\mathrm{n,p}}\frac{1}{\rho_{J}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(\rho,\delta)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}+\frac{A_{\ell}(\rho_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}+\rho_{\mathrm{n}}^{2})}{2\rho\rho_{0}}

\displaystyle+\frac{A_{\mathrm{u}}\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\rho_{\mathrm{n}}}{\rho\rho_{0}}+\frac{B}{\sigma+1}\left(\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{\sigma}(1-x\delta^{2}),

\displaystyle+\sum_{J,J^{\prime}}\frac{C_{J,J^{\prime}}}{\rho\rho_{0}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}f_{J}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})f_{J^{\prime}}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\Omega(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}).

(3-75)

The relation between the phase space distribution and the single-nucleon momentum distribution function is

f_{J}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})=\frac{2}{h^{3}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(\rho,\delta)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{3}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}(\rho,\delta),~~\mathord{\mathchar 126h}=1.

(3-76)

In the FFG model,
f_{J}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})=(1/4\pi^{3})\Theta(k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}-|\mathbf{k}|)
.
In the calculation, we use the regulator
\Omega
of
\Omega(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime})=1+{a}[({\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/{\Lambda^{2}})^{2}]^{1/4}+{b}[({\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/{\Lambda^{2}})^{2}]^{1/6}
,
where
a
and
b
are two phenomenological parameters used
[
97
]
, and
\Lambda
the high-momentum cutoff. If
\Lambda

is large compared to the momentum scale in studying, we then have
\Omega(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\approx 1-{\mathbf{k}^{2}}/{\Lambda^{2}}-{\mathbf{k}^{\prime 2}}/{\Lambda^{2}}+{2\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/{\Lambda^{2}}
.
This regulator function can be treated as a surrogate model of the original MDI regularization function.
If we change
b

to
\xi b
, then after redefining
a^{\prime}=a/\xi^{3/2}
and

\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda/\xi^{3/2}
, we have
\Omega(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime})=1+a^{\prime}[(\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime}/\Lambda^{\prime 2})^{2}]^{1/4}+b[(\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime}/\Lambda^{\prime 2})^{2}]^{1/6}
, indicating the effect of
b
can be absorbed into
\Lambda
and
a
,
i.e., we have one degree of freedom to fix one of the three
parameters
a
,
b
and
\Lambda
without affecting the physical
quantities.
The SRC-induced reduction of

E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
within the non-relativistic EDF approach is qualitatively
consistent with the RMF-HMT result shown in FIG.
49
. Nevertheless,
since there is no explicit momentum dependence in the RMF model, the
corresponding reduction of
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
is smaller. Obviously, the
momentum-dependent interaction in the modified Gogny EDF makes the SRC-induced softening of the symmetry
energy at supra-saturation densities more evident, see the upper panel of FIG.
52
.
The reduction on the symmetry energy at supra-saturation densities also generates corresponding reduction of the curvature coefficient
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
of the symmetry energy, whose
value changes from
-109
MeV in the FFG model to about

-121\,\mathrm{MeV}
/
-223
MeV in the HMT-SCGF/HMT-exp model.
While the
K_{\mathrm{sym}}\approx-223\,\mathrm{MeV}
in the HMT-exp model is slightly smaller than today’s fiducial constraint on it, these in the FFG and the HMT-SCGF model are very consistent.
Physically, the pressure of the neutron-rich nucleonic matter is
expected to decrease as some of the the nucleons tend to pair with
each other due to the SRC. If we write out the pressure, i.e.,

\displaystyle P/3\rho\approx

\displaystyle L(\rho)\delta^{2}+3\rho\frac{\mathrm{d}E_{0}(\rho)}{\mathrm{d}\rho}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle L\delta^{2}\left(1+3\chi+\frac{9}{2}\chi^{2}\right)+K_{0}\chi(1+3\chi)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\chi^{2}J_{0}+K_{\mathrm{sym}}\delta^{2}\chi(1+3\chi),

(3-77)

where
K_{0}
and

L
are fixed in our study. The decreasing of the pressure
P
(as well as a
slight increasing in
J_{0}
) needs that the
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
should to
be correspondingly reduced.
This explains the reduction of the
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
.
The HMT-induced reduction on the isospin incompressibility
coefficient
K_{\mathrm{sat},2}
can be similarly analyzed, it changes from

-365\,\mathrm{MeV}
in the FFG model to about

-378\,\mathrm{MeV}
or
-492\,\mathrm{MeV}
in the HMT-SCGF or the HMT-exp model. The maximum reduction is about 127 MeV.
Similarly, the HMT models yield a slightly higher pressure for SNM at supra-saturation densities compared to the FFG model due to the enhanced kinetic contribution from high-momentum nucleons as shown in the lower panel of FIG.
52
, yet all three models still produce pressures consistent with heavy-ion flow constraints
[
138
]
. Moreover, because the HMT-SCGF model contains fewer high-momentum nucleons than the HMT-exp model, its predicted pressure remains much closer to the FFG result.

3.4 EOS of ANM in
d
Dimensions with/without SRC-induced HMT Effects

In this subsection, we briefly discuss the role of dimensionality in the EOS of SNM, with or without the effects of SRC-HMT effects. Dimensionality characterizes the number of independent degrees of freedom required to specify the state of a system. In physical contexts, it is typically defined as the minimum number of spatial coordinates needed to identify any point in the system. More broadly, dimensionality is a universal concept extending beyond conventional physics; for instance, the dimensionality of animal behavior can be defined as the minimum number of features of the past required to optimally predict the future
[
57
]
. Although everyday phenomena occur in three spatial dimensions, treating the spatial dimension
d
as a continuous or discrete variable has historically been indispensable for developing modern theoretical methodologies and deepening our understanding of complex physical phenomena.

A prominent example is dimensional regularization in quantum field theory
[
125
]
, in which divergent Feynman integrals are analytically continued to non-integer space–time dimensions
d+1
, enabling the first consistent renormalization of non-Abelian gauge fields
[
233
]
. Likewise, Onsager’s exact solution of the two-dimensional square-lattice Ising model
[
346
]
revolutionized the study of phase transitions, laying foundations for low- and high-temperature expansions, universality, critical phenomena, and the renormalization group
[
418
,
257
]
. In the past several decades, reduced-dimensional systems have become a central focus of condensed-matter and materials physics, with remarkable discoveries such as the two-dimensional electron gas
[
21
]
, graphene
[
394
]
, and topological insulators
[
214
,
368
]
. The constant density of states in two dimensions
[
36
]
is one of the key features behind their unconventional behavior.

Recent experimental advances, including Feshbach resonances
[
113
]
, laser cooling
[
399
]
, optical lattices and box potentials
[
439
,
337
]
, and synthetic gauge fields
[
135
]
, have made it possible to realize and probe strongly correlated quantum systems across one, two, and three dimensions
[
202
,
60
,
365
]
. These efforts have led to breakthroughs such as the realization of spin-orbit coupling in degenerate Fermi gases
[
516
]
, quantum-limited spin and sound transport in 2D systems
[
310
,
414
,
64
,
448
,
357
,
292
,
117
]
, observation of non-Hermitian phase transitions
[
351
]
, superfluidity and scale anomalies in 2D Fermi gases
[
413
,
334
]
, turbulence in low dimensions
[
335
,
336
,
201
,
249
]
, generalized hydrodynamics in 1D Bose gases
[
320
]
, spin-charge separation in 1D Fermi gases
[
402
]
, and the direct measurement of the EOS of a 2D photon gas
[
81
]
. On the theoretical side, mixed-dimensional Fermi gases
[
343
]
, resonant Bose-Fermi mixtures
[
54
]
, p-wave Fermi-Bose dimers in 2D
[
47
]
, and mappings between few- and many-body systems via higher-dimensional embeddings
[
206
]
have revealed unexpected structures in low-dimensional quantum matter. The
\epsilon
-expansion approach to the unitary Fermi gas
[
342
]
, inspired by methods developed for critical phenomena
[
479
,
315
,
453
]
, further highlights the power of dimensional continuation.

The concept of dimensionality also plays fundamental roles beyond physics, notably in computational and data sciences, where high-dimensional data often possess effective low-dimensional structure. Techniques such as principal component analysis identify these low-dimensional manifolds
[
250
]
, while modern statistical learning theory relies critically on dimensional scaling
[
61
,
178
,
444
,
451
]
.
Nuclear systems, finite nuclei and infinite nucleonic matter, are among the most complex many-body systems
[
183
]
. Their properties arise from intricate momentum-, density-, spin- and isospin-dependent nuclear interactions
[
281
]
, relativistic effects
[
452
,
115
,
403
]
, extreme astrophysical environments
[
406
]
, finite-size effects, and, potentially, effective dimensionality. While nearly all nuclear many-body calculations assume three spatial dimensions, several physical scenarios naturally introduce reduced or quasi-reduced dimensional structures. Toroidal or bubble nuclei, as suggested by J. Wheeler and later explored theoretically and experimentally
[
410
,
483
,
484
,
101
]
, possess high symmetry that effectively lowers the dimensionality of nucleonic motion. Heavy-ion collisions can generate approximately 1D jet-like subsystems or 2D collective flow patterns
[
281
]
, while NS mergers exhibit strongly anisotropic emission
[
42
,
371
,
76
]
. Dimensionality is also crucial for simulating neutrino-driven supernova explosions
[
78
,
79
,
80
,
485
,
243
]
. Even within a NS, the crust’s small thickness
{t\lesssim 1\,\mathrm{km}}
relative to stellar radius
{R\approx 12\,\mathrm{km}}
raises the question of whether it may be modeled as an effectively quasi-2D layer. Similarly, the stellar core, governed by the Tolman-Oppenheimer-Volkoff equation in a single radial coordinate, invites inquiry into whether it may be treated as an effectively 1D system.
These observations motivate a systematic investigation of nuclear quantities, including the EOS of ANM, in a general spatial dimension
d
.

Exploring how the properties of nuclear matter evolve with
d
, with or without SRC-HMT effects, may reveal new structures, simplify existing treatments, and provide complementary routes to understanding dense matter in astrophysical and laboratory environments.
In the following, we first study the EOS in 2D and that in general
d
using the
\epsilon
-expansion technique, and then discuss how SRC-HMT effects may generate new features in the EOS of ANM
[
95
]
.

Example 1.
The EOS of ANM in
d
D expressed in terms of the single nucleon potential as well as its momentum and isospin decomposition could be obtained by using the HVH theorem
[
239
,
488
,
112
,
85
,
293
,
294
]
. In 2D, the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
is determined via the density
\rho
as
k_{\mathrm{F}}=(\pi\rho)^{1/2}
, and the dimensions of density and Fermi momentum scale as
[\rho]=[\mathrm{fm}]^{-2}=[\mathrm{MeV}]^{2}
. The corresponding formulas for the EOS of ANM are
[
95
]
:

\displaystyle E_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi\rho}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{1}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}\left(f,k_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\right)\mathrm{d}f=\frac{\pi\rho}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}\left(f,k_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\right)\mathrm{d}f,~~k_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}=(\pi f)^{1/2},

(3-78)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi\rho}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{\sqrt{\pi\rho}}{4}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}}),

(3-79)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym},4}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{64}\left(\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}-k_{\mathrm{F}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{3}\frac{\partial^{3}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{3}}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{32}\left(\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+k_{\mathrm{F}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{8}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym},2}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{4}U_{\mathrm{sym},3}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}}),

(3-80)

\displaystyle P_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{3\pi\rho^{2}}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}-\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}\left(f,k_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\right)\mathrm{d}f+\rho U_{0}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}}),

(3-81)

\displaystyle K_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle 9\rho\frac{\mathrm{d}U_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\rho}+\frac{18}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}\left(f,k_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\right)\mathrm{d}f-18U_{0}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}}),

(3-82)

\displaystyle J_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle 27\rho^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}U_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\rho^{2}}-81\rho\frac{\mathrm{d}U_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\rho}

\displaystyle-\frac{162}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}\left(f,k_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\right)\mathrm{d}f+162U_{0}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}}),

(3-83)

\displaystyle L(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{3\pi\rho}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{3\pi\rho}{8}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\rho}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right]_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle+\left.\sqrt{\pi\rho}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{3}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+3U_{\mathrm{sym},2}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}}),

(3-84)

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{SNM}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle 9\frac{\partial P_{0}(\rho)}{\partial\rho}=\frac{9\pi\rho}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}+9\rho\frac{\mathrm{d}U_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\rho},

(3-85)

see also Eqs. (
3-102
) and (
3-103
) for the symmetry energies.
All the kinetic parts of the characteristic coefficients beyond the linear terms
L_{0}(\rho)
and
L(\rho)
are zero (including the coefficients
K_{0}(\rho),J_{0}(\rho)
and
K_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
), i.e., the EOS are totally represented by the effective potentials. Furthermore, the higher-order kinetic symmetry energies including the
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)
are also zero (the sixth-order term is given by
E_{\mathrm{sym},6}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=(3d-2)(d-1)(d-2)(2d-1)k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/180d^{5}M_{\mathrm{N}}
) which vanishes exactly at
d=2
, indicating that the conventional parabolic approximation of the kinetic EOS of ANM is exact in 2D. Since the potential contribution to the EOS is essentially smaller than its kinetic part (especially at low densities), the absence of the kinetic part in the
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)
already indicates the parabolic approximation should be good in 2D.

In 2D, the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
scales with the density as
\rho^{1/2}
, which implies that the kinetic symmetry energy is linear in density. Moreover, if the single-nucleon potential
U_{0}
depends on momentum
k
according to
U_{0}(\rho,k)\approx a+bk+ck^{2}+\cdots
and
U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}})\approx a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}k_{\mathrm{F}}+c^{\prime}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
, then
\partial U_{0}/\partial k\approx b+2ck+\cdots
(similarly for the symmetry potential). Consequently, the density dependence of
E_{0}(\rho)
or the symmetry energy can be expressed as

\sim f_{1}\sqrt{\rho}+f_{2}\rho+f_{3}\rho^{3/2}+\cdots,

(3-86)

indicating that the effective expansion is based on
\rho^{1/2}
, in contrast to the 3D case where
k_{\mathrm{F}}\sim\rho^{1/3}
serves as the expansion element.
To illustrate, consider
U_{0}
as a function of density
\rho
alone (i.e., neglecting momentum dependence), in a polynomial form,

U_{0}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}c_{i}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{i},~~k_{\mathrm{F}}=\sqrt{\pi\rho}.

(3-87)

Then one finds

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}U_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\rho}=\frac{1}{2\rho}\sum_{i=1}ic_{i}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{i},~\frac{1}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}(f)\mathrm{d}f=2\sum_{i=1}\frac{c_{i}}{i+2}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}.

(3-88)

Accordingly, the EOS of SNM can be written as

E_{0}(\rho)=\frac{\pi\rho}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+2\sum_{i=1}\frac{c_{i}}{i+2}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{i},

(3-89)

while the pressure and incompressibility are

\displaystyle P_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\rho\left(\frac{3\pi\rho}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\sum_{i=1}\frac{i}{i+2}c_{i}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}\right),

(3-90)

\displaystyle K_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle 9\sum_{i=1}\frac{i}{2}\frac{i-2}{i+2}c_{i}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}.

(3-91)

FIG. 53
:
Sketch of the single-nucleon potential
U_{0}(\rho)
as a function of
\rho
in 2D, here
M\leftrightarrow M_{\mathrm{N}}
.
Figures taken from Ref.
[
95
]
.

If only the first two terms in the density expansion (
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
) are retained, we then have

\displaystyle E_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}c_{1}\sqrt{\pi\rho}+\left(\frac{1}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{c_{2}}{2}\right)\pi\rho,

(3-92)

\displaystyle P_{0}(\rho)/\rho=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}c_{1}\sqrt{\pi\rho}+\left(\frac{3}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{c_{2}}{2}\right)\pi\rho,

(3-93)

\displaystyle K_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}c_{1}\sqrt{\pi\rho}.

(3-94)

Assuming that the 2D nuclear system behaves qualitatively like the 3D one, i.e.,
E_{0}(\rho)
is negative at low densities and eventually becomes positive as density increases, we obtain the condition
c_{1}<0
, and also
1/4M_{\mathrm{N}}+c_{2}/2>0
and
3/4M_{\mathrm{N}}+c_{2}/2>0
, implying
c_{2}>-1/2M_{\mathrm{N}}
. Notably, the first condition can alternatively be inferred from
K_{0}(\rho)
, which also predicts a
\sqrt{\rho}
-dependence of
K_{0}(\rho)
.
Furthermore, if one assumes that the potential
U_{0}
eventually turns positive as
\rho
increases, the coefficient
c_{2}
must satisfy
c_{2}>0
. Under this assumption, three characteristic densities can be defined: the saturation density
\rho_{0}
where
P_{0}(\rho_{0})=0
, the density
\rho_{\mathrm{v}}
corresponding to the minimum of
U_{0}
with
\mathrm{d}U_{0}/\mathrm{d}\rho|_{\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{v}}}=0
, and the crossing density
\rho_{+}
where
U_{0}(\rho_{+})=0
,

\sqrt{\pi\rho_{0}}=-\frac{c_{1}}{3}\cdot\frac{1}{3/4M_{\mathrm{N}}+c_{2}/2},~\sqrt{\pi\rho_{\mathrm{v}}}=-\frac{c_{1}}{2c_{2}},~\sqrt{\pi\rho_{+}}=-\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}}.

(3-95)

Consequently,

\frac{\rho_{+}}{\rho_{\mathrm{v}}}=4,\quad\frac{\rho_{+}}{\rho_{0}}=\frac{9}{4}\left(1+\frac{3}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}c_{2}}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{9}{4}.

(3-96)

These relations illustrate that
U_{0}
is attractive at low densities, reaches a minimum at
\rho_{\mathrm{v}}
, and becomes repulsive above
\rho_{+}
. See FIG.
53
for an illustration.

Next, we can examine the symmetry energy and fourth-order symmetry energy using Eqs. (
3-79
) and (
3-80
). Suppose the single-nucleon potential has a significant first-order symmetry potential
U_{\mathrm{sym}}
while higher-order terms are negligible (
U_{\mathrm{sym,2}}\approx U_{\mathrm{sym,3}}\approx\cdots\approx 0
), and the momentum-dependence is weak. Then one can approximate

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{4}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}},

(3-97)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{64}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}.

(3-98)

In 3D, using
M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}(\rho_{0})\gtrsim 0.8M_{\mathrm{N}}
[
279
]
and
U_{\mathrm{sym}}\lesssim 37\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
279
,
293
,
294
]
, the ratio

\displaystyle\frac{E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})}{E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})}\approx

\displaystyle\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{64}\left(\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}}-\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)\right/\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{32}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}}-1\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{128},

(3-99)

indicating that the parabolic approximation remains reasonable in 2D.
By comparison, in 1D, the symmetry energy and fourth-order symmetry energy are given as
[
95
]

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}},

(3-100)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}}{24}\frac{\partial^{3}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{3}}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{8}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{4}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym,2}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{4}U_{\mathrm{sym,3}},

(3-101)

with
k_{\mathrm{F}}=\pi\rho/4
, using the general formulas:

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2d}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{2d}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}}),

(3-102)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym},4}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{24d}\left(\frac{2}{d}-1\right)\left(\frac{2}{d}-2\right)\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}

\displaystyle+\Bigg[\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}}{24d^{3}}\frac{\partial^{3}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{3}}+\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{8d^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{d}-1\right)\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{24d}\left(\frac{1}{d}-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{d}-2\right)\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\Bigg]_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{8d^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}-\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{8d}\left(\frac{1}{d}-1\right)\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right]_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{4d}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym},2}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{4}U_{\mathrm{sym},3}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}}).

(3-103)

Under the same assumption of weak higher-order terms, the fourth-order symmetry energy can be approximated as zero, so the parabolic approximation works even better in 1D.

Finally, in the large-
d
limit, due to small pre-factors like
1/24d,1/8d^{2},1/24d^{3}
[
95
]
, etc., the fourth-order symmetry energy is expected to remain small. If the first-order symmetry potential dominates over the third-order term (
2^{-1}U_{\mathrm{sym}}>4^{-1}U_{\mathrm{sym,3}}
), then
{E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})}/{E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})}
remains small, confirming the validity of the parabolic approximation. In the infinite-
d
limit, one finds
{E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})}/{E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}(\rho_{0})}=U_{\mathrm{sym,3}}/2U_{\mathrm{sym}}\ll 1
. Therefore, writing the EOS of ANM in a general dimension
d
provides a clear understanding of the relative importance of different expansion terms and explains why the conventional parabolic approximation in 3D is not coincidental
[
95
]
.
See similar discussions of Subsection
3.1
.

Example 2.
We perturb the EOS of ANM in
d=3+\epsilon
with
\epsilon
being the perturbative dimension, we need to expand the relevant quantities assuming
\epsilon
is small.
The expansion of the function
a(d)=[2^{d-2}\pi^{d/2}\Gamma({d}/{2}+1)]^{1/d}
around
\epsilon=0
is

\displaystyle a(3+\epsilon)\approx({3\pi^{2}}/{2})^{1/3}\left(1+\sigma\epsilon\right),

(3-104)

where,

\sigma=\frac{4}{9}-\frac{{}_{\mathrm{E}}}{6}+\frac{1}{18}\ln\left(\frac{4}{9\pi}\right)\approx 0.2396,

(3-105)

where
{}_{\mathrm{E}}\approx 0.5773
is Euler’s constant.
The Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
in
3+\epsilon
dimensions to linear order of
\epsilon
is
k_{\mathrm{F}}\approx\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}(1+\sigma\epsilon)
, where
\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}=({3\pi^{2}\rho}/{2})^{1/3}
is the conventional 3D Fermi momentum (indicated by the “
\overline{~~}
” over the quantity).
The final expressions for relevant quantities are
[
95
]

\displaystyle{E}_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{3\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{10M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{1}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}\left(f,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\right)\mathrm{d}f+\epsilon\left[\frac{3\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{5M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\sigma+\frac{1}{15}\right)+\frac{\sigma}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}\left(\left.\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}^{f}_{\mathrm{F}}}\cdot\overline{k}^{f}_{\mathrm{F}}\right)\mathrm{d}f\right],

(3-106)

\displaystyle P_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\rho}{5M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}\left(f,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\right)\mathrm{d}f+\rho U_{0}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left[\frac{\rho\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{10M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{5}\right)-\sigma\int_{0}^{\rho}\left(\left.\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}^{f}_{\mathrm{F}}}\cdot\overline{k}^{f}_{\mathrm{F}}\right)\mathrm{d}f+\rho\sigma\left.\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\right],

(3-107)

\displaystyle K_{0}(\rho)=

\displaystyle-\frac{3\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{5M_{\mathrm{N}}}+9\rho\left(\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial\rho}+\left.\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{3\rho}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\right)+\frac{18}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}U_{0}\left(f,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\right)\mathrm{d}f-18U_{0}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left[3\sigma\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}-\frac{3\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{5M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{7}{15}+2\sigma\right)+\frac{18\sigma}{\rho}\int_{0}^{\rho}\left(\left.\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}^{f}_{\mathrm{F}}}\cdot\overline{k}^{f}_{\mathrm{F}}\right)\mathrm{d}f-15\sigma\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\right]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}},

(3-108)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left[\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\left({\displaystyle\sigma\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}+\left(\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right)+\frac{\sigma}{2}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\right]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}},

(3-109)

\displaystyle L(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{3M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left(\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}+\left.\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{3}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})+3U_{\mathrm{sym},2}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left[\left(2\sigma-\frac{2}{3}\right)\left(\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{3M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}\right)+\frac{\sigma}{6}\frac{\partial^{3}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{3}}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}+\frac{\sigma}{6}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\left(\sigma-\frac{2}{3}\right)\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\left.+\left(\frac{5}{2}\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}+\sigma\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+3\sigma\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym,2}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\right]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}.

(3-110)

These expressions are physically intuitive and useful since all the quantities involved are those in 3D, where they are well known and most of them are tightly constrained either theoretically or experimentally. Let us now turn to the situation in which a nonzero
\epsilon
is applied and examine several main features. In addition to the kinetic contribution, a non-trivial part of the EOS of SNM arises from integrating over the momentum dependence of the potential
U_{0}
, i.e.,
(\sigma/\rho)\int_{0}^{\rho}[\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{f}\cdot\partial U_{0}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}^{f}_{\mathrm{F}}}\mathrm{d}f
. From nuclear optical model analyses of nucleon-nucleus scattering data, one knows that the nucleon isoscalar potential
U_{0}
increases with momentum
[
293
,
294
,
213
]
; thus the above integral is positive since
\sigma>0
. Consequently, the linear
\epsilon
-term contributes a positive correction to the unperturbed EOS of SNM, as shown in Eq. (
3-106
). Extrapolating the 3D EOS of SNM to lower (higher) dimensions therefore reduces (enhances) its magnitude, with 2D SNM being more strongly bound than its 3D counterpart.
For the symmetry energy, we first neglect the momentum dependence of the symmetry potential and the second derivative
\partial^{2}U_{0}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}
. Under these simplifications, the
\epsilon
-term of Eq. (
3-109
) becomes

\displaystyle\epsilon\left[\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\left(\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}}\left(\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)+\sigma\right]\epsilon\approx\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(0.24-0.09\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}}\right)\epsilon,

(3-111)

where
M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}\gtrsim 0.8M_{\mathrm{N}}
[
279
]
is the scalar Landau effective mass. Since the bracket is very likely positive, the linear
\epsilon
-correction becomes negative for
\epsilon<0
, implying that the symmetry energy in 2D is reduced (similar to the EOS of SNM). However, because the symmetry potential
U_{\mathrm{sym}}
still carries large uncertainties, especially regarding its momentum dependence
[
293
,
294
,
279
]
, the final implications of Eq. (
3.4
) and of the term
(\sigma/2)[\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\cdot\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}
remain inconclusive and require further analysis.
To illustrate the sensitivity, we rewrite the linear
\epsilon
-term in Eq. (
3-109
) as

\Pi=\sigma\left(\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{3M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{2}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}-\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{18M^{\ast}_{\mathrm{s}}},

(3-112)

and adopt the optical-model fitted values in Ref.
[
293
]
. These give
{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}/{3M_{\mathrm{N}}}+6^{-1}[{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\cdot{\partial U_{0}}/{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\approx 37.7\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
6^{-1}[{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}\cdot{\partial^{2}U_{0}}/{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\approx-2.3\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and
[\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\cdot\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\approx-46.0\,\mathrm{MeV}
. As a result,
\Pi\approx(12.4\sigma-5.1)\epsilon\,\mathrm{MeV}\approx-2.1\epsilon\,\mathrm{MeV}
, suggesting that the symmetry energy becomes enhanced when the dimension is perturbed downward. Nevertheless, even a small change in
[\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\cdot\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}
could flip the sign of
\Pi
. More accurate constraints on
U_{\mathrm{sym}}
and/or higher-order
\epsilon
-calculations are thus crucial.

The perturbative expression Eq. (
3-109
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1+\epsilon\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\left[1+\epsilon\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\right]\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\sigma\left(\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{2}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}-\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}.

(3-113)

To streamline the structure, we introduce the effective mass
M_{\mathrm{eff}}
(relative to the bare mass), the effective isoscalar potential
U_{0}^{\mathrm{eff}}
, and the effective symmetry potential
U_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{eff}}
:

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{N}}\left[1+\epsilon\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\right]^{-1},

(3-114)

\displaystyle U_{0}^{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho,|\mathbf{k}|)=

\displaystyle\left[1+\epsilon\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\right]U_{0},

(3-115)

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho,|\mathbf{k}|)=

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,|\mathbf{k}|)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt+2\epsilon\sigma\left(\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{2}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}-\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}.

(3-116)

These quantities all depend on the perturbative parameter
\epsilon
. With these definitions, the symmetry energy becomes

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{eff}}}\left.+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}^{\mathrm{eff}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{s},\mathrm{eff}}^{\ast}}+\frac{1}{2}U^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}),

(3-117)

where
M_{\mathrm{s},\mathrm{eff}}^{\ast}/M_{\mathrm{eff}}=[1+(M_{\mathrm{eff}}/|\mathbf{k}|)\partial U_{0}^{\mathrm{eff}}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|]^{-1}_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}
is the scalar Landau effective mass based on
M_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Remarkably, (
3.4
) preserves the same functional form as the unperturbed symmetry energy in 3D. For the special case of 2D (
\epsilon=-1
), we have
M_{\mathrm{eff}}>M_{\mathrm{N}}
and similarly
0<\partial U_{0}^{\mathrm{eff}}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|<\partial U_{0}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|
, since
-1<\epsilon(2\sigma-1/3)<0
. Thus Eq. (
3.4
) indicates that, in 2D, the nucleon behaves as if it moves with a heavier mass in a less momentum-dependent potential compared with 3D. The effective form Eq. (
3.4
) therefore offers a convenient starting point for studying
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
in reduced dimensions.

To explore the performance of the
\epsilon
-expansion, we next compare it with exact
d
-dimensional expressions for the kinetic contributions. A natural issue is how to define the Fermi momentum in
d
D. We adopt two schemes: (a) The Fermi momentum is taken to be the same in all dimensions (e.g.,
k_{\mathrm{F}}\approx 200
-300 MeV), eliminating the need for perturbations in
k_{\mathrm{F}}
; the factor
\sigma
is then unnecessary; (b) Alternatively, we fix the 3D density (as in the previous perturbative treatment) and relate
\rho_{d}
in
d
D to it by
\rho_{d}=\rho^{d/3}
, so that
k_{\mathrm{F}}\sim\rho^{1/3}
holds in any dimension. In this scheme,
\sigma
must be retained.

For the total EOS, the kinetic trend under dimensional perturbation is relatively transparent. Let us extrapolate the kinetic EOS of SNM and the kinetic symmetry energy to other dimensions and compare with the exact results. For
\epsilon=-1
(2D), we obtain

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{3\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{10M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(1-2\sigma-\frac{2}{15}\right)=\frac{3\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{10M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{13}{15}-2\sigma\right).

(3-118)

If
\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}
is assumed equal in all dimensions and hence
\sigma
is set to zero, the ratio of this approximation to the exact 2D kinetic EOS
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/4M_{\mathrm{N}}
becomes
26/25\approx 1.04
, indicating that the perturbation from
d=3
to
d=2
is reasonably accurate. Similar extrapolation to
d=4
(
\epsilon=1
) and
d=1
(
\epsilon=-2
) yields ratios
51/50\approx 1.02
and
33/25\approx 1.32
, respectively, confirming that the approximation worsens as
|\epsilon|
increases.
For the kinetic symmetry energy, we have as
\sigma\to 0
,

E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1+\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\epsilon\right]\to\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{3}\right).

(3-119)

Thus for 2D this gives
2\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/9M_{\mathrm{N}}
, close to the exact
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/4M_{\mathrm{N}}
, with ratio
8/9\approx 0.89
. For 4D (
\epsilon=1
) and 1D (
\epsilon=-2
), the ratios using (
3-102
) are again
8/9\approx 0.89
and
5/9\approx 0.56
, confirming the method’s effectiveness for the kinetic part.

If instead the Fermi momenta differ across dimensions according to
\rho_{d}=\rho^{d/3}
, then
\sigma
must remain. For 2D, where
k_{\mathrm{F}}=(\pi\rho_{2})^{1/2}
, the ratio becomes

\left.\frac{3\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{10M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{13}{15}-2\sigma\right)\right/\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}=\frac{6}{5}\pi^{1/3}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2/3}\left(\frac{13}{15}-2\sigma\right)\approx 0.89,

(3-120)

where the perturbative 3D kinetic EOS gives about 8.56 MeV and the exact 2D value is about 9.60 MeV. For 4D (
\epsilon=1
) and 1D (
\epsilon=-2
), the corresponding kinetic EOS values lead to ratios
\approx 0.98
and
-3.96
, respectively. While the extrapolation to 1D is clearly unreasonable, the absolute magnitudes are still comparable.
For the kinetic symmetry energy, the ratio becomes

\pi^{1/3}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{5/2}\left(\frac{4}{3}-2\sigma\right)\approx 1.09,

(3-121)

corresponding to 10.49 MeV from the perturbative expression versus 9.60 MeV from the exact 2D value. Again, the
\epsilon
-expansion performs well. For reference, the 4D and 1D cases give ratios 1.04 and 2.31, respectively.

We emphasize that all perturbative calculations of the EOS of ANM presented above are performed in conventional 3D space. However, there is no fundamental reason why the reference dimension must be fixed at
3
. Indeed, several examples in many-body and quantum field theories show that the reference dimension can naturally differ from
3
[
342
,
479
]
. Motivated by this, we generalize the correction factor
\sigma
to an arbitrary reference dimension
d_{\mathrm{f}}
and examine how the corresponding
\epsilon
-expansion modifies the symmetry energy in
d_{\mathrm{f}}
dimensions.
The generalized form of
\sigma
reads

\displaystyle\sigma(d_{\mathrm{f}})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2d_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}\left(d_{\mathrm{f}}\Psi\left(\frac{d_{\mathrm{f}}}{2}\right)+2(d_{\mathrm{f}}+2)\ln 2+d_{\mathrm{f}}\ln\pi+2\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{d_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}\ln\left(2^{d_{\mathrm{f}}}\pi^{d_{\mathrm{f}}/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d_{\mathrm{f}}}{2}+1\right)\right),

(3-122)

where
\Psi(x)\equiv\mathrm{d}\ln\Gamma(x)/\mathrm{d}x
is the digamma function. The conventional 3D value is recovered as
\sigma(3)\equiv\sigma\approx 0.2396
, see Eq. (
3-105
).
As an illustration, the symmetry energy to linear
\epsilon
-order, neglecting the second derivative of
U_{0}
with respect to
|\mathbf{k}|
, becomes then:

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\approx\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2d_{\mathrm{f}}M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{2d_{\mathrm{f}}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\Bigg[\left(\sigma_{\mathrm{f}}-\frac{1}{2d_{\mathrm{f}}}\right)\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{d_{\mathrm{f}}M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}}+\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{2}\sigma_{\mathrm{f}}\left(\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}-\frac{1}{d_{\mathrm{f}}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}\Bigg],

(3-123)

where
\sigma_{\mathrm{f}}\equiv\sigma(d_{\mathrm{f}})
and the second line is a direct generalization of Eq. (
3-112
).
Assuming the momentum dependence of the single-particle potentials remains qualitatively the same as in 3D, i.e.,

\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|<0
and
\partial U_{0}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|>0
, the first
\epsilon
-order term
(\sigma_{\mathrm{f}}-1/{2d_{\mathrm{f}}}){k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}/{d_{\mathrm{f}}M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}}

decreases (but remains positive) as
d_{\mathrm{f}}
increases. The second term eventually saturates to
({k_{\mathrm{F}}}/{2})\sigma_{\mathrm{f}}{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}/{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}
,
so that in the large-
d_{\mathrm{f}}
limit the density dependence of the perturbed symmetry energy is dominated by the momentum dependence of the symmetry potential. Given
\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|<0
, an upward perturbation (
\epsilon>0
) tends to reduce
E_{\mathrm{sym}}
relative to its
d_{\mathrm{f}}
-dimensional value.
For the special case
d_{\mathrm{f}}=1
, the symmetry energy becomes

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\approx\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{2}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,k_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left[\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}}(\sigma_{1}-1)+\sigma_{1}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{2}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right)_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}\right].

(3-124)

Since
\sigma_{1}>1
and the combination
[{k_{\mathrm{F}}}/{M_{\mathrm{N}}}+{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}/{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}]_{|\mathbf{k}|=k_{\mathrm{F}}}>0

as in 3D
[
293
]
, both
\epsilon
-order contributions are positive. Consequently, an upward perturbation (
\epsilon>0
) enhances
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
.

These results demonstrate that dimensionality can qualitatively change the conclusions regarding the EOS of ANM. Furthermore, the relatively smaller values of
\sigma_{\mathrm{f}}
for
d_{\mathrm{f}}\approx 2
or
3
compared to
\sigma_{1}\approx 1.5253
indicate that
\epsilon
-expansion around
d_{\mathrm{f}}=1
may be challenging in certain applications.
Although the linear-order
\epsilon
-expansion based on 3D provides reasonably accurate estimates for both the kinetic EOS of SNM and the kinetic symmetry energy compared with exact results, further studies are needed to consolidate its validity. It will be important to extend the expansion to higher orders (since the breakdown at
\epsilon=-2
from 3D base already signals the limits of the linear approximation) and to incorporate effective NN interactions.

Specifically, by perturbing the symmetry energy in 3D to second order in
\epsilon=d-3
, we obtain, by generalizing Eq. (
3-109
),

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\Bigg[\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\left({\displaystyle\sigma\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}+\left(\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\sigma}{2}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\Bigg]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\Bigg[\frac{\sigma^{2}\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{12M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}M_{\mathrm{N}}\frac{\partial^{3}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{3}}+2M_{\mathrm{N}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+3M_{\mathrm{N}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+2\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{\sigma\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{18M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(M_{\mathrm{N}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+2\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\sigma^{\prime}\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(M_{\mathrm{N}}\frac{\partial^{2}U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}+\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+\frac{3M_{\mathrm{N}}}{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+2\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{54M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+1\right)\Bigg]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}},

(3-125)

where
\sigma^{\prime}\approx-0.0307
characterizes the second-order correction of the Fermi momentum via

k_{\mathrm{F}}\approx\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}(1+\sigma\epsilon+\sigma^{\prime}\epsilon^{2})
.
TAB.
2
lists the contributions from different models to the symmetry energy at order
\epsilon^{2}
[
95
]
.
In the ImMDI model, for instance, if the
y
parameter is chosen as
y\approx-115
MeV
[
492
]
, all
\epsilon^{2}
contributions to
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
are negative. A general feature, however, is that the second-order contribution does not change the qualitative conclusion from the linear-order term. For example, in the Skyrme model
[
455
]
, although the second-order contribution is negative, the overall result remains positive due to the dominant linear term.
This demonstrates that the second-order term indeed acts as a small “perturbation” even when
\epsilon=-1
is applied for the 2D symmetry energy. Similarly, one can define an effective form of the 2D symmetry energy as in Eq. (
3.4
).

-contribution

]

[

]
