:
Second order of
\epsilon
correction to the symmetry energy from different models. Table taken from Ref.
[
95
]
.

If one neglects the second- and third-order momentum dependence of
U_{0}
, i.e.,
\partial^{2}U_{0}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\approx 0
and
\partial^{3}U_{0}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{3}\approx 0
, as well as the second-order momentum dependence of
U_{\mathrm{sym}}
, i.e.,
\partial^{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}/\partial|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\approx 0
, then the symmetry energy perturbed to order
\epsilon^{2}
can be approximated as
[
95
]
:

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)=\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}+\left.\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\right|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho,\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left[\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(2\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}{6}\left(\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\cdot\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}\right]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\Bigg[\frac{\sigma^{2}\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}-\frac{\sigma\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{18M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+2\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\sigma^{\prime}\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+\frac{3M_{\mathrm{N}}}{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\frac{\partial U_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+2\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{54M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{N}}}{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}\frac{\partial U_{0}}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}+1\right)\Bigg]_{|\mathbf{k}|=\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}}.

(3-126)

For the four models listed in TAB.
2
, the
\epsilon^{2}
-order contributions are
-0.46\epsilon^{2}
,
-0.36\epsilon^{2}
and
8.2\epsilon^{2}-0.003y\epsilon^{2}
, respectively.
In addition, the correction to the kinetic symmetry energy in 2D at order
\epsilon^{2}
is given by

[\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/(54M_{\mathrm{N}})]\cdot[(3\sigma-1)^{2}+18\sigma^{\prime}]\approx-0.65\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
leading to
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 9.84\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
Compared with the linear-order estimate of approximately
10.49\,\mathrm{MeV}
(see estimate of Eq. (
3-121
)), the second-order correction brings the result much closer to the exact value of about
9.60\,\mathrm{MeV}
. For
|\epsilon|\lesssim 1
, higher-order corrections (i.e., beyond
\epsilon^{2}
) are expected to be small.

SRC-HMT Effects on Kinetic EOS in
d
Dimensions.

Now we combine the ideas of the above two examples, namely the EOS of ANM in general
d
dimensions and the method of
\epsilon
-expansion, to study the effects of SRC-HMT on the kinetic EOS. When generalizing
n_{\mathbf{k}}
to arbitrary dimensions, certain assumptions on the involved parameters are necessary. The contact coefficient
C_{J}
, explored so far in nuclear systems using different theoretical approaches, or in ultra-cold gases with various trapping potentials at zero or finite temperatures, all exhibit a remarkable universality
[
70
,
265
,
421
,
157
,
266
,
477
,
389
,
195
,
228
,
447
,
385
,
501
,
332
,
105
,
244
,
364
]
. We therefore assume that
C_{J}
(including its
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
components) is also (approximately) universal with respect to the spatial dimension
d
.
On the other hand, to our knowledge, there are no fundamental constraints on the high-momentum cutoffs in dimensions other than
3
. We thus assume that they are also universal and investigate the kinetic EOS of ANM based on these assumptions. Although the SRC-induced HMT affects only the kinetic EOS (through the single-nucleon momentum distribution function) in the present study, it can be regarded as an effective nucleon-nucleon interaction.

For nucleons in ANM in
d
dimensions, the normalization relation of the HMT parameters should be generalized as:

\Delta_{J}+\frac{dC_{J}}{d-4}\left(\phi_{J}^{d-4}-1\right)=1,

(3-127)

where the second term on the left-hand side corresponds to
x_{J}^{\mathrm{HMT}}
in
d
dimensions.
A notable feature is that
\phi_{J}
in
d=1,2,3
can be mathematically taken to be infinite, whereas in
d=4
it has a natural upper bound,
\phi_{J}\leq\exp(1/4C_{J})
.
Similarly, the kinetic EOS of ANM in
d
dimensions is given by

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho,\delta)=\sum_{J=\mathrm{n,p}}\frac{d}{d+2}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(1+\tau_{3}^{J}\delta\right)^{1+2/d}

\displaystyle\times\left[1+C_{J}\left(\frac{8}{(d-2)(d-4)}+\frac{d+2}{d-2}\phi^{d-2}_{J}-\frac{d}{d-4}\phi^{d-4}_{J}\right)\right].

(3-128)

The dependence of
E^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho,\delta)
on the spatial dimension
d
is non-trivial due to the intricate interplay among
C_{J}
,
\phi_{J}
, and
d
.

According to the general formula (
3.4
), the kinetic part of the SNM EOS in
d
D can be directly obtained as

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=\frac{d}{d+2}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}

\displaystyle\times\left[1+C_{0}\left(\frac{8}{(d-2)(d-4)}+\frac{d+2}{d-2}\phi^{d-2}_{0}-\frac{d}{d-4}\phi^{d-4}_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\Lambda(d,C_{0},\phi_{0}),

(3-129)

where the last relation defines the function
\Lambda(d,C_{0},\phi_{0})
, which encodes the dependence of the EOS on the dimension
d
. We note that
\Lambda(d,C_{0},\phi_{0})
apparently diverges at
d=d_{\mathrm{f}}=2
and/or
d=d_{\mathrm{f}}=4
. Nevertheless, away from the poles with
d=d_{\mathrm{f}}+\epsilon
, the diverging terms cancel completely and naturally in the
\epsilon
-expansion. For example, in the 2D case, one obtains the
\epsilon
-expansion

\displaystyle\frac{8}{(d-2)(d-4)}+\frac{d+2}{d-2}\phi^{d-2}_{0}-\frac{d}{d-4}\phi^{d-4}_{0}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-1+4\ln\phi_{0}+\frac{1}{\phi_{0}^{2}}+\epsilon\left(1+\ln\phi_{0}+2\ln^{2}\phi_{0}+\frac{\ln\phi_{0}+1}{\phi_{0}^{2}}\right),

(3-130)

to order
\epsilon=d-2
, using
a^{x}\approx 1+x\ln a+2^{-1}x^{2}\ln^{2}a
for small
x
.
Expanding the EOS of SNM around the dimension
d_{\mathrm{f}}
(with
d_{\mathrm{f}}=1,2,3,4
) to order
\epsilon
yields the corresponding expression
E_{0,d_{\mathrm{f}}=1\sim 4}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
[
95
]
. The
\epsilon^{0}
-order terms of these expansions represent the EOS of SNM in each dimension:

\displaystyle E_{0,{(\mathrm{1D})}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1+C_{0}\left(\frac{8}{3}-\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}+\frac{1}{3\phi_{0}^{3}}\right)\right],

(3-131)

\displaystyle E_{0,{(\mathrm{2D})}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{4M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1+C_{0}\left(4\ln\phi_{0}+\frac{1}{\phi_{0}^{2}}-1\right)\right],

(3-132)

\displaystyle E_{0,{(\mathrm{3D})}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{5}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1+C_{0}\left(5\phi_{0}+\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}-8\right)\right],

(3-133)

\displaystyle E_{0,{(\mathrm{4D})}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{3M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1+C_{0}\left(3\phi_{0}^{2}-4\ln\phi_{0}-3\right)\right],

(3-134)

where the nucleon Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
in
d
dimensions is related to the nucleon density
\rho
[
95
]
.

FIG. 54
:
(Color Online). Perturbative calculations of
\Lambda(d_{\mathrm{f}}+\epsilon,C_{0},\phi_{0})
(upper) and the kinetic EOS
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
(lower) for different
d_{\mathrm{f}}
. Only second-order results are shown in the lower panel. Figures from Ref.
[
95
]
.

In the upper panel of FIG.
54
, the values of
\Lambda(d,C_{0},\phi_{0})
from perturbative calculations are shown. The dotted, dash-dotted, and solid lines correspond to zeroth-order, first-order, and second-order approximations, respectively, while the dashed cyan lines represent exact results for each
d_{\mathrm{f}}
. It is clear that the difference between first- and second-order results is smaller than that between zeroth- and first-order approximations (e.g., for
-1\lesssim\epsilon\lesssim 1
), and this difference increases with
d
. This indicates convergence of the
\epsilon
-expansion, even when
\epsilon
takes sizable values like 1 or
-1
. Results starting from
d_{\mathrm{f}}=1,2,3
are all reasonable. For example, starting from
d_{\mathrm{f}}=1
with
\epsilon=1
or
\epsilon=2
gives the
\Lambda
function in 2D or 3D, see the dash-dotted black line in FIG.
54
. In particular,
\Lambda(1+1,C_{0},\phi_{0})\approx 0.728
to linear order and
\Lambda(1+1,C_{0},\phi_{0})\approx 0.683
to quadratic order, while the exact 2D value is 0.713, using
C_{0}\approx 0.161
and
\phi_{0}\approx 2.38
[
91
]
. Starting from
d_{\mathrm{f}}=2
and taking
\epsilon=-1
or
\epsilon=1
(to order
\epsilon^{2}
) gives reasonable approximations for 1D and 3D, see the dash-dotted magenta line. Similarly, starting from
d_{\mathrm{f}}=3
yields reliable results (blue lines). In particular,
d_{\mathrm{f}}=3
and
\epsilon=-1
gives
\Lambda(3-1,C_{0},\phi_{0})\approx 0.607
and
0.754
for linear and quadratic orders, respectively, close to the exact value 0.713. However, starting from
d_{\mathrm{f}}=4
with
\epsilon=-1
does not yield effective results. Overall, perturbations behave well for
1\lesssim d_{\mathrm{f}}\lesssim 3
and
-1\lesssim\epsilon\lesssim 1
.

Next, we consider the Fermi energy
E_{\mathrm{F}}=k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/2M_{\mathrm{N}}
in
d
dimensions. For 2D,
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{2D})}=\sqrt{\pi\rho_{2}}
with
\rho_{2}=\rho^{2/3}
. Taking
\rho\approx 0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, we have
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{2D})}\approx 189.88\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
E_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{2D})}\approx 19.20\,\mathrm{MeV}
, giving
E_{0,(\mathrm{2D})}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx E_{\mathrm{F}}^{(2\mathrm{D})}\Lambda(2,C_{0},\phi_{0})\approx 13.69\,\mathrm{MeV}
. Starting from 1D,
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{1D})}=\pi\rho_{1}/4\approx 84.13\,\mathrm{MeV}
with
\rho_{1}=\rho^{1/3}
, and
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{2D})}\approx k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{1D})}(1+\sigma_{1}\epsilon)\approx 212.46\,\mathrm{MeV}
with
\epsilon=1
and
\sigma_{1}\equiv\sigma(1)\approx 1.5253
(see Eq. (
3.4
)), giving
E_{0,(\mathrm{2D})}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 17.50\,\mathrm{MeV}
using
\Lambda(1+\epsilon,C_{0},\phi_{0})\approx 0.728
. Starting from 3D,
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{2D})}\approx k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{3D})}(1-\sigma)\approx 200.02\,\mathrm{MeV}
with
\sigma\approx 0.2396
(see Eq. (
3-105
)) and
\Lambda(3-1,C_{0},\phi_{0})\approx 0.607
, giving
E_{0,(\mathrm{2D})}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 12.93\,\mathrm{MeV}
. Hence, both 1D and 3D perturbative expansions are effective, with better accuracy starting from 3D due to smaller
\sigma(d_{\mathrm{f}})
.

As an application of the
\epsilon
-expansion, we estimate the 2D kinetic EOS as
\phi_{0}\to\infty
using the 1D kinetic EOS. The dependence of
\Lambda(d,C_{0},\phi_{0})
on
\phi_{0}
is given in Ref.
[
95
]
. Analytically, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Lambda(d,C_{0},\phi_{0})}{\partial\phi_{0}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{C_{0}d\phi_{0}^{d-3}}{d+2}\frac{1}{d+2}\left(\frac{d}{d+2}\frac{1}{\phi_{0}^{2}}-1\right).

(3-135)

Since
\phi_{0}\geq 1
, this derivative is always positive, indicating
\Lambda(d,C_{0},\phi_{0})
increases with
\phi_{0}
regardless of
d
. The kinetic EOS diverges if
\phi_{0}\to\infty
, except in 1D. The divergence order increases with
d
: logarithmic in 2D, linear in 3D, quadratic in 4D. More generally, for
d\geq 3
, the divergence order is
d-2
, as in Eq. (
3-129
). In 1D, the high-momentum part contributes
8C_{0}/3
(see Eq. (
3-131
).
Taking
\phi_{0}\to\infty
in
E_{0,d_{\mathrm{f}}=2}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
[
95
]
gives to linear order in
\epsilon
:

\displaystyle E_{0,d_{\mathrm{f}}=1,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{1D}),2}(1+2\sigma_{1}\epsilon)}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1+\frac{8}{3}C_{0}+\left(\frac{2}{3}+\frac{16}{3}C_{0}\right)\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle+\sum\mathrm{term\;approaches\;zero\;as\;}\phi_{0}\to\infty.

(3-136)

Consequently,

\displaystyle E_{0,\mathrm{(2D)}}^{\mathrm{kin},\phi_{0}\to\infty}(\rho)\approx

\displaystyle E_{0,d_{\mathrm{f}}=1,\epsilon=1}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{1D}),2}(1+2\sigma_{1})}{6M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{5}{3}+8C_{0}\right)\approx 15.04\,\mathrm{MeV}.

(3-137)

This is slightly larger than the exact
E_{\mathrm{F}}^{(2\mathrm{D})}\Lambda(2,C_{0},\phi_{0})\approx 13.69\,\mathrm{MeV}
with
\phi_{0}\approx 2.38
. The FFG model predicts
E_{0,\mathrm{(2D)}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\approx k_{\mathrm{F}}^{(\mathrm{2D}),2}/4M_{\mathrm{N}}=\pi\rho_{2}/4M_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 9.60\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
Interestingly, applying
\epsilon=2
in Eq. (
3.4
) gives
E_{0,\mathrm{(3D)}}^{\mathrm{kin},\phi_{0}\to\infty}(\rho)\approx 39.97\,\mathrm{MeV}
, close to the 3D value of
\sim 40.45
MeV
[
91
]
with
\phi_{0}\approx 2.38
and
C_{0}\approx 0.161
. Physically,
\phi_{0}
cannot be extended to infinity in 3D because the
k^{-4}
HMT breaks down at high momenta due to higher-order correlations such as three-nucleon interactions
[
221
,
164
,
184
,
186
,
504
,
392
]
. Other forms of HMT in
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}
should then be considered.
These results indicate that the
\epsilon
-expansion from a reference dimension
d_{\mathrm{f}}
(e.g., 1D) is reasonable and effective. However, as the expansion order
n
increases, extrapolation becomes less reliable, since
(1+1)^{n}=2^{n}
can become very large/divergent.

Once the nucleon momentum distribution
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}
is specified, one can study its moments, which characterize various aspects of nucleon motion. A particularly useful quantity is the variance of the nucleon momentum,
\langle k^{2}\rangle-\langle k\rangle^{2}
, which describes the strength of momentum fluctuations. To compare across different dimensions, we define the relative nucleon momentum fluctuation in SNM in
d
dimensions as
[
95
]
:

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{k}(d)\equiv

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\langle k^{2}\rangle-\langle k\rangle^{2}}{\langle k\rangle^{2}}}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle k^{2}\rangle}{\langle k\rangle^{2}}-1},

(3-138)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{k}\rangle=

\displaystyle\left.\int n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}\mathbf{k}\mathrm{d}^{d}\mathbf{k}\right/\int n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}\mathrm{d}^{d}\mathbf{k}.

(3-139)

For our analysis, only
\gamma=1
and
\gamma=2
are relevant:

\displaystyle\frac{\langle k\rangle}{k_{\mathrm{F}}}=\frac{d}{d+1}

\displaystyle\times\left[1+C_{0}\left(\frac{4}{(d-3)(d-4)}+\frac{d+1}{d-3}\phi_{0}^{d-3}-\frac{d}{d-4}\phi_{0}^{d-4}\right)\right],

(3-140)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle k^{2}\rangle}{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}=\frac{d}{d+2}

\displaystyle\times\left[1+C_{0}\left(\frac{8}{(d-2)(d-4)}+\frac{d+2}{d-2}\phi_{0}^{d-2}-\frac{d}{d-4}\phi_{0}^{d-4}\right)\right].

(3-141)

For example, we have in 3D:

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{k}^{\mathrm{HMT}}(3)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{16\phi_{0}}{15}\frac{5C_{0}\phi_{0}^{2}-8C_{0}\phi_{0}+3C_{0}+\phi_{0}}{4C_{0}\phi_{0}\ln\phi_{0}-3C_{0}\phi_{0}+3C_{0}+\phi_{0}}-1}

\displaystyle\to\Upsilon_{k}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(3)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{1}{15}}\text{ as }\phi_{0}\to 1.

(3-142)

For the FFG model, one has

\Upsilon_{k}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(d)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d^{2}+2d}},

(3-143)

with, for example,
\Upsilon_{k}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(3)\approx 0.258
and
\Upsilon_{k}^{\mathrm{FFG}}(2)\approx 0.354
.

FIG. 55
:
(Color Online). Fluctuation of the momentum
\Upsilon_{k}
at saturation density
\rho_{0}
as a function of
d
. Figure taken from Ref.
[
95
]
.

FIG.
55
presents the first and second moments of the momentum
\mathbf{k}
as functions of the spatial dimension
d
(upper panel), along with the relative momentum fluctuation
\Upsilon_{k}
(lower panel), for both the FFG and HMT models. From the figure, one observes: (a) as
d
decreases, both the first and second moments of
k
diminish, while the relative fluctuation
\Upsilon_{k}
increases. This indicates that nucleonic matter in lower dimensions exhibits larger relative momentum fluctuations compared to higher-dimensional systems, for both the FFG and HMT cases; (b) when the SRC-induced HMT is included, the fluctuations at a given
d
are enhanced, showing that interactions increase momentum fluctuations by allowing nucleons to occupy high-momentum states above the Fermi surface. In the FFG model, the moments
\langle k\rangle^{1/\gamma}
are constrained by the Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
, as nucleons cannot exceed the Fermi surface. The stronger fluctuation enhancement in low dimensions due to interactions is consistent with the Mermin–Wagner theorem
[
325
]
, which states that continuous symmetries cannot be spontaneously broken at finite temperatures in systems with short-range interactions for
d\leq 2
. Physically, this implies that long-range fluctuations can arise with relatively low energy cost. Furthermore, the fluctuation
\Upsilon_{k}
in low dimensions approaches its FFG value, although both remain quantitatively large.

By expanding the kinetic EOS of ANM and extracting the coefficient of the
\delta^{2}
term, we obtain the kinetic symmetry energy; similarly, the expressions for the fourth-order kinetic symmetry energy can also be derived. Analytical expressions for both the kinetic symmetry energy and the fourth-order kinetic symmetry energy in general
d
dimensions are available
[
95
]
.
In the limit of high-momentum cutoff parameters
\phi_{0}=1
and
\phi_{1}=0
, all kinetic symmetry energies reduce to the FFG predictions, e.g.,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)={k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}/{2dM_{\mathrm{N}}}
, see Eq. (
3-102
). Moreover, in dimensions
d=1
and
d=2
,
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)
vanishes, consistent with the FFG expression Eq. (
3-103
).

FIG. 56
:
(Color Online). Kinetic EOS of ANM in different dimensions. Figures taken from Ref.
[
95
]
.

FIG. 57
:
(Color Online). Kinetic EOS of PNM to order
\delta^{6}
in the FFG/
\mathrm{HMT}
model. Figure taken from Ref.
[
95
]
.

FIG. 58
:
(Color Online). Kinetic EOS of ANM as a function of
d
at saturation density
\rho_{0}
.
The upper boundary of the red area in the lower panel corresponds to the FFG prediction (to order
\delta^{6}
), while the blue dash line shows the exact kinetic EOS of PNM from the FFG model. Figures taken from Ref.
[
95
]
.

For comparison, the FFG model predicts the kinetic EOSs as
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=dk_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/(d+2)
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/2dM_{\mathrm{N}}
as well as
E_{\mathrm{sym,4}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=(2/d-1)(2/d-2)k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/24dM_{\mathrm{N}}
and
E_{\mathrm{sym},6}^{\mathrm{kin},d}(\rho)=(3d-2)(d-1)(d-2)(2d-1)k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/180d^{5}M_{\mathrm{N}}
, yielding numerically
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin},d}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 36.20\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin},d}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 13.57\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}^{\mathrm{kin},d}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 0.85\,\mathrm{MeV}
, and
E_{\mathrm{sym},6}^{\mathrm{kin},d}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 0.12\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
1\leq d\leq 4
. FIG.
56
compares the SRC-induced
\mathrm{HMT}
kinetic EOS components with the FFG predictions. For
d=1
and
d=2
, all
\mathrm{HMT}
components are very close to FFG results, with the fourth- and sixth-order symmetry energies exactly zero in the FFG model and remaining nearly identical in the
\mathrm{HMT}
model. At higher dimensions, the
\mathrm{HMT}
effects become more significant. FIG.
57
shows the PNM kinetic EOS to order
\delta^{6}
in FFG and
\mathrm{HMT}
models, indicating minimal differences for
d\lesssim 3
due to the low HMT fraction in PNM
[
91
]
.
Treating
d
as a continuous variable allows us to plot the different-order terms of the kinetic EOS versus
d
, as shown in the upper panel of FIG.
58
. The red segment in each shaded area includes the SRC-induced
\mathrm{HMT}
. The fourth- and sixth-order kinetic symmetry energies are smaller than the SNM kinetic EOS and the quadratic kinetic symmetry energy, with
E_{\mathrm{sym},4}^{\mathrm{kin},d}(\rho_{0})
peaking around
d\approx 4
(see also the third panel of FIG.
56
). The SNM kinetic EOS, quadratic, fourth-, and sixth-order symmetry energies are all analytical in
d
as
d\to 0
, and are continuous at
d=2
and/or
d=4
, showing that the apparent singularities in the general expressions are artificial. For
d\lesssim 2
, all kinetic EOSs deviate only slightly from the FFG predictions, consistent with the relative momentum fluctuations
\Upsilon_{k}
(lower panel of FIG.
55
).
The lower panel of FIG.
58
shows the corresponding PNM kinetic EOS results. The blue dash line and magenta long dash line represent the exact FFG and
\mathrm{HMT}
kinetic EOS, respectively. The red region indicates the approximation to order
\delta^{6}
, with upper and lower boundaries corresponding to FFG and
\mathrm{HMT}
. The kinetic EOS of PNM is weakly affected by the
\mathrm{HMT}
for
d\lesssim 3
, reflecting the small HMT fraction in PNM.

Examining several limiting cases, we first note that in 3D the exact FFG model gives

E_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})=2^{1/3}\cdot 3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/10M_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 35.1\,\mathrm{MeV}
. As the dimension
d
approaches zero, the kinetic energy behaves as

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\to

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\frac{d}{d+2}\cdot 2^{1+2/d}\left[1+C_{\mathrm{n}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{n}}^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\frac{d}{d+2}\left(2+\frac{4\ln 2}{d}\right)\left[1+C_{\mathrm{n}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{n}}^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\ln 2}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[1+C_{\mathrm{n}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{n}}^{2}}\right)\right]\to 0,

(3-144)

since
k_{\mathrm{F}}\approx\rho_{d}2^{1-2/d}\sqrt{\pi}\to 0
as
d\to 0
, with
C_{\mathrm{n}}=C_{0}(1+C_{1})
and
\phi_{\mathrm{n}}=\phi_{0}(1+\phi_{1})
. In four dimensions, the exact
\mathrm{HMT}
prediction gives
E_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 51.8\,\mathrm{MeV}
, while the
\delta^{6}
approximation yields
48.5\,\mathrm{MeV}
, indicating that higher-order kinetic symmetry energies remain significant at higher dimensions. In one dimension, taking the high-momentum cutoff
\phi_{0}\to\infty
introduces an additional
\mathrm{HMT}
contribution to the kinetic symmetry energy, given by
k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/2M_{\mathrm{N}}\cdot[8C_{0}(1+C_{1})/3]
[
95
]
, which is positive since
1+C_{1}>0
. The corresponding contribution to the fourth-order symmetry energy is
4C_{0}C_{1}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/9M_{\mathrm{N}}
[
95
]
, which is generally negative if
C_{1}<0
. When combining the contributions from the SNM, quadratic, and fourth-order symmetry energies, one finds

\Delta E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{n},(\mathrm{1D})}(\rho)=({16k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}/{9M_{\mathrm{N}}})\cdot C_{\mathrm{n}},~~\mathrm{as}~~\phi_{0}\to\infty.

(3-145)

This then gives the 1D PNM EOS as
E_{\mathrm{n}}(\rho)=2k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/3M_{\mathrm{N}}+({16k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}/{9M_{\mathrm{N}}})\cdot C_{\mathrm{n}}
, since the sixth- and higher-order symmetry energies vanish in this limit. Numerically, using the conventional 3D saturation density
\rho_{0}\approx 0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, one obtains
E_{\mathrm{n}}(\rho_{0})\approx 5.03\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
\Delta E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{\mathrm{n},(\mathrm{1D})}(\rho_{0})\approx 1.62\,\mathrm{MeV}
, which corresponds to an effective 1D density of
\rho_{0}^{1/3}\approx 0.54\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
.

In this subsection, we study the kinetic EOS of ANM in
d
dimensions, taking into account SRC-induced HMT effects. Complementing conventional approaches, we employ the
\epsilon
-expansion, treating the spatial dimension
d
as a continuous variable and introducing
\epsilon=d-d_{\mathrm{f}}
as a small perturbative parameter. Historically developed for critical phenomena in the 1970s
[
479
,
315
,
453
]
, the
\epsilon
-expansion has also been successfully applied in modern contexts, such as the EOS of a unitary Fermi gas, accurately predicting Bertsch’s parameter about 0.367 in agreement with experiment
[
342
,
31
,
264
,
202
,
60
]
. Applying this method to nuclear matter, we demonstrate that starting from the conventional 3D EOS, one can reasonably approximate the kinetic EOS of both symmetric and pure neutron matter in lower dimensions, including 2D and 1D, with higher-order
\epsilon
-corrections to the symmetry energy generally small. The analysis shows that in reduced dimensions, the nucleon specific energy and pressure decrease, the symmetry energy is softened, and the system behaves increasingly like a FFG, especially when SRC-induced HMTs are included, with predictions from the FFG and HMT models converging for
d\lesssim 3
. Moreover, the parabolic approximation remains valid across dimensions, and the conventional 3D EOS already encapsulates substantial information relevant for near-3D systems. While this subsection is exploratory in nature, it highlights the utility of the
\epsilon
-expansion in bridging knowledge from 3D to low-dimensional nuclear matter and provides analytical formulas for nucleon-specific energy, pressure, incompressibility, skewness, symmetry energy, and higher-order symmetry coefficients in general
d
D. Many open questions remain, including the systematic incorporation of nucleon-nucleon interactions in
d
D, the precise role of momentum-dependent potentials on the symmetry energy, and potential applications of low-dimensional EOSs in astrophysical contexts such as NS crusts or sub-systems in heavy-ion collisions
[
293
,
294
,
138
,
213
,
279
,
136
,
428
,
515
,
372
,
329
]
.

There is currently no systematic study on the EOS of ANM in a general dimension
d
, partially due to experimental limitations, theoretical challenges, or lack of interest. While our work presented here is exploratory, it demonstrates the potential usefulness of low-dimensional analyses. Important future directions include the self-consistent incorporation of nucleon-nucleon interactions in
d
D, development of appropriate principles or symmetry constraints, and exploration of the HVH theorem in the presence of HMTs, where the chemical potential is no longer trivially related to the single-nucleon energy
[
5
]
. Given the close similarity between low-dimensional systems and FFG behavior, higher-order
\epsilon
-expansions may help extract information relevant for the 3D EOS, including correlations between the symmetry energy and its slope parameter. It would also be interesting to explore relativistic effects in combination with nucleon interactions in
d
D and to clarify the momentum dependence of symmetry potentials. Finally, potential applications of low-dimensional EOSs are numerous and exciting, such as modeling the quasi-2D crust or quasi-1D core of NSs and certain sub-systems in heavy-ion collisions. These applications may require modifications to conventional equations, such as the Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation, to account for dimensional effects. Overall, the
\epsilon
-expansion provides a practical framework for connecting 3D nuclear matter properties to low-dimensional systems, offering insights into both theoretical structure and potential astrophysical applications, and we anticipate it will stimulate further theoretical and phenomenological investigations.

3.5 Estimate on the Kinetic EOS due to an Extra HMT of the form
k^{-\sigma}
with
\sigma>4

In Subsection
2.2
, we discussed that the HMT in nuclear systems may deviate from the
k^{-4}
form
[
27
]
, as also illustrated in FIG.
25
and the related discussion around Eq. (
2.2
). In this subsection, we investigate this issue by assuming that the single-nucleon momentum distribution in SNM takes the form
*3
*3

*3

For simplicity, we focus only on SNM in this subsection and denote the distribution as
n_{\mathbf{k}}
, which corresponds to
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}
in general. Similarly,
\Delta\leftrightarrow\Delta_{0}
,
C\leftrightarrow C_{0}
,
D\leftrightarrow D_{0}
and
\phi\leftrightarrow\phi_{0}
.

n_{\mathbf{k}}=\begin{cases}\Delta,&k\leq k_{\mathrm{F}},\\
C\left(k_{\mathrm{F}}/k\right)^{4},&k_{\mathrm{F}}\leq k\leq\phi k_{\mathrm{F}},\\
D\left(k_{\mathrm{F}}/k\right)^{\sigma},&k\geq\phi k_{\mathrm{F}}.\end{cases}

(3-146)

As previously noted, the standard
k^{-4}
behavior of the HMT does not necessarily persist at very high momenta (e.g.,
k\gtrsim 2.5k_{\mathrm{F}}
), where the distribution may instead follow a modified trend. Choosing
\sigma=6
provides an effective parametrization that incorporates more intricate nucleon-nucleon interactions
[
328
,
27
]
, potentially including contributions from three-body correlations.
Here
\sigma\geq 6
is taken as an integer, and according to Eq. (
2-23
), only even values of
\sigma
appear
[
328
]
. In the analysis below, we employ the conventional contact parameter
C
with its established value
0.16\pm 0.015
, while the parameter
D
represents the contribution from the higher-order contact term.

In the presence of the
D
-term contribution, the high-momentum fraction in SNM takes the form

x_{\mathrm{HMT}}=3C\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi}\right)+\frac{3D}{\sigma-3}\phi^{3-\sigma},

(3-147)

and it satisfies
\Delta+x_{\mathrm{HMT}}=1
. Inverting this relation gives the expression for the coefficient
D
in terms of
C
,
\phi
and
\sigma
:

D=\frac{1}{3}\frac{\sigma-3}{\phi^{3-\sigma}}\left[x_{\mathrm{HMT}}-3C\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi}\right)\right].

(3-148)

While the high-momentum cutoff
\phi
is in principle a free parameter, one can constrain its physically reasonable range. Since the coefficient
D
, which characterizes the part of the HMT beyond Tan’s
k^{-4}
form
[
424
,
426
,
425
]
, should naturally be positive, a first constraint arises,

\phi\leq\phi_{\max}\equiv\left(1-\frac{x_{\mathrm{HMT}}}{3C}\right)^{-1},

(3-149)

assuming
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}/3C<1
. For instance, taking
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%
and
C\approx 0.16
gives
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}/3C\approx 0.583
and hence
\phi_{\max}\approx 2.4
, the value used in the preceding subsections. At the transition momentum
k=\phi k_{\mathrm{F}}
, the momentum distribution takes the two values
C/\phi^{4}
and
D/\phi^{\sigma}
from the two branches below and above
\phi k_{\mathrm{F}}
. Requiring that
n_{\mathbf{k}}
decrease with
k
leads to
C/\phi^{4}\geq D/\phi^{\sigma}
, which provides a second constraint,

\phi\geq\phi_{\min}\equiv\frac{\sigma-4}{\sigma-3}\cdot\left(1-\frac{x_{\mathrm{HMT}}}{3C}\right)^{-1}=\frac{\sigma-4}{\sigma-3}\cdot\phi_{\max}.

(3-150)

Together, (
3-149
) and (
3-150
) restrict the cutoff to an effective range
\phi_{\min}\leq\phi\leq\phi_{\max}
, with
\delta\phi\equiv\phi_{\max}-\phi_{\min}=\phi_{\max}/(\sigma-3)
.

FIG. 59
:
(Color Online). Momentum distribution
n_{\mathbf{k}}^{0}
with two different values of
\sigma
,
C\approx 0.16
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%
are fixed.

The upper bound
\phi_{\max}
is independent of
\sigma
, whereas the lower bound
\phi_{\min}
depends on it. For example,
\sigma=6
gives
2/3\leq\phi/\phi_{\max}\leq 1
, and
\sigma=8
gives
4/5\leq\phi/\phi_{\max}\leq 1
. As
\sigma
increases, the allowed interval for
\phi
narrows as
\phi_{\max}/(\sigma-3)
. FIG.
59
illustrates the single-nucleon momentum distribution for two values of
\sigma
, using
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%
,
C\approx 0.16
[
91
]
, and a representative
\phi=2.2
for demonstration. Here the normalization strength at the Fermi surface is
Z=\Delta-C
, inversely proportional to the effective mass
[
327
]
, and the discontinuity at
k=\phi k_{\mathrm{F}}
is
Y=C/\phi^{4}-D/\phi^{\sigma}
, which we required to be non-negative when deriving
\phi_{\min}
. With fixed
\sigma
, the coefficient
D
increases monotonically as
\phi
decreases from
\phi_{\max}
to
\phi_{\min}
as shown by Eq. (
3-148
); for fixed
\phi
,
D
is larger for larger
\sigma
. For example,
1.6\lesssim\phi\lesssim 2.4
for
\sigma=6
, and
1.92\lesssim\phi\lesssim 2.4
for
\sigma=8
(using
C\approx 0.16
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%
). The high-momentum fraction contributed specifically by the
D
-term is

x_{\mathrm{HMT}}^{(D)}=1-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}^{(C)}=1-3C\left(1-\phi^{-1}\right),

(3-151)

which does not depend on
\sigma
.
The kinetic EOS of SNM is

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{4}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}n_{\mathbf{k}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{5}\frac{k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\Bigg[1-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}+5C(\phi-1)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\frac{5\phi^{2}}{3}\frac{\sigma-3}{\sigma-5}\left[x_{\mathrm{HMT}}-3C\left(1-\frac{1}{\phi}\right)\right]\Bigg],

(3-152)

which becomes divergent as
\phi\to\infty
if
\sigma\leq 5
. This expression is used at densities around
\rho\approx\rho_{0}\approx 0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, and its dependence on
\phi
is non-trivial, with the allowable range of
\phi
itself depending on
\sigma
.
The lower cutoff
\phi_{\min}
given in Eq. (
3-150
) can also be obtained directly from the kinetic EOS. Since (
3-148
) shows that
D
increases as
\phi
decreases, the kinetic EOS also increases accordingly. The kinetic EOS therefore reaches its maximum when the
D
-branch of
n_{\mathbf{k}}
becomes linked to the
C
-branch, corresponding to the condition

\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\right|_{\phi=\phi_{\min}}=0.

(3-153)

This leads to
\phi_{\min}=[(\sigma-4)/(\sigma-3)]\cdot\phi_{\max}
, see Eq. (
3-150
), and for
\sigma\geq 6
one has

\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\phi^{2}}E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\right|_{\phi=\phi_{\min}}=-\frac{3\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{M_{\mathrm{N}}}\frac{C}{\phi_{\max}}\frac{\sigma-3}{\sigma-5}<0,

(3-154)

with
\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}=k_{\mathrm{F}}(\rho_{0})
, confirming that
\phi_{\min}
indeed corresponds to the maximum of
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
. In addition to the representative
\sigma=6
case, we also consider
\sigma=8
, which has appeared in an earlier work
[
26
]
.

FIG. 60
:
(Color Online). Kinetic EOS of SNM at
\rho_{0}
as a function of
\phi
corresponding to FIG.
59
,
C\approx 0.16,x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%
are fixed.

The dependence of
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
on the high-momentum fraction
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
is linear,

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\mathrm{HMT}}}E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})=\frac{3}{5}\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(-1+\frac{5\phi^{2}}{3}\frac{\sigma-3}{\sigma-5}\right).

(3-155)

The negative “
-1
” term shows that increasing
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
reduces the contribution from the zero-momentum depletion (
\Delta=1-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
), while the positive term
5\phi^{2}/3\cdot(\sigma-3)/(\sigma-5)
shows that the
k^{-\sigma}
tail increases the kinetic energy. The part from the conventional
k^{-4}
tail characterized by
5C(\phi-1)
is independent of
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
. The net effect is positive, i.e.,
\partial E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})/\partial x_{\mathrm{HMT}}>0
, since typically
\phi^{2}\gtrsim 2
and
(\sigma-3)/(\sigma-5)>1
for
\sigma\geq 6
. A smaller
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
gives a smaller
\phi_{\max}
, e.g.,
\phi_{\max}=2
for
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 24\%
with
C\approx 0.16
, which implies
\phi_{\min}\approx 1.33
for
\sigma=6
and
\phi_{\min}=1.6
for
\sigma=8
. The corresponding kinetic EOS values span
26.3\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 67.4\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
24\%\lesssim x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\lesssim 32\%
with
\sigma=6
, and
31.5\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 53.5\,\mathrm{MeV}
with
\sigma=8
. Thus extending
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
from 24% to 32% changes the kinetic EOS by approximately
41.1\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
\sigma=6
or
22.0\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
\sigma=8
. Although
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
at
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 24\%
is larger for
\sigma=8
, the slope
\partial E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})/\partial x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
is larger for
\sigma=6
.
For fixed
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
and
\phi
, the kinetic EOS decreases as
\sigma
increases, which follows from the factor
(\sigma-3)/(\sigma-5)
in Eq. (
3.5
). Its limit as
\sigma\to\infty
is 1, and its maximum for
\sigma\geq 6
is 3 at
\sigma=6
. In the limit
\sigma\to\infty
, one has
\phi_{\min}\to\phi_{\max}=1/(1-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}/3C)
and
\delta\phi\to 0
, so
\phi
is fixed to
\phi_{\max}
and the contribution from the extra HMT vanishes. These features appear in FIG.
60
, where for example
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 46.9\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
\sigma=6
with
\phi=2.2
. The corresponding ranges are
40.7\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\lesssim 54.8\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
\sigma=6
and
40.7\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho)\lesssim 43.5\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
\sigma=8
, where the lower value
40.7\,\mathrm{MeV}
corresponds to the pure
k^{-4}
tail. The extra HMT always enhances the kinetic EOS because it allows integration to arbitrarily large momenta without divergence.

FIG. 61
:
(Color Online). Effective contribution
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}(\sigma,C)
to the kinetic EOS from
k^{-\sigma}
tail as a function of
\sigma
,
C\approx 0.16\pm 0.015,x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%\pm 4\%
.

An important quantity is the correction on the
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
due to the extra HMT of the form
k^{-\sigma}
, compared with the
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
adopting only the conventional
k^{-4}
HMT.
The effective contribution
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)\equiv E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0},\phi_{\min})-E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0},\phi_{\max})
could be written explicitly as,

\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma-5}-\frac{1}{\sigma-3}\right)\frac{9C^{2}}{3C-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}}\frac{\overline{k}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}.

(3-156)

Consequently, the effective contribution from the
k^{-\sigma}
is about
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)=54.8\,\mathrm{MeV}-40.7\,\mathrm{MeV}\approx 14.1\,\mathrm{MeV}
, see the upper panel of FIG.
60
(indicated by the light-blue arrows), since as the high momentum cutoff
\phi
varies the corresponding decomposition of the kinetic EOS also varies.
Similarly, the effective contribution from the
k^{-\sigma}
tail with
\sigma=8
is about
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)=43.5\,\mathrm{MeV}-40.7\,\mathrm{MeV}\approx 2.8\,\mathrm{MeV}
, see the lower panel of FIG.
60
. Naturally one has
\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)=0\,\mathrm{MeV}
, see FIG.
61
.
Considering the range spanned by
C
and that by
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
, one can find that the contribution
14.1\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
\sigma=6
is not the maximum.
In order to find the maximum effects from the extra HMT on the
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
, we notice the derivatives,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\mathrm{HMT}}}\left(\frac{C^{2}}{3C-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{C^{2}}{(3C-x_{\mathrm{HMT}})^{2}},

(3-157)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C}\left(\frac{C^{2}}{3C-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{2(3C-2x_{\mathrm{HMT}})}{(3C-x_{\mathrm{HMT}})^{2}},

(3-158)

where
C^{2}/(3C-x_{\mathrm{HMT}})
is the relevant factor appearing in
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)
.
The first relation tells that
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 32\%
should be taken for maximizing
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)
, while the second relation indicates that the derivative is negative as
3C-0.64<0
for
0.145\leq C\leq 0.175
[
91
]
. Consequently
C\approx 0.145
should be adopted for maximizing
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)
.
Thus, by taking
C\approx 0.145
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 32\%
, one obtains
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{6}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)\lesssim\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{6}(0.32,0.145)\approx 20.2\,\mathrm{MeV}
with
59.6\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 79.8\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
2.52\lesssim\phi\lesssim 3.78
.
By comparing with the
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\approx 59.6\,\mathrm{MeV}
only adopting the
k^{-4}
HMT, the enhancement is about
20.2/59.6\approx 33.9\%
(which is not the maximum relative enhancement).
Similarly, we have for
\sigma=8
that
59.6\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 63.7\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
3.03\lesssim\phi\lesssim 3.78
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{8}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)\lesssim\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(0.32,0.145)\approx 4.1\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
See FIG.
61
for the uncertainties on the
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)
, i.e.,
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)_{-\mathrm{down}}^{+\mathrm{up}}
, e.g., we have
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{6}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)\approx 14.1_{-2.2}^{+6.1}\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{8}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},C)\approx 2.8_{-0.4}^{+1.3}\,\mathrm{MeV}
.

FIG. 62
:
(Color Online). Relative enhancement
R_{\sigma}
as a function of
\sigma
,
0.145\lesssim C\lesssim 0.175,24\%\lesssim x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\lesssim 32\%
.

Similarly, in order to analyze the relative contribution of the extra HMT (compared with the kinetic EOS only with the conventional HMT of the form
k^{-4}
), one needs to consider the ratio between the
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin},(D)}(\rho_{0})
and the kinetic EOS with only the HMT of the form
k^{-4}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle R_{\sigma}\equiv

\displaystyle{E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin},(D)}(\rho_{0})}\left/\left[\frac{3k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{10M_{\mathrm{N}}}\cdot\left(1-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}+5C(\phi_{\max}-1)\right)\right]\right.

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\frac{C^{2}}{3C-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}}\frac{1}{1-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}+5C(\phi_{\max}-1)},

(3-159)

where
\phi_{\max}=3C/(3C-x_{\mathrm{HMT}})
.
Straightforward calculations give
\partial R_{\sigma}/\partial C>0
and
\partial R_{\sigma}/\partial x_{\mathrm{HMT}}>0
for
0.145\lesssim C\lesssim 0.175
and
24\%\lesssim x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\lesssim 32\%
, indicating in order to obtain the maximum value of
R_{\sigma}
, one needs to take
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 32\%
as well as
C\approx 0.175
.
The corresponding results are
R_{\sigma}^{\max}\approx 36.5\%
with
45.2\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 61.7\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},0.175)\approx 16.5\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
\sigma=6
, and
R_{\sigma}^{\max}\approx 7.3\%
with
45.2\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})\lesssim 48.5\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},0.175)\approx 3.3\,\mathrm{MeV}
for
\sigma=8
.
It is obvious that the absolute value of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},0.175)
is smaller than that of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\sigma}(x_{\mathrm{HMT}},0.145)
, but the relative contribution is maximized.
The reason is simple: when
C
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
vary, the
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
adopting only the
k^{-4}
HMT may also change correspondingly.
FIG.
62
shows the relative enhancement
R_{\sigma}
as a function of
\sigma
of the extra HMT in the form
k^{-\sigma}
, here
R_{\sigma}^{\max}
is the
R_{\sigma}
under
C\approx 0.175
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 32\%
while
R_{\sigma}^{\min}
is the
R_{\sigma}
under
C\approx 0.145
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 24\%
.
Numerically, one has
32.6\%\lesssim R_{\sigma}\lesssim 36.5\%
for
\sigma=6
and
6.5\%\lesssim R_{\sigma}\lesssim 7.3\%
for
\sigma=8
.
The results for
\sigma=10
and
\sigma=12
on
R_{\sigma}
are also shown in FIG.
62
.
Similarly the maximum absolute contribution for
\sigma=10
and
\sigma=12
could also be obtained, and they are 1.7 MeV and 1.0 MeV, respectively.
As discussed in Subsection
2.2
, the maximum high momentum cutoff
\phi_{\max}
for the
k^{-4}
form of the contact may physically be less then 2.
If
\phi_{\max}\approx 1.5
, then
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}/C\approx 1
since
\phi_{\max}=1/(1-x_{\mathrm{HMT}}/3C)
. If one artificially adopts
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%\pm 4\%
and the approximated relation
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}/C\approx 1
, then
44.1\%\lesssim R_{\sigma}\lesssim 54.1\%
for
\sigma=6
while
8.8\%\lesssim R_{\sigma}\lesssim 10.8\%
for
\sigma=8
, both are sizable. The contribution from the
k^{-6}
HMT is even comparable to that from the conventional
k^{-4}
HMT in this case. On the other hand, if a smaller
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
is adopted, e.g., the one from the self-consistent Green’s function theories, about
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 10\%\pm 5\%
(together with
\phi_{\max}\approx 1.5
), the
R_{\sigma}
for
\sigma=6
is found to be about
11.6\%\lesssim R_{\sigma}\lesssim 30.6\%
, and
2.3\%\lesssim R_{\sigma}\lesssim 6.1\%
for
\sigma=8
.

FIG. 63
:
(Color Online). Comparison on the average kinetic energy for finite nuclei with experiments (cyan solid circles) and those from VMC calculations (red diamonds), where
C\approx 0.16
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%
are used at
\rho_{0}\approx 0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
,
\rho_{A}/\rho_{0}=(1+\alpha/\beta A^{1/3})^{-1}
,
\phi_{\min}
is adopted for
k^{-6}
or
k^{-8}
HMTs.

FIG. 64
:
(Color Online). Same as FIG.
63
but consider the uncertainties on
C
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
, i.e.,
C\approx 0.16\pm 0.015
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%\pm 4\%
.

In order to further study the extra HMT of the form
k^{-\sigma}
, we compare the predictions on the kinetic energy of finite nuclei with those extracted from experiments, including
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
,
{}^{27}\mathrm{Al}
,
{}^{56}\mathrm{Fe}
and
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
[
219
]
as well as from VMC calculations
[
481
]
, including
{}^{7}\mathrm{Li}
,
{}^{9}\mathrm{Be}
and
{}^{11}\mathrm{B}
.
The average nucleon kinetic energy in finite nucleus is obtained as
\langle T\rangle=[\langle T_{\mathrm{n}}\rangle(1+\delta)+\langle T_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle(1-\delta)]/2
, where
\langle T_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle
and
\langle T_{\mathrm{n}}\rangle
are the average kinetic energies for protons and neutrons
[
219
]
.
For finite nucleus, the isospin asymmetry
\delta
is generally small, this means one can approximate the kinetic EOS
E_{\mathrm{ANM}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{A})\approx E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{A})+\delta^{2}E_{\mathrm{sym}}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{A})
simply as
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{A})
, here the (average-finite-nucleus) density
\rho_{A}
could be obtained from
\rho_{0}
via
\rho_{A}/\rho_{0}\approx(1+\alpha/A^{1/3})^{-1}
with
\alpha/\beta\approx 2.8
[
93
]
, see Subsection
3.3
for details on these quantities.
The comparison is shown in FIG.
63
where the central values for
C
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
, i.e.,
C\approx 0.16
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%
are adopted at
\rho_{0}\approx 0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, and the discrete points are shown for
\langle T\rangle
.
From the figure one can find that the kinetic EOS with HMT in the form of
k^{-4}
alone slightly under-estimates the experimental data
[
93
]
, and in addition, the extra HMT taking the form of
k^{-6}
performs better than the HMT with the form
k^{-8}
.
If one considers the uncertainties on the parameters
C
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}
, i.e.,
C\approx 0.16\pm 0.015
and
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%\pm 4\%
, the results are shown in FIG.
64
.
Finally, even if a small value for
\alpha/\beta\approx 2.0
is adopted
[
140
]
, the prediction from the model including the extra HMT with form of
k^{-6}
or
k^{-8}
performs better than the model with the conventional
k^{-4}
HMT alone.

Finally, we emphasize that the treatment of
n_{\mathbf{k}}
in Eq. (
3-146
) is merely a pragmatic approximation, since the momentum dependence of the distribution at large
k
is very complicated. Nevertheless, it provides a useful estimate of how the HMT
k^{-\sigma}
(with
\sigma>4
) affects the kinetic energy in finite nuclei
[
219
]
; in particular, such a tail avoids the divergence of
E^{\mathrm{kin}}_{0}(\rho)
when performing the integration. Very similarly, one may introduce multiple extra high-momentum components into the distribution function, namely

n_{\mathbf{k}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta,&k\leq k_{\mathrm{F}},\\
C(k_{\mathrm{F}}/k)^{4},&k_{\mathrm{F}}\leq k\leq\phi k_{\mathrm{F}},\\
D_{1}(k_{\mathrm{F}}/k)^{\sigma_{1}},&\phi k_{\mathrm{F}}\leq k\leq\phi_{1}k_{\mathrm{F}},\\
D_{2}(k_{\mathrm{F}}/k)^{\sigma_{2}},&\phi_{1}k_{\mathrm{F}}\leq k\leq\phi_{2}k_{\mathrm{F}},\\
\cdots,&\end{array}\right.

(3-160)

with the contact coefficients
D_{1},D_{2},\cdots
and cutoffs
\phi,\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\cdots
, where typically
\sigma_{1}=6
,
\sigma_{2}=8
,
\cdots
. The resulting contribution to the kinetic EOS from Eq. (
3-160
) is smaller than that obtained by adopting only a single
\sigma=6
tail, but larger than that from a single
\sigma=8
tail. In particular, we find that the contribution from the
k^{-6}
HMT can be as large as about
20.2\,\mathrm{MeV}
, while that from the
k^{-8}
HMT can reach about
4.1\,\mathrm{MeV}
, based on the empirical
x_{\mathrm{HMT}}\approx 28\%\pm 4\%
and
C\approx 0.16\pm 0.015
. Thus, maximally the correction due to the
k^{-\sigma}
with
6\leq\sigma\leq 8
on the
E_{0}^{\mathrm{kin}}(\rho_{0})
is about
4.1\sim 20.2\,\mathrm{MeV}
.

§4 SRC-HMT Impacts on Heavy-ion Reactions or Scattering Experiments

In this section, we discuss the effects of SRC and HMT on various phenomena in low- and intermediate-energy heavy-ion collisions. In Subsection
4.1
, we examine the coexistence of a proton-skin in momentum space and a neutron-skin in coordinate space in neutron-rich nuclei, a feature that naturally arises from Liouville’s theorem in statistical physics and the uncertainty principle of quantum mechanics. Subsection
4.2
focuses on several isospin-sensitive observables in heavy-ion reactions, such as yield ratios and particle flows, highlighting the influence of both the proton-skin and neutron-skin. In Subsection
4.3
, we discuss sub-threshold particle production in
\mathrm{p}A
collisions with explicit consideration of SRC-HMT effects. Finally, Subsection
4.4
discusses the determination of the HMT fraction in finite nuclei via neutron-proton bremsstrahlung photon emission in low-energy reactions, offering a novel approach to probe SRC physics in finite nuclear systems.

4.1 Coexistence of Proton-skin in
k
-space and Neutron-skin in
r
-space

As discussed in previous sections, the SRC between a neutron-proton pair is about 18-20 times stronger than that between two protons
[
219
,
422
]
, and the HMT in PNM was therefore estimated to be about 1-2%
[
223
]
. Although the precise size of the HMT remains uncertain, a qualitative consensus has been reached; in the previous sections, we use the HMT-exp and HMT-SCGF parameter sets
[
90
]
to illustrate several relevant issues.
The resulting neutron fractions in the HMT as a function of
\delta
at
\rho_{0}
for the two parameter sets are compared in the right panel of FIG.
65
. The corresponding reduced nucleon momentum distributions (normalized to unity at zero momentum) for
\delta\approx 0.21
and
\delta\approx 0.5
are shown in the left and middle panels, respectively. Clearly, relative to the center of momentum space, nucleonic matter exhibits a distinct proton skin in momentum space, whose thickness grows with the isospin asymmetry
\delta
at a rate that depends on the adopted HMT parameters
[
90
]
.
The neutron skins in coordinate space and proton skins in momentum space coexist in heavy nuclei, and their correlation is governed by Liouville’s theorem and the Heisenberg uncertainty principle
[
90
]
. A careful study of both skins simultaneously may help to better constrain the underlying isovector interaction. In the following discussions in this subsection, we adopt the HMT-exp parameter set unless specified otherwise.

FIG. 65
:
(Color Online) Reduced nucleon momentum distribution of neutron-rich nucleonic matter with an isospin asymmetry of
\delta\approx 0.21
(left) and
\delta\approx 0.50
(middle), and the fraction of neutrons in the HMT as a function of
\delta
using the HMT-exp and HMT-SCGF parameter sets (right). Taken from Ref.
[
90
]
.

An extended Thomas–Fermi method (denoted as
\mathrm{ETF}^{+}
) was adopted in Ref.
[
90
]
. In the original ETF framework, which is a semi-classical approximation to the Hartree-Fock theory, the nucleon kinetic energy density profile in nuclei is given by
[
90
]

\boxed{\varepsilon_{J}^{\mathrm{kin}}(r)=\frac{1}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\left[\alpha_{J}^{\infty}\rho_{J}^{5/3}+\eta_{J}\frac{1}{36}\frac{(\nabla\rho_{J})^{2}}{\rho_{J}}+\frac{1}{3}\Delta\rho_{J}\right],}

(4-1)

obtained by truncating the Wigner–Kirkwood expansion of the Block density matrix at order
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{2}
[
66
,
262
,
100
,
45
]
. Importantly, this relationship is independent of the interactions involved. The first term, originally with
\alpha_{J}^{\infty}=(3/5)(3\pi^{2})^{2/3}
, represents the bulk contribution as if nucleons are in infinite nuclear matter with a step-function momentum distribution. The second term, with
\eta_{J}=1
, was originally proposed by Weizsäcker
[
66
]
and is highly sensitive to surface properties. In practice,
\eta_{J}
is often adjusted to account for higher-order terms and other missing effects, reproducing nuclear binding energies or predictions from more microscopic theories. In the following, we fix
\eta_{J}
by fitting the average kinetic energies of protons and neutrons extracted from SRC experiments at JLab
[
219
]
. The last term, involving a Laplacian, is generally small as nuclear surfaces are typically smooth (so the second-order derivative is small).

With the SRC-modified single-nucleon momentum distribution function of Eq. (
2-8
), the kinetic energy density in infinite matter becomes
[
90
]

\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{\phi_{J}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{J}}\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}n_{\mathbf{k}}^{J}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}=\frac{1}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}\frac{3}{5}(3\pi^{2})^{2/3}\rho_{J}^{5/3}\Phi_{J},

(4-2)

where
\Phi_{J}=1+C_{J}(5\phi_{J}+3/\phi_{J}-8)>1
depends on the properties of the HMT, see Eq. (
3-31
) for the factor in SNM. Consequently, the bulk factor
\alpha_{J}^{\infty}
in Eq. (
4-1
) is enhanced by the SRC factor
\Phi_{J}
, i.e.,
\alpha_{J}^{\infty}=(3/5)(3\pi^{2})^{2/3}\Phi_{J}
. In neutron-rich systems, because more protons are depleted from the Fermi sea to populate the HMT, the bulk kinetic energy density is enhanced more for protons than neutrons. For
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
, we find
\Phi_{\mathrm{p}}\approx 2.09\pm 0.50
and
\Phi_{\mathrm{n}}\approx 1.60\pm 0.33
.
To quantify the proton-skin in momentum space, one defines
[
90
]

\boxed{\Delta E_{\mathrm{pn}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\equiv\langle E_{\mathrm{p}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\rangle-\langle E_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\rangle,\quad\langle E_{J}^{\mathrm{kin}}\rangle=\frac{\int_{0}^{\infty}\varepsilon_{J}^{\mathrm{kin}}(r)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}}{\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{J}(r)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}}\equiv\frac{\langle k_{J}^{2}\rangle}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}},}

(4-3)

where the average kinetic energies are given here.
For the nucleon density profile
\rho_{J}(r)
, we adopt the widely used 2-parameter Fermi (2pF) distribution
[
251
,
90
]
,

\rho_{J}(r)=\rho_{0}^{J}[1+\exp(({r-c_{J})}/{a_{J}})]^{-1}
,
where
c_{J}
and
a_{J}
are the half-density radius and diffuseness parameter, respectively, and
\rho_{0}^{J}
is determined by normalization. Using the experimental average kinetic energy
\langle E_{\mathrm{p}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\rangle\approx 41.9\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
219
]
, one finds
\eta_{\mathrm{p}}\approx 26.7
for
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
. For neutrons, with the constraints
\langle E_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\rangle\approx 34.0\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
219
]
and
\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}\approx 0.159\,\mathrm{fm}
[
137
]
, the unknowns
a_{\mathrm{n}}
,
c_{\mathrm{n}}
, and
\eta_{\mathrm{n}}
yield
\eta_{\mathrm{n}}\approx 7.2\sim 9.2
, with an average of
\eta_{\mathrm{n}}\approx 8.2
[
90
]
.

FIG. 66
:
(Color Online). Upper: density profile and local momentum in
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
under
\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}\approx 0.159\,\mathrm{fm}
[
137
]
and
\Delta E_{\mathrm{pn}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\approx 7.9\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
219
]
. Lower: correlation between neutron-skin in r-space
\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}
and proton-skin in k-space
\Delta E_{\mathrm{pn}}^{\mathrm{kin}}
for
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
within the ETF
+
approach with (HMT) and without (FFG) SRC effects. The black star represents the central cross point of the constraints on both
\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}
and
\Delta E_{\mathrm{pn}}^{\mathrm{kin}}
. Taken from Ref.
[
90
]
where the HMT-exp set is adopted in the calculations.

In the upper panel of FIG.
66
, the density profile and the local momentum defined via
k_{J}^{\mathrm{loc},2}(r)/2M_{\mathrm{N}}=\varepsilon_{J}^{\mathrm{kin}}(r)/\rho_{J}(r)
are shown. Two main features emerge: (a) high-momentum nucleons with
300\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim|\mathbf{k}|\lesssim 600\,\mathrm{MeV}
[
219
]
are mainly distributed near the neutron skin; and (b) in the neutron skin, the local momentum of protons exceeds that of neutrons, implying faster proton motion in this region.
Without the HMT factor
\Phi_{\mathrm{p/n}}
and
\eta_{\mathrm{p/n}}
,
k_{\mathrm{p}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(r)
is smaller than
k_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(r)
in the center and bulk skin region (
R\approx 1.12A^{1/3}\,\mathrm{fm}\approx 6.6\,\mathrm{fm}
), reversing only in the surface skin region. In this region,
k_{\mathrm{p/n}}(r)\approx(1/72M_{\mathrm{N}})(\nabla\rho_{\mathrm{p/n}}/\rho_{\mathrm{p/n}})^{2}\approx 1/(72M_{\mathrm{N}}a_{\mathrm{p/n}}^{2})
, so
k_{\mathrm{p}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(r)>k_{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(r)
for
a_{\mathrm{p}}<a_{\mathrm{n}}
, but this is sensitive to small changes in
a_{\mathrm{p/n}}
.
Using the Skyrme-Hartree-Fock method, FIG.
67
shows the similar average local density and momentum of nucleons in
208
Pb
[
209
]
.
The lower panel of FIG.
66
shows correlations between neutron-skin
\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}
and proton-skin
\Delta E_{\mathrm{pn}}^{\mathrm{kin}}
in
{}^{208}\mathrm{Pb}
using ETF
+
with HMT or FFG, along with SHF predictions. Several observations are noteworthy: (a) SHF and ETF
+
(FFG) predictions fail to satisfy the combined constraints on both skins. Measuring both neutron and proton skins provides a more stringent test of nuclear models; (b)
\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}
and
\Delta E_{\mathrm{pn}}^{\mathrm{kin}}
are approximately linearly correlated. Along the red line satisfying both constraints, a precise measurement of one skin determines the other, assuming ETF
+
(HMT) is correct.

FIG. 67
:
(Color Online).
Upper: input local density (a) and calculated momentum distributions (b) for nucleons in
208
Pb using the Skyrme functional, and for neutrons with neutron skin thicknesses
S=\langle\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}\rangle=0.1
fm and
S=\langle\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}\rangle=0.3
fm. In this case, the nucleon kinetic-energy density of the colliding nuclei includes only the bulk contribution.
Lower: same as the upper panel, but now including the surface term in the nucleon kinetic-energy density of the colliding nuclei.
Figure taken from Ref.
[
209
]
.

Physically, neutron skins in r-space and proton skins in k-space coexist and are intrinsically correlated by Liouville’s theorem and Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle, so precise measurements of either can constrain the other and improve understanding of nuclear surfaces in full phase space.
The normalization condition is
[{2}/{(2\pi)^{3}}]\int f(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}=N
, where
f(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})
is the phase-space distribution function and
N
the total number of particles.
*4
*4

*4

In nuclear systems,
N\leftrightarrow A
, with
A
the total nucleon number.
For a uniform distribution
f(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})=s_{\mathbf{r}}n_{\mathbf{k}}=\Theta(R-|\mathbf{r}|)\Theta(k_{\mathrm{F}}-|\mathbf{k}|)
, one finds
Rk_{\mathrm{F}}=({9\pi N}/{4})^{1/3}
, and similarly

\displaystyle\langle r^{2}\rangle\langle k^{2}\rangle=

\displaystyle\left[\left.{\int_{0}^{R}r^{4}\mathrm{d}r}\right/{\int_{0}^{R}r^{2}\mathrm{d}r}\right]\left[\left.{\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}}k^{4}\mathrm{d}k}\right/{\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{F}}}k^{2}\mathrm{d}k}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9}{25}R^{2}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}=\frac{4}{25\pi}\left(\frac{9\pi}{4}\right)^{5/3}N^{2/3},

(4-4)

and more generally,

\langle r^{\ell}\rangle\langle k^{\ell}\rangle=\left(\frac{3}{\ell+3}\right)^{2}R^{\ell}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ell}.

(4-5)

For general distributions, the normalization conditions for
s_{\mathbf{r}}
and
n_{\mathbf{k}}
are
\int s_{\mathbf{r}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}=4\pi R^{3}/3
and
[{2}/{(2\pi)^{3}}]\int n_{\mathbf{k}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}={k_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}}/{3\pi^{2}}
, which define the sharp radius
R
and Fermi momentum
k_{\mathrm{F}}
. In the HMT case, assuming
s_{\mathbf{r}}
is uniform, we have (for
\ell\neq 1
)

\displaystyle\langle r^{\ell}\rangle\langle k^{\ell}\rangle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{3}{\ell+3}\right)^{2}R^{\ell}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{\ell}\left[1+C_{0}\left(\frac{\ell+3}{\ell-1}\phi_{0}^{\ell-1}+\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}-\frac{4\ell}{\ell-1}\right)\right].

(4-6)

The limit
\ell\to 1
could be obtained by this expression appropriately.
Liouville’s theorem ensures that
Rk_{\mathrm{F}}
is fixed regardless of the forms of
s_{\mathbf{r}}
and
n_{\mathbf{k}}
, but the average
\langle r^{\ell}\rangle\langle k^{\ell}\rangle
depends on
\ell
and the shapes of
s_{\mathbf{r}}
and
n_{\mathbf{k}}
.
For a nucleon in symmetric nuclear matter, Liouville’s theorem reads

\displaystyle\mbox{(spin factor)}\times\frac{\mbox{(r-vol)}\times\mbox{(k-vol)}}{h^{3}}=\#\mbox{ of particle}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle 2\times\left.{\displaystyle\frac{4}{3}\pi\langle r\rangle^{3}\times\frac{4}{3}\pi\langle k\rangle^{3}}\right/{(2\pi\mathord{\mathchar 126h})^{3}}=N,

(4-7)

where
\langle r\rangle
and
\langle k\rangle
are effective radii in r- and k-space, respectively. Therefore,
\langle r\rangle\langle k\rangle\sim\mathrm{const.}
; together with the uncertainty principle
\delta r\delta k\sim\mathrm{const.}
, we obtain

\langle r^{2}\rangle\langle k^{2}\rangle=\langle r\rangle^{2}\langle k\rangle^{2}\left[1+\left(\frac{\delta r}{\langle r\rangle}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\delta k}{\langle k\rangle}\right)^{2}\right]+(\delta r)^{2}(\delta k)^{2}.

(4-8)

If the fluctuations are small,
\delta r\ll\langle r\rangle
and
\delta k\ll\langle k\rangle
, then
\langle r^{2}\rangle\langle k^{2}\rangle\approx\langle r\rangle^{2}\langle k\rangle^{2}+(\delta r)^{2}(\delta k)^{2}
remains nearly constant.
For the 2pF distribution in r-space, we have
\langle r\rangle\approx({3c}/{4})(1+\pi^{2}\chi^{2})
and
\langle r^{2}\rangle\approx({3c^{2}}/{5})(1+{7}\pi^{2}\chi^{2}/3)
where
\chi=a/c
,
so that
{\delta r}/{\langle r\rangle}\approx 0.26(1+8\pi^{2}\chi^{2}/3)
as well as
({\delta r}/{\langle r\rangle})^{2}\approx 0.07(1+16\pi^{2}\chi^{2}/3)
.
For k-space including HMT, we have:

\displaystyle\langle k^{2}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{5}k_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\left[1+C_{0}\left(5\phi_{0}+\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}-8\right)\right],

(4-9)

\displaystyle\langle k\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}k_{\mathrm{F}}\left[1+C_{0}\left(4\ln\phi_{0}+\frac{3}{\phi_{0}}-3\right)\right],

(4-10)

see equations of (
3.4
), numerically giving
\delta k/\langle k\rangle\approx 0.18
.
For ANM, the phase-space distribution
f_{J}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})
of nucleon type
J
is normalized as
[2/(2\pi)^{3}]\int f_{J}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}=N_{J}
, with
N_{\mathrm{n}}=N
and
N_{\mathrm{p}}=Z
. Assuming a uniform distribution,
f_{J}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})=\Theta(R-|\mathbf{r}|)\Theta(K-|\mathbf{k}|)
, we have
H_{J}\equiv\langle r_{J}^{2}\rangle\langle k_{J}^{2}\rangle=(4/25\pi)(9\pi/4)^{5/3}N_{J}^{2/3}
, which depends only on
N_{J}
.
In realistic cases, several parameters in
\rho_{J}(r)
, such as
\Lambda_{\mathrm{av}}^{J}
(average/bulk properties) and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{df}}^{J}
(diffuseness properties), characterize the profile. By dimensional analysis, the dimensionless product
H_{J}=\langle r_{J}^{2}\rangle\langle k_{J}^{2}\rangle
depends only on the ratio
\Sigma_{J}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{df}}^{J}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{av}}^{J}
:

\displaystyle H_{J}\approx

\displaystyle\vartheta_{0}(N_{J})+\vartheta_{1}(N_{J})\left(\frac{\Lambda_{\mathrm{df}}^{J}}{\Lambda_{\mathrm{av}}^{J}}\right)+\vartheta_{2}(N_{J})\left(\frac{\Lambda_{\mathrm{df}}^{J}}{\Lambda_{\mathrm{av}}^{J}}\right)^{2}+\cdots,

(4-11)

where
\vartheta_{0}
,
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
are dimensionless functions of
N_{J}
. Consequently,
H_{J}=\langle r_{J}^{2}\rangle\langle k_{J}^{2}\rangle
keeps almost constant due to the smallness of the factor
\Lambda_{\mathrm{df}}^{J}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{av}}^{J}
.
Specifically, by expanding
H_{\mathrm{n}}
around

\sigma_{\mathrm{n}}=a_{\mathrm{n}}/c_{\mathrm{n}}=0
, we have

H_{\mathrm{n}}\approx(4/25\pi)(9\pi/4)^{5/3}N^{2/3}[1-2.4\sigma_{\mathrm{n}}+18.7\sigma_{\mathrm{n}}^{2}+\cdots]\approx 1.33N^{2/3}[1-2.4\sigma_{\mathrm{n}}+18.7\sigma_{\mathrm{n}}^{2}+\cdots]
; and moreover

\Delta r_{\mathrm{np}}\approx(H_{\mathrm{n}}/8M_{\mathrm{N}})^{1/2}\langle E_{\mathrm{p}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\rangle^{-3/2}\Delta E_{\mathrm{pn}}^{\mathrm{kin}}+[(H_{\mathrm{n}}/2M_{\mathrm{N}})^{1/2}-(H_{\mathrm{p}}/2M_{\mathrm{N}})^{1/2}]\langle E_{\mathrm{p}}^{\mathrm{kin}}\rangle^{-1/2}
.
The constancy of
H_{J}
naturally follows from these expressions (see Ref.
[
90
]
for more examples).

This situation is very similar to the case in fluid mechanics: the velocity distribution
\mathbf{v}
of a body in a fluid is given by
\mathbf{v}=u\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{r}/\ell,\mathrm{R})
, where
\mathbf{r}
is the position of the body, and
\mathrm{R}=\rho u\ell/\eta
is the Reynolds number constructed from the density
\rho
, the linear dimension of the problem
\ell
, the velocity of the main stream
u
and the shear viscosity
\eta
. Then in two different flows of the same type, the velocities
\mathbf{v}/u
are the same functions of the ratio
\mathbf{r}/\ell
if the Reynolds number is the same for each flow. Furthermore, if the Reynolds number is small, Navier-Stokes equation gives the drag force on a sphere moving slowly in a fluid as
F\approx 6\pi\eta ua(1+3\mathrm{R}/8)
where
a
is the radius of the sphere. The factor
6\pi\eta ua
depending on the properties of the fluid and the sphere macroscopically is independent of the detailed structure of the sphere (corresponding to the parametrization of the density profile of the nucleon), which is like the number
N_{J}
in the above analysis for
\langle r_{J}^{2}\rangle\langle k_{J}^{2}\rangle=H_{J}
.

:
(Color Online). Two-dimensional proton and neutron Wigner distributions
w(r,k)
for

{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
,
{}^{40}\mathrm{Ca}
, and
{}^{48}\mathrm{Ca}
computed in the IPM and LCA.
Top: IPM; middle: full LCA; bottom: SRC contribution. White lines indicate
r_{\mathrm{rms}}
and
k_{\mathrm{rms}}
.
Figure taken from Ref.
[

]
.

:
(Color Online).
Upper:
40
Ca and
48
Ca radial dependence of the kinetic energy expectation value
T(r)
(top row) and the kinetic energy density
\rho_{T}(r)
(bottom row). Proton and neutron results are identical in
40
Ca. Lower: as in the upper panels, but now showing the momentum dependence of
r_{\mathrm{rms}}(k)
(top row) and the density
\rho_{r^{2}}(k)
(bottom row). Figures taken from Ref.
[

]
.

FIG. 70
:
(Color Online). Effects of the neutron skin in coordinate (
r
) space and proton skin in momentum (
k
) space on the multiplicity of hard photons with energies
E=100
, 150, 200, and 250 MeV in peripheral
208
Pb+
208
Pb collisions at a beam energy of 45 MeV/nucleon. Figure taken from Ref.
[

]
.

The density and momentum distribution functions are very fundamental concepts. In Ref.
[
128
]
, the Wigner distribution
w(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})
as a quasi-probability representation of nucleon dynamics in simultaneous coordinate-momentum space is studied. It begins with the spectral decomposition of the quantum Wigner operator
[
128
]
,

\displaystyle\widehat{w}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\left|\mathbf{r}-\frac{\mathbf{x}}{2}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{r}+\frac{\mathbf{x}}{2}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\left|\mathbf{k}+\frac{\mathbf{q}}{2}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{k}-\frac{\mathbf{q}}{2}\right|,

(4-12)

and defines the Wigner quasi-probability distribution for a pure state
|\psi\rangle
as

w(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})=\langle\psi|\widehat{w}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})|\psi\rangle
.
Integrating
\widehat{w}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})
over
\mathbf{r}
or
\mathbf{k}
yields the standard one-body momentum and spatial density operators,

\widehat{n}(\mathbf{k})=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}\widehat{w}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})=|\mathbf{k}\rangle\langle\mathbf{k}|
and
\widehat{\rho}(\mathbf{r})=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}\widehat{w}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})=|\mathbf{r}\rangle\langle\mathbf{r}|
, respectively.
Expectation values of general operators
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}
can be represented as phase-space averages,
\langle\widehat{\mathcal{O}}\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{k}w(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})O(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})
,
where
O(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})
is the Wigner transform of
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}
,

\displaystyle O(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\left\langle\mathbf{r}-\frac{\mathbf{x}}{2}\right|\widehat{\mathcal{O}}\left|\mathbf{r}+\frac{\mathbf{x}}{2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\left\langle\mathbf{k}+\frac{\mathbf{q}}{2}\right|\widehat{\mathcal{O}}\left|\mathbf{k}-\frac{\mathbf{q}}{2}\right\rangle.

(4-13)

Assuming spherical symmetry, the study adopts the angle-averaged Wigner distribution,
w(r,k)\equiv\int\mathrm{d}{}_{\mathbf{r}}\mathrm{d}{}_{\mathbf{k}}w(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{k})
,
which is normalized as
\int r^{2}\mathrm{d}r\int k^{2}\mathrm{d}kw(r,k)=A
.

Within the lowest-order correlation operator approximation (LCA), SRCs generate many-body operators when acting on Slater determinants, which are truncated at the two-body level. This leads to a decomposition of the Wigner distribution into four isospin pair channels as
w(r,k)=[w_{\mathrm{pp}}(r,k)+w_{\mathrm{pn}}(r,k)]+[w_{\mathrm{nn}}(r,k)+w_{\mathrm{np}}(r,k)]
,
where each
w_{ij}(r,k)
includes an uncorrelated mean-field component and a correlated SRC component.
The Wigner distribution is then used to compute quasi-expectation values of the kinetic-energy operator
T_{\mathbf{k}}=\mathbf{k}^{2}/2M_{\mathrm{N}}
and the rms-radius operator
\mathbf{r}^{2}
as functions of position and momentum
[
128
]
:

\displaystyle T(r)=

\displaystyle\langle T_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle=\left.{\int k^{2}\mathrm{d}k\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}w(r,k)}\right/{\int k^{2}\mathrm{d}kw(r,k)},

(4-14)

\displaystyle r^{2}_{\mathrm{rms}}(k)=

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{r}^{2}\rangle=\left.{\int r^{4}\mathrm{d}rw(r,k)}\right/{\int r^{2}\mathrm{d}rw(r,k)}.

(4-15)

Because the Wigner distribution is not positive definite, these local quasi-expectation values can become negative.
The authors further construct properly normalized distributions,

\displaystyle\rho_{T}(r)\equiv

\displaystyle\left(r^{2}\left/\int r^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right.\right)\cdot\left.{\int k^{2}\mathrm{d}k\frac{\mathbf{k}^{2}}{2M_{\mathrm{N}}}w(r,k)}\right/{\int k^{2}\mathrm{d}kw(r,k)},

(4-16)

\displaystyle\rho_{r^{2}}(k)\equiv

\displaystyle\left(k^{2}\left/\int k^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right.\right)\cdot\left.{\int r^{4}\mathrm{d}rw(r,k)}\right/{\int r^{2}\mathrm{d}rw(r,k)},

(4-17)

which encode the spatial structure of the kinetic energy and the momentum structure of the rms radius, while integrating to the correct scalar observables. Consequently,
\int\mathrm{d}r\rho_{T}(r)=\langle{T}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle
and
\int\mathrm{d}k\rho_{r^{2}}(k)=\langle\mathbf{r}^{2}\rangle
.

FIG.
68
illustrates the two-dimensional Wigner distribution
w(r,k)
obtained in the LCA framework
[
128
]
. The results have been validated through several checks, including consistency with independently computed momentum distributions and rms radii, as well as the correct normalization of all isospin combinations. The remaining sub-percent deviations originate from the truncation of the quantum-number summation and the use of finite numerical grids. Physically, the figure shows that the IPM
w(r,k)
spans the full radial range and a well-defined momentum region common to all nuclei studied, whereas SRCs generate a pronounced high-momentum tail that is largely confined to the nuclear interior. This behavior reflects the fact that, in the LCA, SRCs arise predominantly from correlation operators acting on nodeless relative
S
-pairs, which peak at
r<r_{\mathrm{rms}}
and become negligible for
r\gtrsim 2r_{\mathrm{rms}}
. Consequently, the SRC contribution to
w(r,k)
is shifted to smaller radii, even though non-negligible components also appear at
k<k_{\mathrm{F}}
. The normalization of the SRC sector (e.g., about
1.8/6
nucleons in
{}^{12}\mathrm{C}
) therefore cannot be directly connected to experimental extractions of the number of SRC pairs, which refer exclusively to high-momentum components. For all nuclei, the SRC part dominates the LCA result for
k>2~\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
at any
r
, despite its rapid falloff at large radii. Although this raises questions regarding experimental access to the nuclear interior, earlier studies have shown that reactions probing correlated pairs remain sensitive to interior dynamics even after accounting for strong final-state interactions. The Wigner distributions also exhibit small negative regions at low
r
and
k
, characteristic of quantum effects, and in
{}^{48}\mathrm{Ca}
a neutron skin is clearly visible. Finally, compared to the deuteron, the SRC-induced momentum tails in finite nuclei extend over a larger momentum range and appear smoother, an effect attributable to center-of-mass motion and the coherent contribution of many nucleon pairs.

FIG.
69
shows the IPM and LCA results for the combinations
[T(r),\rho_{T}(r)]
and
[r_{\mathrm{rms}}(k),\rho_{r^{2}}(k)]
for
40
Ca and
48
Ca, with a focus on the effects of SRCs and the role of the neutron excess in
48
Ca
[
128
]
. The IPM results, using the harmonic oscillator frequency, are compared with LCA calculations. For
48
Ca, IPM neutrons have higher kinetic energy than protons at all radii, while SRC contributions in LCA reverse this trend, making protons more energetic in the interior (roughly at
r\lesssim 1.8\,\mathrm{fm}
) and neutrons slightly more energetic at intermediate radii. The radial kinetic energy density
\rho_{T}(r)
shows that IPM protons contribute more to the bulk kinetic energy in the interior, with LCA enhancing this effect, resulting in overall more energetic protons. The momentum-space radii
r_{\mathrm{rms}}(k)
plateau around
k\approx 2\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
, with neutrons larger than protons below the Fermi momentum, while tensor correlations make proton and neutron radii nearly equal for
1.5\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}\lesssim k\lesssim 2.5\,\mathrm{fm}^{-1}
. High-momentum nucleons contribute minimally to the rms radius, even in LCA. The total kinetic energies and rms radii obtained from phase-space integration of
w(r,k)
agree within 10% with ab initio AV18 calculations for
40
Ca. For
48
Ca, all LCA variants predict a proton kinetic energy
\sim 1
MeV higher than neutrons, a “kinetic energy inversion”
[
393
]
, with SRCs reducing the neutron skin by 5-10%. Overall, SRCs strongly affect kinetic energies (uncertainties 2-3 MeV) and moderately reduce radii (3-6%), while the largest uncertainty for radii arises from the choice of harmonic oscillator parameters, and the kinetic energies are slightly less sensitive to this choice (1-2 MeV).

As a short summary, in their work
[
128
]
, the authors extend the study of nuclear SRCs beyond the traditional momentum-space perspective by incorporating a spatial perspective through nuclear Wigner distributions
w(r,k)
. For
12
C,
40
Ca, and
48
Ca, they find that SRCs affect
w(r,k)
over the full momentum range while being confined to the deep nuclear interior. From these distributions, they compute kinetic energies and point-nucleon radii, showing that SRCs nearly double proton and neutron kinetic energies and induce a proton-neutron asymmetry in neutron-rich nuclei such as
48
Ca, where SRCs reduce the neutron skin by 5-10%. They further emphasize that the Wigner distributions provide valuable input for semi-classical transport calculations and suggest that models including both long- and short-range correlations could lead to measurable changes in nuclear radii.

4.2 Particle Production, Flows and Yield Ratios in Heavy-ion Reactions

Building on the previous discussion of proton skins, Ref.
[
209
]
investigated hard-photon emission from neutron-proton bremsstrahlung in
208
Pb+
208
Pb collisions near the Fermi energy, focusing on the roles of neutron skins in coordinate (
r
) space and proton skins in momentum (
k
) space on the time evolution at different beam energies. They found that direct hard-photon emission is sensitive to neutron skins, with stronger effects for higher-energy photons, while proton skins have an even larger impact, indicating that proton-skin effects must be considered when extracting neutron skin thickness from transport-model predictions.
Specifically, FIG.
70
shows the effects of proton skins in
k
space and neutron skins in
r
-space on the time evolution of hard photons with
E=100
, 150, 200, and 250 MeV in peripheral
208
Pb+
208
Pb collisions at
E_{\mathrm{b}}=45
MeV/nucleon and an impact parameter of 11 fm. Photon production decreases for thicker neutron skins, especially for higher-energy photons, because the larger neutron densities reduce emission via incoherent
\mathrm{p}+\mathrm{n}\to\mathrm{p}+\mathrm{n}+\gamma
bremsstrahlung. In contrast, proton skins enhance hard-photon emission more strongly than neutron skins due to the surface term in the nucleon kinetic-energy density
[
90
]
, which increases local proton momentum in the nuclear surface and thus the emission probability. The hardest photons (
E=250
MeV) likely originate from multiple scatterings of nucleons below the Fermi momentum rather than from high-momentum tail collisions, and are therefore insensitive to the surface term and SRCs.
Other quantities are also studied in Ref.
[
209
]
, including the angular distributions, the cross section as well as the transverse momentum dependence of photons. For example, the transverse momentum dependence of the photons is shown in FIG.
71
; the spectra exhibit typical exponential shapes for different neutron and proton skins. Notably, when the proton skin in
k
-space is included, the influence of varying neutron skins on the transverse momentum spectrum becomes more pronounced, particularly at high transverse momentum.
The hard photon emission problem with SRC-HMT was also studied previously in Ref.
[
507
]
.

:

r
) space and proton skin in momentum (
k
) space on (a) the transverse momentum dependence of hard photons and (b) the ratios of each transverse momentum spectrum to the average spectrum for four cases, in peripheral
208
Pb+

E_{\mathrm{b}}=45
MeV/nucleon. Figure taken from Ref.
[
